Nestle · NESN-CH · the new Memo conveyor's card of 16 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Nestle

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 55,496 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “57th percentile” means the stock beats 57% of them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

D4528th percentile

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment does not carry the Nestlé case. It roughly cancels out over 18 months and leans against it thereafter. The positives are cyclical and dated:

  • commodity deflation reaching the P&L from late 2026 into 2027;
  • a sector-wide volume recovery as the 2022–24 price shock laps;
  • emerging-market and digital channel growth.

The negatives are structural:

  • a Swiss franc that has taken about 5pp a year off reported growth;
  • a European retail system that will claim much of any cost relief;
  • slow-moving demand erosion from GLP-1 and demographics;
  • a discount-rate regime that de-rates leveraged defensives.

The most important modelling consequence: any margin recovery in 2027 is a window, not a trend. MS expects commodity inflation to return in H2 2027, and retailers are already demanding price cuts. The case must therefore rest on Nestlé's own share gains and cost delivery, not on the environment. Build constant-currency forecasts first. Overlay a currency drag of -1% to -3% a year on reported CHF figures with explicit sensitivities. Remove Russia from FY27.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0232C-0563C-0730C-0948C-3546C-3972
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3215th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Competitive intensity is rising and will rise further over the next 6–12 months. The main mechanism is not other brands but retailers and private label. They will use falling coffee and cocoa costs to demand price give-backs, cut their own-label prices faster (they hedge shorter) and coordinate delistings. Branded peers have also turned from price to volume, so everyone is reinvesting at once.

  • Private label is premiumising and growing.
  • It holds 29% of European coffee and over 40% value share in Spain and the UK (Morgan Stanley). US store brands rose from 13.7% to 14% dollar share.
  • Premium own-label lines are scaling: Walmart Bettergoods reached USD 1bn, and Costco has launched Kirkland fresh dog food.
  • Bernstein's counter is that private label is "mainly cyclical": UK share grew only 8bp a year from 2015 to 2024. That moderates, but does not remove, the threat.
  • Retailer power is being exercised.
  • Pan-European buying alliances (Concordis, Eurelec) coordinate delistings across borders. Intermarché delisted 30 Nestlé brands in early 2026.
  • Zone Europe RIG was 0.0% in Q2-26 and pricing only 0.2% in Q3-26 (consensus 1.3%).
  • Morgan Stanley's "Retailers vs Suppliers: a new playbook?" (12 November 2026) argues that shorter-hedged private label will cut chocolate prices as cocoa falls.
  • Branded rivals are reinvesting harder.
  • Unilever is volume-led: Q2-26 volume up 5.5%, 180 power brands, "reinvests savings to gain share".
  • Mondelez puts its gross margin gains into double-digit advertising.
  • Mars is bigger after buying Kellanova and is aggressive in digital pet.
  • Chobani took US creamers from 4% to 12% share and is spending USD 1.2bn on capacity.
  • Danone and Abbott offer retailers 15% trade margins on infant formula against Nestlé's 9%.
  • Where intensity is stable. Premium coffee systems and petcare remain rational oligopolies: Nestlé and Mars hold about half of US pet retail.
  • Model implication. Pricing in Europe is near zero to slightly negative into 2027, and trade spend rises as a share of gross sales. Commodity relief will not drop through fully to gross margin.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A6396th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: For the next 6–9 months the net effect is a gross-margin tailwind. The cocoa and coffee spike is reversing and Nestlé's 6–12 month hedge book rolls onto lower prices from late 2026 into 2027. The tailwind is smaller and shorter than the spot move implies, for three reasons:

  • Retailers will claim part of it as price give-backs.
  • Freight, energy, milk and packaging are rising.
  • Morgan Stanley expects soft-commodity inflation to return to the P&L in H2 2027, with El Niño a live risk.

Supplier concentration is low and favourable, with one proven exception: single-source specialty ingredients, the cause of the recall.

  • Size of the exposure. Raw and packaging materials are about 33% of sales. Green coffee is about 5% of sales (CHF 4–5bn, 8–10% of COGS; over 20% of raw materials per Jefferies). Cocoa is about 1% of sales (7–10% of raw materials). Milk and dairy are about 20% of raw materials.
  • Direction of travel.
  • Spot coffee is down about 28% year to date (mid-2026) and Expana projects a 9-million-bag 2025/26 surplus.
  • Cocoa has corrected over 60% from above USD 10,000/t. MS chocolate pipeline costs moved from 5% inflation (Q1-26) to 45% deflation (H2-26).
  • Against that: Jefferies' food index points to +4.9% commodity cost growth in H2-26. Milk is firming in India. Freight and energy rose with the Middle East conflict and Strait of Hormuz rerouting, which is why H2-26 margin guidance is flat on H1.
  • The FY25 damage that is now unwinding. Gross margin fell 110bp to 45.6% in FY25. Nespresso margin fell about 360bp to under 18–20%, and Powdered & Liquid Beverages excluding Nespresso fell about 540bp (Jefferies).
  • Supplier power. Three procurement hubs control over 70% of spend and Nestrade centralises hedging. E-auctions are eroding the pricing power of flavour houses.
  • Supplier risks. A Chinese ARA-oil supplier caused the Q1-26 recall, which took 90bp off Q1 organic growth. AAK supplies 10% of Nestlé's vegetable oils, and replacing it would take two years.
  • Model implication. Assume:
  • gross margin recovery of +50–100bp from FY25's 45.6% by FY27 (H1-26 was already 46.4%);
  • about half of the coffee and cocoa deflation retained, the rest given back in price or reinvested;
  • a renewed headwind risk from H2 2027.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F307th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Macro bites hardest at the company level, through the Swiss franc. Three distinct forces are at work:

  • Company level: the Swiss franc. No euro-reporting peer faces translation on this scale. The franc has removed about 5pp a year from reported growth since 2023.
  • Sector level: the discount rate. High bond yields have de-rated European staples to 18-year relative lows. Nestlé is an expensive, leveraged member of a "bond-proxy" sector.
  • Industry level: squeezed real incomes. Developed-market consumers are pressured, which limits price and favours private label.

Net macro is a headwind, mildly easing at the margin.

  • Currency.
  • Translation cut reported sales by 7.8% (2023), 3.7% (2024), 5.7% (2025), 6.2% (H1-26) and 9.3% at the worst point (Q1-26).
  • Underlying EPS fell 7.3% reported in FY25, and in H1-26 rose 4% at constant currency but fell 2.4% at actual rates.
  • About 80–90% of trade flows are naturally hedged locally, but translation is deliberately left unhedged.
  • A 5% franc appreciation costs 10–15bp of margin. Nespresso (all production in Switzerland) carries the transaction risk.
  • Forecasters expect a "gentle" further franc strengthening: USD/CHF 0.80 by Q2 2027 (Maybank), EUR/CHF toward 0.87 (Morgan Stanley), with the SNB at 0% and new Swiss trade agreements supporting the franc.
  • Wisdom Library: "Home-Cost Exporters Often Leave Currency Unhedged, So Reported Earnings Follow The Currency".
  • Rates. The US 10-year is near 5%. European staples trade at 18-year relative lows (DB). Nestlé's interest bill is insulated: 80% fixed, average cost 2.6%, +100bp adds only CHF 17m a year. The equity multiple is not insulated.
  • Consumers. US real wages are falling back. Europe has subdued disposable income and weak confidence. Emerging markets are resilient (AOA, India, Brazil, Mexico).
  • Oil and freight. Brent fell below USD 100 (5 November 2026, from about USD 115) after the Middle East escalation, which eases the H2-26 freight headwind.
  • Model implication. Build the model in constant currency, then overlay a currency path of -1% to -3% a year on reported CHF sales and EPS, with a ±5pp sensitivity. That sensitivity alone moves reported EPS by about ±4% per 5% move (Redburn).
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–48

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Four further external dynamics matter, and they net to roughly neutral:

  • The sector carve-out wave (positive). It gives Nestlé's own perimeter shrinking a valuation template.
  • Competitor consolidation (negative). Mars plus Kellanova builds a stronger rival in snacking and pet.
  • Climate (two-sided). It raises volatility in coffee, cocoa and grains.

  • L'Oréal's share price (large swing). Nestlé's roughly 20% stake, worth about CHF 42bn and close to 19% of Nestlé's CHF 226bn market capitalisation, moves Nestlé's sum-of-the-parts and its leverage optionality independently of food.

  • Carve-outs. Corporate carve-out deal value reached GBP 59bn in H1 2026. Examples: Unilever Ice Cream demerged December 2025, Foods sold to McCormick for USD 44.8bn, about EUR 6bn of buybacks planned. AlphaSense rates the carve-out "the most reliable self-help mechanism" over 12–24 months, but leaving businesses also leaves stranded costs (Unilever EUR 400–500m).

  • Mars. Mars is the #1 global pet nutrition player (33% share against Purina's 30%) and private and long-term in its behaviour. With Kellanova (snacks) it gains scale in Nestlé's Food & Snacks category. An ex-Mars account manager says the deal strengthens it against Nestlé.
  • Climate. El Niño agricultural shocks peak 11–19 months after the anomaly (MS). ING projects wheat -3% and rice -2%. West African cocoa remains deficit-prone.
  • L'Oréal. Redburn's target-price rise to CHF 72 was "entirely driven" by the L'Oréal stake's value. Management treats the stake as passive. A sale would retire most net debt (CHF 56bn).
  • Trade. Swiss–China and pending US agreements support the franc.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4634th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Adjacent entrants are the most material threat: retailers as brand owners, and ingredient houses that enable them. Standard challenger brands are a moderate, category-specific threat. Orthogonal entrants (pharma through GLP-1, AI shopping agents, cocoa substitutes) are a slow but rising threat. None threatens Nestlé's core franchises in petcare science, medical nutrition or Nespresso systems within the model horizon.

  • Adjacent: retailers and contract makers. High threat.
  • Walmart Bettergoods (USD 1bn); Costco Kirkland (over 25% of Costco grocery, plus fresh dog food); Aldi's clean-label plan across its own range through 2027; Tesco Finest growing three times the group rate.
  • Ingredient suppliers "democratise retail launch capabilities, making private label outsourcing highly viable" (Bernstein).
  • Shared contract manufacturers let own labels reach "quality parity".
  • Standard challengers. Moderate threat.
  • Chobani (creamers), Ryze (coffee), Freshpet (over 95% of fresh pet), Grüns and Dr Squatch-type digital natives (in HPC), subscription pet food.
  • Barriers stay high in medical nutrition (clinical trials, FSMP 14–30 months) and infant formula.
  • Orthogonal. Low now, rising.
  • GLP-1 drugs, with UK penetration heading to 13% by 2027.
  • AI shopping agents comparing on price and nutrition (MS "Agentic Commerce").
  • Cocoa-free and cell-cultured alternatives: ChoViva, Celleste Bio, AAK fractionation. Nestlé uses these itself, so they may help it.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A7294th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Suppliers are mainly a support. They are fragmented, price-takers, increasingly commoditised by Nestlé's procurement AI, and now in surplus in coffee and cocoa. Two specific risks remain:

  • Quality failure at single-source specialty ingredient suppliers, which caused the 2026 recall.

  • Dependence on supplier-finance (reverse factoring) programmes that flatter working capital.

  • Support.

  • 100% of cocoa and 99.9% of coffee sourced through certified programmes.
  • Long-term farmer programmes (Nestlé Cocoa Plan; USD 1.3bn regenerative agriculture plan).
  • Robusta varieties lifting yields up to 86% under stress.
  • Vendor consolidation; e-auctions eroding flavour-house pricing power.
  • Local sourcing: about 80% of Nestlé Malaysia's raw materials unaffected by shipping tensions.
  • Risk.
  • ARA oil from a Chinese supplier (Cabio) carried cereulide, leading to the global recall (about CHF 200–270m of sales, -90bp to Q1 growth).
  • AAK supplies 10% of vegetable oils; switching specialty infant lipids takes two years.
  • Reverse factoring is CHF 6.21bn (6.8% of sales, over 37% of payables), with a large share through one counterparty. If banks withdraw it, payables shrink and there is a multi-billion cash outflow (the Business Foundations report's sceptical counterweight).
  • Milk, sugar and edible oils firming in India.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0150C-0338C-0442C-0860C-3255C-3744C-4350
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5026th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: End-market demand is changing cyclically for the better in volume while staying structurally slow in value. As the price shock of 2022 to 2024 fades, shoppers are buying more units again, but per-capita demand in developed markets is capped and mature-market wallets are squeezed. This is a volume recovery from a trough, not a structural acceleration. Visibility is about 12 months, not two-plus years.

  • Evidence of the cyclical turn. Nestlé's RIG went from -0.3% (FY23) and 0.8% (FY24 and FY25) to 1.2% (Q1-26), 1.8% (Q2-26) and 2.8% (Q3-26), positive in all zones. The turn followed pricing falling from 2.8% (FY25) to 1.1% (Q3-26). Across the sector, Western European scanner data shows a "transition from inflation to volume-led growth" with flat pricing expected through end-2026 (Bernstein).
  • Evidence the structure is unchanged.
  • Global food volume growth is constrained by per-capita calorie limits and below-replacement births.
  • US food retail volumes tracked by Jefferies fell -3.8% (September 2026).
  • US real wages are back to early-2025 levels (Morgan Stanley). European consumer confidence is weak: GfK -30.8 for November and euro-area flash -17.9.
  • Consumer wallets are underperforming nominal GDP (Deutsche Bank).
  • China infant formula faces births down 17% in 2025 to 7.9 million, a permanent decline.
  • GLP-1 usage among US adults rose from 3% to 11% (2024 to 2026).
  • The demand mix is K-shaped. Premium lines hold up (Nespresso, Purina Pro Plan, wet cat, medical nutrition). Low-income households trade down to smaller packs and private label.
  • Emerging markets are the one structural positive. Zone AOA grew 6.4% organic with 5.0% RIG in Q3-26, and India's quick commerce is growing about 50% to 100% a year.
  • What the Consumer should model. Group RIG of 1.5–2.5% over 2027–28 is defensible. Treat Q3's 2.8% as flattered:
  • Waters, about 4% of sales, grew RIG 7.3% on a summer heatwave, worth about 0.2–0.3pp of group RIG.
  • DB says confectionery shipment phasing helped.
  • Coffee and nutrition decelerated sequentially.
  • Q3-25's comparison base was easy.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F388th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Two disruption forces are strengthening, and both act against Nestlé:

  • GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which hit US frozen meals and confectionery.
  • Retailer and agentic digital power, which erodes brand search premiums and moves margin to retailers' media networks.

Both are slow-moving. The near-term financial drag is small, about 0.1–0.3pp of group volume a year. The "up to 40% of sales at risk" headline is not a credible modelling input. One disruption force is clearly positive and strengthening: emerging-market quick commerce and e-commerce.

  • GLP-1 is strengthening.
  • US adult usage rose from 3% (2024) to 11% (2026), per Gallup via Jefferies. UK penetration is about 5%, heading to 13% by 2027 with oral formulations (Redburn).
  • Active users cut chocolate consumption by 42% (Morgan Stanley AlphaWise), and households cut grocery spending by about 8%.
  • Deutsche Bank estimates a 0.7% annual volume drag on North American packaged food.
  • Nestlé's exposed lines (US frozen such as Stouffer's and Lean Cuisine, and confectionery) are a minority of sales, perhaps 10–15% (my estimate from category splits).
  • Partly disproving evidence: Nestlé reported no material impact on ice cream or confectionery (FY25 call). DB argues appetite suppression encourages trading up to premium treats. Nestlé's own response is Vital Pursuit and protein.
  • Channel and data disruption is strengthening.
  • E-commerce is 21.6% of year-to-date group sales and grew 15.3% organic in Q3-26.
  • Amazon accounted for "over 100% of Nestlé's US sales growth" at one point (DB).
  • Retailer media networks and AI shopping agents are emerging that compare on price and nutrition metrics. This favours private label and erodes brand equity that rests on searching costs (Wisdom Library: "The Buying Agent Does Not Feel Loyalty").
  • The positive side: Nestlé India gets nearly 40% of its e-commerce from quick commerce, serves over 6,000 dark stores and replenishes every two days.
  • Nespresso system disruption is mature, not new. Compatible capsules have taken over 60% of Original-machine consumption. Vertuo is on the flat part of its S-curve in mature Europe, though active consumers grew in Europe for the first time in years after the Vertuo Up launch.
  • Model implication. Apply a small, persistent negative volume adjustment (0.1–0.2pp a year) to North American food and confectionery, and a positive mix effect from digital channels. Do not model a step-change.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D4227th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Political and regulatory change is under way on several fronts and nets to a mild negative. The largest immediate effect is the loss of Russia. Most other rules raise costs that Nestlé's scale lets it absorb better than smaller rivals. The one clear improvement is the US tariff on Swiss goods.

  • Russia (the largest negative). Nestlé Russia was placed under temporary external administration in September 2026 and deconsolidated from end-September 2026.
  • It was about 2% of sales but nearly 4% of business profits (Bernstein), plus interest income on high-yielding local cash.
  • Brokers cut estimates: Redburn -1% on FY27–28 EPS, Morgan Stanley -4% on FY27 EPS (with freight).
  • Jefferies flagged a possible CHF 1bn write-off of un-repatriated cash.
  • Packaging and environmental rules. The EU Packaging and Packaging Waste Regulation brings deposit return schemes by 2029 and 10% reusable beverage packaging by 2030; UK plastics tax and US state Extended Producer Responsibility schemes add costs. The Peranel water JV removes most of the beverage-specific exposure.
  • Health and nutrition rules. UK HFSS advertising limits, front-of-pack warning seals in Mexico and Chile (and proposed in India, where they could flag up to 80% of packaged food) and ultra-processed food scrutiny all raise reformulation and research costs. These favour scale players.
  • Infant formula. The EU set a cereulide action limit of 0.43ng/g, and Nestlé applies its own 0.20ng/g. France and Austria have opened criminal and judicial probes. India's FSSAI has three adjudication cases against Nestlé India.
  • Tariffs. Switzerland expects a 15% US tariff cap, against the 39% levied earlier. This matters because Nespresso capsules are 100% made in Switzerland. Tariffs still cost Zone Americas about 30bp of margin in 1H26.
  • Model implication.
  • Take Russia out of FY27: about -2% of sales and roughly -3% to -4% of EBIT, given its above-average margin.
  • Assume no margin benefit from regulation.
  • Allow 10–20bp a year of compliance and reformulation cost inside overheads.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D6035th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: One industry-level driver is now accelerating Nestlé's demand: the sector's switch from price-led to volume-led growth as price shocks lap and brand investment rises. It is moderately helpful, not transformational. Two structural category drivers add steady support: coffee's "caffeinated consumer" and premiumisation, and pet humanisation. Nestlé is positioned in both.

  • Volume-led reinvestment. DB finds that staples raising their advertising-to-sales ratio outperform, with about a 500bp EPS growth premium. Nestlé's A&P rose from about 8.1% to 8.6% (FY25) and 8.9% (H1-26), heading for over 9%, concentrated on 135–150 brands (from over 400).
  • Coffee.
  • Coffee is Nestlé's largest category (29% of sales). Morgan Stanley's "Wired for Growth" (August 2026) sees structural caffeine demand, including from GLP-1 users seeking energy.
  • Cold coffee and out-of-home are a CHF 3.5bn+ double-digit growth opportunity.
  • Nescafé and Starbucks grew 5.8% in Q2-26.
  • Petcare. Global pet food is a USD 95bn-plus market growing structurally at 4–5%, and the US pet functional food segment has a 14.2% five-year CAGR. Wet cat is growing at mid-to-high single-digit RIG now that US capacity has been added.
  • Protein and health. GLP-1 drives a structural shift to protein and portion control. Nestlé is a player (Vital Pursuit, Orgain, Vital Proteins, medical nutrition) but not the category leader that Danone (Oikos) or Glanbia are.
  • Emerging markets. India digital is up 38% and quick commerce about 50%. Zone AOA RIG was 5.0% in Q3-26.
  • Limits. Category growth is mid-single-digit nominal at best. Infant formula is shrinking (-1% globally, -5% in China). The benefit accrues to share gainers, and Nestlé's share trend has only just stabilised.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5538th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Consumer demand currently supports the thesis on volume. Shoppers are responding to lower price increases, better marketing and restored supply with more units, across all zones in Q3-26. The support is fragile for three reasons:

  • it is partly technical (weather, phasing, the recall lapping, destocking ending);
  • low-income shoppers in developed markets remain squeezed and trade down;

  • Morgan Stanley's view is that the inflation-exposed categories showed low price elasticity in FY25, so cutting price may not buy volume.

  • Supportive.

  • Q3-26 RIG by zone: Americas 2.4%, AOA 5.0%, Europe 1.0%, Nespresso 1.1%, Waters 7.3%.
  • Petcare about 5% with retail sell-in matching sell-out.
  • Coffee volumes recovered as price rises lapped.
  • E-commerce up 15.3%.
  • Premium tiers resilient: Nespresso, Pro Plan, wet cat.
  • Fragile.
  • DB: warm summer (Waters) and confectionery shipment phasing; coffee and nutrition slowed sequentially; "underlying demand remains soft in key premium categories".
  • The US consumer: real wages back to early-2025 levels; frozen category weak; Kraft Heinz Q2 volume and mix -2.6%.
  • European confidence weak; private label "sticky" after inflation cycles (MS).
  • Former Nestlé insiders see sub-2% RIG as "the long-term norm".
  • Model implication. RIG of about 2% is the central case for 2027. A bear case of about 1% (if the pricing roll-off does not keep stimulating volume) is as plausible as a bull case of about 3%.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4429th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance regulators are tightening on product content, packaging and food safety, which hurts at the margin. The burden falls more heavily on smaller competitors, so for Nestlé it is a cost more than a competitive threat. Political actors hurt directly in Russia, which is now lost, and in currency (Swiss safe-haven status). They help at the margin through the improved Swiss–US tariff. The position is fragile only in infant formula, where a second safety incident would invite punitive scrutiny.

  • Hurting.
  • Russia: administration and deconsolidation.
  • India FSSAI cases on infant nutrition claims.
  • Austrian criminal probe and French judicial probe and Foodwatch complaints over the recall.
  • EU PPWR; US state EPR (California SB 54, a USD 1.87bn programme budget in 2027).
  • UK HFSS and plastics tax; Latin American warning labels; ultra-processed food scrutiny and litigation (a Wisconsin lawsuit); Health Star Rating benchmarks.
  • Helping.
  • The 15% US tariff cap on Swiss goods, against 39% earlier.
  • Complex compliance "favour[s] larger consumer product manufacturers".
  • Clinical and regulatory barriers (FSMP) protect medical nutrition.
  • The Peranel JV removes the water business's regulatory exposure (PPWR deposit schemes, French extraction probes, microfiltration fines).
  • Political risk to sector multiples. Proposed UK voluntary grocery price caps; Hungarian margin caps on retailers. These are not direct to Nestlé but signal politics against food inflation.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5023rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Nestlé's addressable market is very large: global packaged food, beverages, pet food and nutrition run to well over a trillion dollars. Nestlé's share is in the low-to-mid single digits, so market size is not the constraint. The constraint is growth. Category value growth allows about 3–5% organic growth for Nestlé's mix over the medium term: volume 1–2.5%, price and mix 1.5–2.5%. That brackets management's 4%+ target at the top of the range. Hitting it needs share gains or mix toward the faster platforms.

  • Category growth rates.
  • Global beverages: about USD 1.4tn, mid-single-digit long-term growth.
  • Pet: a USD 132bn industry in 2019, 71.5% of it food, growing structurally at 4–5%. US pet functional food grew at a 14.2% five-year CAGR.
  • Coffee: mid-single digit, with cold coffee and out-of-home growing double digit.
  • Medical nutrition: structural ageing tailwind; home enteral feeding.
  • Infant formula: -1% globally, -5% in China.
  • Mainstream packaged food and snacks: about 0–3% nominal. GLP-1 may cost about 0.7% a year of volume in North American packaged food.
  • Nestlé's mix arithmetic (my estimate).
  • Growth platforms (30% of sales) at high single digit, about 8%, add about 2.4pp.
  • The core (70%) at 2–3% adds about 1.4–2.1pp.
  • Total: about 3.8–4.5%, consistent with the 4%+ target, but only if the platforms really grow at high single digit. They grew 7% (mid-single-digit RIG) in H1-26.
  • Peer frames. Unilever 4–6% (HPC, higher growth). Danone 3–5%. Mondelez at least 2%. Bernstein's long-term baseline for Orkla is 3.7%.

Overall external picture

C-0660C-1042
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C-6053rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Nestlé's own cycle is turning up in volume after a three-year trough. Pricing is rolling off and reported revenue is near its trough. The turn is real but young: three quarters of rising RIG, helped by easy comparisons and one-offs. The most likely path is organic growth of about 3.5–4% with a growing volume share. That makes FY27 the first year since FY22 in which reported revenue can grow before scope changes. After disposals, reported revenue still shrinks.

  • Volume cycle.
  • RIG: -0.3% (FY23), 0.8% (FY24), 0.8% (FY25), 1.3% (Q4-25, about 20bp flattered by US pet pre-buying), 1.2% (Q1-26), 1.8% (Q2-26), 2.8% (Q3-26).
  • Nestlé says the rate of value market share loss fell 60% and billionaire brands are share-neutral for the first time in a decade.
  • Price cycle. Pricing went from about 8% (FY22–23), to 1.5% (FY24), 2.8% (FY25, coffee pass-through), 2.1% (H1-26) and 1.1% (Q3-26), with Europe at 0.2%. Further roll-off and commodity give-backs point to pricing of 0.5–1.5% in 2027.
  • Reported cycle.
  • Revenue fell for three years (-1.5%, -1.8%, -2.0%) and H1-26 was down 2.5%.
  • Currency translation was -7.8% (FY23), -3.7% (FY24), -5.7% (FY25), -6.2% (H1-26) and -3.7% (9M-26). Q3-26 had a favourable FX effect "for the first time in years", and the full-year translation guide improved to about -2% from -3% to -6%.
  • Q3-26 sales were CHF 22.756bn against CHF 21.641bn.
  • Category and zone dispersion.
  • Coffee: +7.5% organic in H1-26 (pricing 4.7%), decelerating in Q3.
  • Petcare: 2.7% (H1), then about 5% (Q3) after US destocking ended and capacity opened (Batavia, Ohio).
  • Nutrition: -1.2% (H1), with the recall drag fading from 90bp (Q1) to 30bp (Q2) and 20bp (Q3), leaving a mechanical tailwind in H1-27.
  • Zone AOA was strong at 6.4% and Europe weak at 1.2%.
  • Model implication. Base FY26 organic growth at about 3.6–3.8% (9M is about 3.7%) and FY27 at 3.5–4.0%: RIG 2–2.5% plus pricing 1–1.5%, plus about 15–20bp from lapping the recall.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F4223rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The external picture is a modest net headwind, improving at the margin over the next 6–12 months. Tailwinds are cyclical and time-limited. Headwinds are structural and persistent. A model should therefore treat the 2027 margin recovery as a window rather than a new trend, and keep a structural reported-currency drag.

  • Sector and industry thematic drivers, one by one. 1. Shoppers resisting price, so growth must come from volume, mix and savings (Industry theme). Nestlé is aligned: RIG is up and A&P is up. Tailwind, moderate. 2. Leaving low-margin businesses lifts the margin (Industry theme). Nestlé is executing: Waters, ice cream, vitamins, Blue Bottle, Russia by force. Tailwind, moderate; watch stranded costs. 3. Protected brands where shoppers cannot swap to a copy (Industry theme). Partly fits Nestlé: medical nutrition, Pro Plan, Nespresso Vertuo. It does not fit instant coffee, frozen or confectionery. Mixed. 4. Retailer advertising on shopper data moves margin to retailers (Industry theme). Headwind, structural, slow. 5. "A commodity's fall reaches a processor first, as cash, and a brand later, as margin" (Sector rule). This points to Nestlé's margin benefit landing in 2027, net of give-backs. Tailwind, cyclical, 2027. 6. Protein for people on weight-loss drugs (Sector theme, "Big" in the United States). Nestlé is a participant, not a leader. Small tailwind, set against the GLP-1 volume headwind. 7. Contract makers for grocers gaining own-label volume (Sector theme). This is the mirror image of private-label pressure on branded makers. Headwind.
  • The aggregate.
  • Headwinds:
    • currency (about -2% to -5% a year on reported figures);
    • retailer and private-label pressure with price give-backs (Europe);
    • GLP-1 and birth-rate volume drags (about 0.2–0.4pp a year combined);
    • the Russia deconsolidation (about -3% to -4% of EBIT);
    • the discount-rate de-rating of staples.
  • Tailwinds:
    • commodity deflation into 2027 (+50–100bp of gross margin before give-back);
    • the volume recovery as pricing laps;
    • emerging-market and digital growth;
    • easing freight as oil falls.
  • Why it matters for the model. Over the 18-month horizon external forces roughly cancel on operating profit. Over 18 months to four years the structural headwinds dominate unless Nestlé's internal changes win share. The internal change forces must do the work.

Inputs

2

Internal change forces/risks

C6459th percentile

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internally, Nestlé is in the most comprehensive change programme of its modern history, and the internal forces are unambiguously positive in direction:

  • new leadership at every level;
  • a coherent, measurable volume-led strategy;
  • hard-wired incentives (the RIG gatekeeper);
  • CHF 3bn of savings, ahead of plan;
  • a shrinking perimeter that lifts mix.

The two constraints that bound the outcome:

  • Capital. The programme is funded by self-help, not the balance sheet. There is no buyback, leverage is about 3x and the dividend takes about 87% of FCF. EPS growth will come almost entirely from operating profit, not from share count or M&A.
  • Proof. Volume has inflected for three quarters, but margin has not. Part of the money that generates volume is the money meant to rebuild margin.

For the model: credit RIG of 2–2.5%, net savings of about CHF 1.5–2bn by 2027, and about +30–50bp of portfolio mix. Treat the 17%+ mid-term margin as unproven until H1-27. The asymmetric internal upside, absent from consensus and management talk, is the L'Oréal stake (about 19% of market capitalisation). It is the only lever that could transform capital returns, and the board has not signalled any intent to pull it.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement52C-1465C-8640C-5936C-8155D-0330
Group judgement

Risks

C+5273rd percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nestlé's risk profile has changed. Fundamental risk is lower than a year ago:

  • volume is recovering;
  • the recall is fading;
  • commodity costs are falling;
  • the cost programme is ahead of plan.

Valuation and sentiment risk is higher:

  • the shares are at a 52-week high;
  • they re-rated about 1 turn without EPS upgrades;
  • the consensus target price equals the price;
  • Nestlé is the most leveraged member of a sector whose leadership is fading in a 5%-yield world.

The fundamental tail risks are a second safety event, a franc shock, a commodity re-spike in H2-27, Russia, reverse factoring and coordinated delistings. They are individually low-to-moderate probability. They are correlated through one mechanism: each forces Nestlé back toward price or cost cuts that undo the volume strategy.

For the model:

  • Carry a franc sensitivity of ±4% EPS per 5% move.
  • Carry a commodity re-inflation scenario from H2-27 (-50 to -100bp of gross margin).
  • Remove Russia.
  • Treat a fair trough price as about CHF 70–75, about 15–20% below today. A severe case is about CHF 60.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B6580th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standards are clearly improving across execution measurement, incentives, governance and cost discipline. This is the most concrete evidence of change at Nestlé. The improvement is in systems and incentives rather than in proven outcomes. Profit has not yet inflected, and the recall and the uncommunicated pension credit show that control and disclosure standards still have gaps.

  • Execution and measurement.
  • Qualitative reviews replaced by about 50 automated KPIs, with monthly KPI reviews that "expose underperforming cells".
  • 18 underperforming business cells targeted with corrective plans.
  • Standardised working-capital KPIs on SAP across units.
  • Marketing return-on-investment tracking across 135 brands.
  • Non-working marketing spend held below 20%.
  • Incentives.
  • A mandatory RIG gatekeeper in the annual bonus.
  • Six-tier performance ratings (0–50% for weak performers, up to 150% for the best).
  • Functional bonus objectives replaced by group KPIs.
  • Long-term incentives on constant-currency EPS, relative TSR, ROIC and GHG.
  • CEO must hold 5x salary in shares; at least 50% of bonus taken in shares blocked three years.
  • Cost discipline.
  • CHF 1.7bn of cumulative savings by H1-26, ahead of plan.
  • Procurement AI across 200,000 purchase orders; vendor consolidation (7,900 to 8 distributors in some categories).
  • Shared services expanded threefold, with 30–75% cost cuts in standardised processes.
  • Capex cut to 4.8% of sales from 6.4%.
  • Executive pay pool cut from CHF 70m (13 members) to CHF 55m (11) for 2027.
  • Governance. New Audit and Finance Committee; Lead Independent Director (Dick Boer); 12-year term limits.
  • Gaps.
  • The 30bp non-cash pension credit in H1-26 was not communicated (MS, Redburn).
  • Recall: supplier oversight failed.
  • A French water-treatment fine (EUR 2m) is on record.
  • Restructuring and litigation charges are rising (CHF 1.7bn in FY25).
C-86

C4061st percentile

Judgement: The negative corporate developments matter because they could break the volume recovery or the margin bridge. The most material:

  • Organisational stress from cutting 16,000 roles. That is about 6% of the workforce, against typical peer programmes of 1–2% a year.
  • A widening gap between reported and underlying earnings.
  • Unresolved brand damage in infant nutrition.

  • Evidence that the H1-26 margin "beat" was non-operational.

  • Organisation.

  • Nestlé's own risk disclosures concede possible "deterioration in employee morale, disrupt[ed] internal control structures".
  • Expert calls cite demotivation and talent leaving for Danone and others.
  • A former director: "a bank that sells food", with a "profound lack of urgency".
  • Earnings quality.
  • Brokers'-basis EPS against reported basic EPS: 4.80 vs 4.24 (FY23), 4.77 vs 4.19 (FY24), 4.42 vs 3.51 (FY25). The gap widened from about 12% to about 21%.

  • Brand trust.

  • Infant formula sales were about 10% below pre-recall levels in mid-2026.

  • Plausible next negatives.
  • A Russia cash write-off of up to CHF 1bn.
  • Stranded costs after Waters and VMS.
  • Reverse-factoring dependence (CHF 6.21bn, 37% of payables).

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3615th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: Nestlé's culture is not yet congruent. Historically it was:

  • bureaucratic and matrixed, "a bank that sells food";
  • slow, "step-driven with numerous approvals";
  • insular, hiring from within.

Respect and technical competence were real strengths. The new leadership is driving a deliberate shift to a performance culture: KPIs, accountability, local P&L ownership, the RIG gatekeeper. That shift is colliding with the demoralising effect of 16,000 job cuts and executive churn. Goals, strategy and incentives are now aligned on paper. Behaviour and morale have not caught up. This is the opposite of a Lifco or Ryanair system where everything reinforces everything else.

  • Evidence of friction.
  • Former directors: "profound lack of urgency".
  • Large coffee brands take up to a year to respond to niche entrants.
  • Local launches forced to absorb central costs.
  • Expert calls: demotivated white-collar teams.
  • Staff cost per head below peers (Jefferies), which may weaken motivation.
  • Unions resisting cuts (Spain).
  • Nestlé's own risk factor: morale and internal controls.
  • Evidence of change.
  • The KitKat campaign signed off fast; nine Cannes Lions.
  • Monthly automated KPI reviews.
  • Six-tier bonus ratings.
  • Navratil admits "sub-optimal cross-divisional alignment".
  • Model implication. Culture adds execution risk to the cost programme and to sustaining RIG. It is a reason for wider error bands, not for a different central case.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

B5575th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Six sudden, high-impact fundamental risks matter for the model. The most plausible within 18 months are a second food-safety event, a sharp franc jump, and a commodity re-spike. The largest by EPS impact is a franc shock.

  1. Second food-safety or recall event (infant formula or other). - Plausibility: low to moderate. Two events have already occurred in Europe (infant formula cereulide; frozen pizza contamination), plus Austrian and French criminal and judicial probes. - Impact: the Q1-26 recall cost about CHF 200–270m of sales and 90bp of quarterly organic growth, plus brand damage (formula sales about 10% below pre-recall levels). A repeat would hit China and European nutrition share hard and could trigger regulatory penalties.
  2. Franc shock (+10–20% against USD and EUR). - Plausibility: low to moderate, given safe-haven flows in a European election risk-off (MS). The SNB tolerates appreciation. - Impact: reported sales -3% to -6%; reported EPS about -8% to -16% (about 4% EPS per 5% move); margin -20 to -60bp.
  3. Coffee or cocoa re-spike (El Niño, Brazil or Vietnam crop failure). - Plausibility: moderate. MS expects commodity costs to rise again and hit the P&L in H2-27. - Impact: the 2025 analogue cost about 110bp of gross margin and about 360–540bp in coffee segments. Hedges delay but do not prevent it.
  4. Russia write-off and political seizure elsewhere. - Plausibility: high for a Russia write-off (CHF up to about 1bn of cash; Jefferies). Low elsewhere, but Nestlé has concentrated exposure to emerging markets (about 41% of sales). - Impact: a reported charge, plus the loss of about 4% of profits (already in motion).
  5. Collapse of the supply-chain finance programme. - Plausibility: low. - Impact: CHF 6.21bn of reverse-factored payables; a one-off cash outflow of several billion, putting the dividend under pressure.
  6. Coordinated European retailer delistings in a deflation fight. - Plausibility: moderate. Intermarché delisted 30 brands in early 2026, and the MS "new playbook" note describes the method. - Impact: Europe (about 20% of sales) RIG to negative for one or two quarters; group organic -0.3 to -0.6pp.

Also present: a sharp fall in L'Oréal's value (about 19% of Nestlé's market capitalisation); a US tariff reversal on Swiss Nespresso; an ultra-processed food litigation wave in the US.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F3012th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The drivers are concentrated, and the two that matter most are outside the company's control. Measured on the company's own basis, EPS was flat over FY19 to FY25 and grows about 2.2% a year in the base case to FY30 (about 1.2% a year to FY27). That small net change hides a large change in composition. Two drivers produce nearly all of the improvement: the franc is assumed to cost about 1.6 points a year, against about 4 in the past, and the margin rises about 20bp a year where it fell about 25bp a year. Two drivers produce nearly all of the deterioration: buybacks stop, and nominal organic growth is lower because pricing is lower. The turnaround itself (volume, savings, advertising) is necessary to the margin line but is mostly consumed inside it. For predictability this is unfavourable. A forecast that improves on history through many small, independent, company-controlled drivers is robust. This one improves through a currency and a commodity cycle.

The composition of EPS growth, past and forecast (approximate points of EPS growth a year; my estimates from the files, except the FY19 EPS of CHF 4.41, which is from my own knowledge of the company's published results).

Driver FY19 to FY25 FY25 to FY30, base case Change
Organic growth About +5.1 (inflated by pricing of about 8% in 2022 and 2023) About +3.7 -1.4
Currency and perimeter together About -5.7 (reported revenue fell 0.6% a year) About -3.2 (currency -1.6, perimeter -1.6) +2.5
Margin About -1.5 (17.6% to 16.1%) About +1.2 (16.1% to 17.1%) +2.7
Net finance cost About -0.7 (CHF 1.0bn to 1.7bn) About +0.1 (flat at CHF 1.4bn to 1.5bn) +0.8
Share count About +1.9 (2,880m to 2,572m shares) About +0.1 -1.8
Associates, tax and other About +0.9 About +0.3 -0.6
EPS About 0.0 (CHF 4.41 to 4.42) About +2.2 (CHF 4.42 to 4.92) +2.2
  • Reading the table. The gross improvement is about 6 points a year (currency and perimeter +2.5, margin +2.7, finance cost +0.8). The gross deterioration is about 3.8 points (organic growth -1.4, share count -1.8, other -0.6). Currency and margin are 87% of the improvement.
  • The currency line is almost all the franc. The perimeter takes about 1.6 points a year off revenue in the forecast. My estimate is that disposals took a similar amount in the past (skin health, United States ice cream and North American waters all left between 2019 and 2021; this is from my own knowledge and is not in the files). The change of +2.5 is therefore the franc: about -4 a year then, about -1.6 a year now. In every year from 2023 to 2026 translation was negative (-7.8%, -3.7%, -5.7% and about -2.4%).
  • About half of the margin line is the commodity cycle. The past fall of 150bp includes the 110bp lost in FY25 to coffee and cocoa. The forecast rise of 100bp includes about +30bp of commodity relief net of give-backs and about +27bp of net perimeter mix. The part earned by management (net savings of about 80bp, less the rise in advertising and cost inflation) is the smaller part.
  • What the table says about the turnaround. Volume growth roughly doubles (RIG of about 2.3% against 0.8% in FY24 and FY25), but in EPS terms that is worth only about 2% of operating profit a year, and the base case spends it. The change in EPS trajectory would occur in much the same size with RIG of 1.5%, provided the franc and the commodity cycle behaved as assumed. It would not occur with RIG of 3% if the franc repeated 2023 to 2025.

The 18-month window: the FY27 bridge by how knowable each driver is. The second set's bridge from FY26 EPS of CHF 4.55 to FY27 EPS of CHF 4.53, regrouped.

Driver Effect on FY27 EPS How knowable today
Russia absent for nine more months About -3.2% Known. The company has given nine-month sales, UTOP and cash flow
Pension credit not repeated About -0.9% Known
Waters, vitamins and ice cream leave part-way through the year About -0.7% Contracted or near-contracted; timing uncertain by about a quarter (±0.4%)
Volume and price (organic growth of 3.6%) About +3.6% High, to about ±0.6 points
Net savings (+30bp) About +1.8% High on delivery; the net figure is never disclosed
Commodity relief net of give-backs (+35bp) About +2.1% Medium to low. Hedge cover is undisclosed and the European negotiations are confidential
Advertising, stranded costs, depreciation and other cost creep About -2.4% Medium. Largely management's choice
Currency (-1.0%) About -0.9% Low. Exogenous
Finance cost and associates About +0.2% Medium to high
Net About -0.4%
  • The negatives are the knowable part. About 4.8 points of decline (Russia, the pension credit, the other exits) are known to within about half a point. This is why I hold the direction of the FY27 gap to consensus with high confidence.
  • The positives are where the uncertainty lies. The engine's +5.1% is the sum of one steady item (volume and price), one well-delivered item (savings) and one item that is neither disclosed nor controlled (the price-cost spread on coffee and cocoa), less discretionary reinvestment. The FY27 outcome inside the 18 months therefore turns on two things: whether Nestlé keeps about half of the coffee and cocoa relief through the European negotiating round of early 2027, and where the franc trades.
  • Comparison with the past four to six years. In FY23 to FY25 the dominant drivers were pricing (about 8%, then 1.5%, then 2.8%), commodity inflation, the franc and buybacks. Over the next 18 months they are volume, commodity deflation, the franc and the perimeter. Three of the four have changed. The franc is the only one common to both periods, and it is the one with the worst record: it has gone against Nestlé in each of the last four years.

Peers and precedents on concentrated drivers (AlphaSense, 18 November 2026). Orkla's dependence on its associate Jotun (19% of net profit in 2025, after 35% in 2023) and Lindt's 3% to 5% cuts to estimates after a 4% to 5% currency update show how one exogenous driver can dominate a staple's reported result. Nestlé has two such drivers in the franc and L'Oréal (about 10% of net income), and a third in coffee.

2b

Internal corporate change forces

C-1176C-1270C-1342C-1465C-1574C-1648C-1733C-1850C-1934C-2058C-8748C-2166C-2244
C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

A7692nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The change is near-total at the top, and it signals a deliberate break with Nestlé's insider-led establishment at board level, alongside continuity of operator culture at executive level. Three groups have turned over:

  • Chief executive. There have been three CEOs in about 13 months. Mark Schneider left suddenly in August 2024 after weak trading. Laurent Freixe was dismissed in September 2025 for an undisclosed relationship with a subordinate. Philipp Navratil, a 24-year insider and ex-Nespresso CEO, took over on 1 September 2025.
  • Chair. Pablo Isla, ex-Inditex, is the first outsider ever to chair the board. He took over six months early, from Paul Bulcke.
  • Board, executive committee and CFO.
  • The board is 100% independent. New directors are Thomas Jordan (ex-SNB), Fama Francisco (P&G), Luca Maestri (ex-Apple CFO) and Rainer Blair (Danaher CEO).
  • Board committees were restructured (Audit and Finance; Nomination and Corporate Governance; Science, Technology and Sustainability).
  • CFO Anna Manz (from March 2024; ex-Diageo, Johnson Matthey, LSEG) now also oversees M&A, after Sanjay Bahadur retired.
  • Nestlé Health Science's separate executive seat went with Anna Mohl's departure.
  • New appointments: Nespresso CEO Alfonso Gonzalez Loeschen; Zone Americas CEO Jeff Hamilton; General Counsel Manuela Bernasconi from January 2027.

What the change signals:

  • Positive. The board has taken control of performance accountability. The outsider Chair and a CFO-led M&A function point to tighter capital discipline. Navratil's first acts show an urgent, accountable operator:
  • he doubled Fuel for Growth to CHF 3bn;
  • he named 16,000 job cuts publicly, "breaking historical communication norms";
  • he put a RIG gatekeeper into bonuses.
  • Negative. Two sudden CEO exits in 13 months reveal earlier governance failure. The executive bench is internal and thin ("insular promotional bias"). Navratil's experience is narrow (coffee and Nespresso). Expert calls report talent leaving for competitors.
  • No activist and no controlling shareholder. Free float is 100% and short interest near 0%. Third Point (2017) is gone.
  • Model implication. Leadership is now a modest positive for execution credibility. Do not credit "new management" with margin above what the cost programme itself delivers. The evidence so far is three quarters of rising RIG, and no profit inflection yet.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B7080th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The strategy has changed materially, from "pricing-led breadth" to "volume-led focus". It is well articulated and adequately resourced by the cost programme, though not by the balance sheet. It is the most coherent strategy statement Nestlé has made in a decade. Its weakness is that the money funding growth is the same money expected to rebuild margin.

  • What changed.
  • Five priorities: winning portfolio, RIG-led growth, transformation and efficiency, cash and capital allocation, performance culture.
  • Four core categories: Coffee 29%, Food & Snacks 29%, Petcare 21%, Nutrition 21%.
  • Growth platforms widened from 10% to 30% of sales, about CHF 27bn: cold coffee, pet therapeutics, medical nutrition, KitKat extensions. Target is high-single-digit growth, with the core at 3–4%.
  • Brand focus: media-supported brands cut from over 400 to 135–150, with non-working marketing below 20%.
  • Structure: zones cut from five to three. Nestlé Health Science folded into the zones. P&L ownership returned to local markets.
  • Portfolio exits: ice cream, Waters (JV), mainstream VMS, Blue Bottle.
  • How it is resourced.
  • Fuel for Growth: CHF 3.0bn gross by end-2027. CHF 1.1bn delivered in FY25 (CHF 350m ahead of plan); CHF 1.7bn cumulative by H1-26; CHF 2.0bn targeted for FY26.
  • CHF 600m of incremental growth-platform investment in 2026.
  • A&P raised to 8.9% of sales.
  • Capacity built: Batavia pet (USD 550m), Thailand pet (CHF 157m), Italy pet (CHF 520m), a Thai coffee plant and the Arvin automated distribution centre (USD 330m).
  • How it is articulated.
  • Clear KPIs: RIG of at least 2%, organic growth of 4%+, margin of 17%+ medium term, FCF above CHF 9bn.
  • About 50 standardised KPIs and the RIG bonus gatekeeper.
  • CEO quote: "driving RIG-led growth is our number one priority".
  • The weak point. Jefferies estimates net savings of CHF 2bn of the CHF 3bn gross. Management promises both reinvestment and margin recovery. Wisdom Library: "Growth Funded by Incentives Cannot Fund Margin". The cash-flow test is whether volume holds once A&P stops rising.
  • Not used to resource the strategy. The balance sheet (2.85–3.2x leverage) and the L'Oréal stake (about CHF 42bn).
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F4222nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Guidance has not been raised. It has been reset lower and then defended. FY26 guidance was narrowed, not lifted, and margin phasing was trimmed. The mid-term ambition was set at a deliberately lower base in November 2024 and re-affirmed under Navratil. The direction of change is credibility-building, not ambition-raising.

  • FY26.
  • Organic growth: "around 3% up to 4%" became "3% to 4%" in July 2026 and was reaffirmed in October.
  • Margin: "improve versus 16.1%", but July changed H2 to "broadly similar" to H1's 16.4%, dropping the earlier expectation of second-half strengthening. That implies a lower exit rate into FY27 (Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank).
  • FCF: above CHF 9bn.
  • Currency: guided at about -3% (July), with a range of -3% to -6% cited, then about -2% (October).
  • Mid-term. Organic growth of 4%+ with RIG of at least 2%. Margin of 17.0%+. That margin target sits below the 17.5–18.5% of 2017–2020 and the 18.5% ambition reset at the 2024 CMD.
  • Cost target raised. Fuel for Growth went from CHF 2.5bn to CHF 3.0bn, with "no ceiling placed" on it (Navratil). The FY26 savings target rose to CHF 2.0bn cumulative after FY25 delivered CHF 1.1bn against CHF 700m planned. This is the one target genuinely raised.
  • Model implication. Treat the 17%+ mid-term margin as a 2028–29 aspiration that consensus does not yet price (consensus EBIT margin is 16.4% FY27 and 16.7% FY28). The upside lever in guidance terms is savings, not top line.
C-14To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?B6580th percentilefull answer under 2a Internal risks
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

B+7487th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Perimeter reduction is substantial, specific and dated. It is the strongest structural change Nestlé has made since the 2018 US confectionery sale. About 9–10% of FY25 sales leaves the consolidated perimeter over 2026–27. Most of it is lower-margin, slower-growth business, so margin and growth mix improve mechanically. Two caveats: the cash released is small against leverage, and Russia is a forced exit of a high-margin unit.

  • The exits.
  • Waters & Premium Beverages to Peranel. 50:50 JV with Platinum Equity at CHF 4.5bn enterprise value (also quoted as EUR 4.9bn). About CHF 3.6bn of sales. Net cash CHF 2.8bn on closing in H1 2027. About +30bp to group margin, +10bp to ROIC, about 0.1x deleveraging, roughly EPS-neutral in year one (Redburn). It also removes PPWR and French water-litigation exposure.
  • Mainstream VMS to Yellow Wood. Nature's Bounty, Puritan's Pride, Osteo Bi-Flex; USD 1.2bn of sales; USD 1.0bn price (some sources say CHF 0.8bn); under 1% EPS dilution (MS). It triggered the CHF 1.3bn H1-26 write-down. Premium VMS (Solgar, Pure Encapsulations, Garden of Life) is kept.
  • Remaining ice cream to Froneri. About CHF 1bn of sales (Canada, Chile, Peru, China, Malaysia, Thailand), in advanced negotiation; earlier quoted at CHF 1.5bn of proceeds (Jefferies). Froneri already paid a CHF 1.98bn extraordinary distribution in 2025.
  • Blue Bottle sold to Centurium Capital.
  • Russia deconsolidated by administration from end-September 2026: about 2% of sales, about 4% of profits.
  • Simplification.
  • Zones cut from five to three; Nestlé Health Science folded into the zones; reportable segments cut from six to five.
  • Brands supported cut from over 400 to 135–150; North America cut SKUs by 20%.
  • Additions, deliberately small. The remaining 51% of yfood (about EUR 150m of sales) and full ownership of Hsu Fu Chi.
  • Model implications. 1. Rebase FY27–28 sales for the exits, including half a year of Waters in FY27. 2. Add about +30–50bp of mix benefit to group margin by FY28. 3. Do not assume the proceeds (about CHF 4bn) fund buybacks; they go to debt. 4. Allow for stranded overheads (unquantified; Unilever's analogue is EUR 400–500m).
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F488th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Nestlé is entering few genuinely new markets. It is redeploying within existing categories toward higher-growth niches. That is sensible given the balance-sheet constraint and the poor record of the 2019–21 acquisitions, but it means new markets will not add a growth leg over the model horizon.

  • Product adjacencies, all small.
  • Cold coffee and out-of-home coffee (Nescafé Gold Espresso, concentrates; a CHF 3.5bn+ opportunity).
  • Pet therapeutics and Purina Vet Direct.
  • GLP-1 companion food (Vital Pursuit) and protein (Vital Proteins, Orgain).
  • Medical nutrition (Compleat, Resource, Peptamen).
  • Smart food (yfood).
  • Cocoa-free confectionery (ChoViva in Germany).
  • Geographic moves are capacity, not entry.
  • Thailand: a CHF 157m pet export hub and a new owned Nescafé plant after the Quality Coffee Products JV was terminated (upheld by the Singapore High Court, May 2026).
  • Italy pet plant (CHF 520m).
  • India: a nutraceutical JV with Dr Reddy's (option to 60% after six years), rural expansion to over 216,000 villages, quick commerce.
  • Judged against the past. The 2019–21 moves into new spaces were poor: Bountiful (USD 5.75bn, now written down), Aimmune/Palforzia (USD 2.1bn impairment) and Blue Bottle (missed its CHF 1bn turnover target, now sold). The current restraint is the right lesson learned.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F338th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: Nestlé is not raising prices in real terms. Price increases in 2022–25 were cost pass-through, nominal and only partly successful: volume fell and margin still compressed. Pricing is now deliberately below food inflation, at 1.1% in Q3-26 and 0.2% in Europe, to rebuild volume. Real pricing power is at most latent, in coffee and premium systems. Management is not signalling it. Its stated rule is to "take price only where consumers can tolerate them".

  • The record.
  • 2022–23: pricing of about 8% protected margin only partly (EBIT margin 16.9–17.2%). RIG fell to -0.3% in FY23. Nestlé was "the biggest value share loser in instant coffee" (MS).
  • FY25: 2.8% pricing on coffee pass-through. Gross margin still fell 110bp, which is pass-through that lagged cost.
  • Starbucks at-home: an 8% price rise brought a 4% fall in volume and mix.
  • Now.
  • Pricing roll-off with deliberate restraint: H1-26 2.1%, Q3-26 1.1% against consensus 1.8%.
  • Europe 0.2% against consensus 1.3%.
  • Nespresso pricing 1.4% as it laps "exceptionally high" prior-year rises.
  • Snack peers are cutting price: Orkla Snacks -1.5%; Lindt cut its 100g recommended retail price from EUR 2.69 to EUR 2.19 in Germany.
  • Where latent power exists.
  • Jefferies argues premium coffee was "under-priced relative to cumulative CPI", so coffee pricing has room over time.
  • Coffee price elasticities were "better than feared" (Q1-26 coffee RIG 3.5% alongside 5.7% pricing).
  • Pro Plan and wet cat carry premiums; medical nutrition is prescriber-led.
  • Model implication. Assume pricing of about 0.5–1.5% a year for 2027–28: roughly in line with, or below, input cost inflation over the cycle, with near-zero or negative pricing in European coffee and confectionery during 2027. Pricing should not be a margin driver.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5035th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Nestlé is stepping down from M&A, not up. It is a net seller, with only small bolt-ons. That is disciplined and sensible given leverage and the recent record of value-destroying deals. It means M&A will neither add to nor threaten the earnings model over the horizon, apart from disposal dilution.

  • Recent acquisition record (poor).
  • Bountiful vitamins (2021, USD 5.75bn): a CHF 1.3bn write-down on reclassification, about 23% of the price (Risks report), and the 18% margin target missed by two years.
  • Aimmune/Palforzia: a USD 2.1bn impairment.
  • Blue Bottle: target missed, now sold.
  • Expert calls describe failed integrations in health science (IT failures causing months-long supply shortages).
  • Now.
  • yfood's remaining 51% (EUR 150m of sales, double-digit growth) and Hsu Fu Chi consolidation are the only buys.
  • M&A now reports to the CFO, after Sanjay Bahadur retired.
  • Morgan Stanley: leverage and the dividend "constrain large-scale strategic M&A".
  • Model implication. No acquisition spend beyond CHF 0.1–0.5bn a year. Disposal proceeds of about CHF 4bn in 2027 reduce net debt.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F348th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The balance sheet is being repaired, not used. There is no buyback, and none is mooted. The dividend is progressive but now absorbs most free cash flow. Management's stated priority is moving leverage toward the middle of its 2.0–3.0x range. Shareholder-return upside depends on de-leveraging first, and on the L'Oréal stake, which management treats as passive.

  • Buyback.
  • The CHF 20bn programme completed in 2024: shares fell from 2,880m (FY19) to 2,572m (FY24), about -2.3% a year.
  • Since then: FY25 net buyback CHF 213m, H1-26 CHF 49m.
  • Dividend.
  • CHF 3.10 for FY25, the 66th consecutive year held or raised. About CHF 7.97bn in cash, a 70% payout of underlying EPS and 72% of the Viewforth EPS definition.
  • It absorbs about 87% of FY25 FCF (CHF 9.15bn).
  • Consensus DPS: 3.15 / 3.20 / 3.27 (FY26–28).
  • Leverage.
  • Net debt CHF 56.3bn (June 2026). Company 2.85x at end-FY25; FactSet 3.2x; MS 3.1x. Up from 0.8x before 2017 and 2.4x in 2023.
  • The highest of the peer set: Danone 1.7x, Orkla 2.2x, Unilever 2.5x, Tesco 2.0x.
  • De-leveraging path.
  • FCF after dividend of about CHF 1.2–2.5bn a year, plus about CHF 4bn of disposals in 2027.
  • Consensus total debt falls from CHF 56.3bn (FY26) to CHF 47.0bn (FY28).
  • That would bring leverage to about 2.3–2.5x by end-2028, the earliest realistic window to restart buybacks.
  • L'Oréal. About CHF 42bn, management "passive". Selling even part of the stake would transform the capital-return picture. It is not mooted.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–58

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Several smaller positive changes reinforce the main programme and are plausibly under-modelled:

  • Petcare capacity is unblocked. It turns a supply constraint into volume.
  • Marketing is being modernised. Concentration, digital and AI content raise return on A&P.
  • Supply-chain automation and procurement AI create a second, post-2027 savings wave.
  • Emerging-market digital channels are compounding.

None alone moves the model much. Together they make RIG of 2%+ more plausible.

  • Petcare. US wet cat capacity (Batavia, Glendale) let Purina "capture previously unmet demand" and take share from General Mills. Petcare organic growth was about 5% in Q3-26, with retail sell-in matching sell-out.
  • Marketing.
  • "Marketing of the Future": over 50,000 staff upskilled.
  • Half of social budgets moved to AI micro-influencer content.
  • Media brands cut to 135.
  • The KitKat "heist" campaign won nine Cannes Lions after rapid local sign-off.
  • Automation and AI.
  • The Arvin distribution centre, the largest automated storage system in the network.
  • Autonomous trucks in China (3–5% less fuel).
  • Procurement AI and vendor consolidation.
  • "No ceiling" on savings (Navratil).
  • Channels. E-commerce 21.6% of sales (+15.3% in Q3); India quick commerce about 40% of e-commerce; Nespresso app in 21 markets.
  • Market share. The rate of value share loss is down 60%. Billionaire brands are share-neutral for the first time in about a decade.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4823rd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: No controlling shareholder or activist exists, and none has signalled anything. The board under Isla is the only controlling actor, and it has shown appetite for change in people and governance, not in capital structure. The scope for an externally forced change, such as an L'Oréal sale, a buyback or a break-up, is therefore low over 18 months.

  • Ownership. No activist in the AlphaSense library. Free float 100%. Short interest about 0%; hedge-fund net exposure 0.2%. Third Point's 2017 campaign (1.3% stake) did produce the 17.5–18.5% margin target, the CHF 20bn buyback and the US confectionery sale.
  • Board behaviour.
  • Accelerated chair succession; dismissed a CEO within a year.
  • Added macro (Jordan) and consumer (Francisco) expertise; restructured committees.
  • Isla brings Inditex supply-chain discipline.
  • No public statement on the L'Oréal stake or on capital returns.
  • Voting rights. Capped at 5% per holder. That is an anti-control feature: it makes a hostile approach or a creeping stake harder, which limits external pressure as a source of change.
  • Plausible triggers. A sustained de-rating or failure of the turnaround could revive activism, as in 2017. L'Oréal's own family dynamics could create an exit route.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C6664th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The positive corporate changes are strong and broad: leadership, strategy, incentives, cost, portfolio and culture all changing at once. They are well resourced in operating money (CHF 3bn of savings) but poorly resourced in capital: leverage is at the top of the range and the dividend absorbs about 87% of FCF. On "Massive Corporate Change" terms this is a genuine transformation, but it is three quarters old. The evidence so far is in volume, not profit.

  • Breadth. 1. CEO, Chair, board and CFO all new since 2024. 2. Strategy: four categories, growth platforms at 30%, RIG-led growth. 3. Incentives: RIG gatekeeper, six-tier ratings. 4. Cost: CHF 3bn, 16,000 roles. 5. Portfolio: about 10% of sales leaving. 6. Organisation: three zones, local P&Ls.
  • Depth of evidence.
  • RIG has risen for three quarters (1.2%, 1.8%, 2.8%).
  • Savings are ahead of plan (CHF 1.7bn cumulative).
  • FCF in H1-26 rose 46% to CHF 3.38bn.
  • Margin has not inflected: H1-26 was 16.4%, including a 30bp one-off, against 16.5% in H1-25.
  • Where it could stall.
  • Savings mostly reinvested.
  • Volume flattered by weather and phasing.
  • Commodity inflation returning in H2 2027.
  • Organisational fatigue.
  • Wisdom Library: "Turnarounds Take Longer Than You Think" and "False Friends". The Risks report's own Analysis of Competing Hypotheses verdict was "False Friend": price-led growth masking volume weakness.
  • Q3 contradicts that verdict on volume. Q3-26 had RIG of 2.8% and pricing of 1.1%, so volume now leads price. The Risks report's verdict therefore needs updating. That report also leaned on the method trap "The Vaguest Explanation Wins a Fewest-Contradictions Contest".
  • Model implication. Credit volume recovery and savings delivery. Withhold credit for a margin above about 16.5–16.8% until a reported half shows it, H1-27 at the earliest.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

D4433rd percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The staircase is well populated with dated steps, but few of them are novel. Most are already in consensus:

  • the recall lap;
  • Peranel's closing;
  • savings completion;
  • the commodity hedge roll-off.

The genuinely novel steps, which could move the narrative from "turnaround in progress" to "earnings recovery under way", are:

  • a reported gross- and operating-margin inflection at H1-27 that exceeds consensus's +10bp for FY27;
  • leverage reaching about 2.5x and a capital-return restart, from 2028;
  • a decision on L'Oréal, which is unsignalled and low probability.

The negative steps on the same staircase are a possible Russia write-off at FY26 and commodity re-inflation from H2-27. These bound the case.

Next 6 months (to mid-May 2027).

  1. FY26 results and FY27 guidance, 18 February 2027. Well-founded. - The first full year under Navratil: margin above 16.1%, FCF above CHF 9bn, cumulative savings of CHF 2.0bn. - Novelty is moderate. The FY27 guidance wording and any medium-term restatement are the news. - Risk: a Russia write-off (up to about CHF 1bn).
  2. Q1-27 sales, late April 2027. Well-founded, mechanical, not novel. - Laps the 90bp recall drag of Q1-26, a boost of about 0.5–0.9pp to reported organic growth. - The first quarter of coffee and cocoa give-back pressure in Europe.
  3. AGM, April 2027. Dividend about CHF 3.15. The 67th year. Not novel.

Months 6 to 18 (mid-2027 to mid-2028).

  1. Peranel closing, H1 2027. Well-founded and contracted. CHF 2.8bn of cash, about +30bp of margin. Mainstream VMS and ice-cream closings follow. Low novelty: announced.
  2. H1-27 results, late July 2027. Moderately founded. The first reported gross-margin step-up from the hedge roll-off on coffee and cocoa. - Coffee segment margins rebuild (lost about 360–540bp in 2025). - It also laps the 30bp pension credit. - This is the most important novel step. It tests net savings and the 17%+ path.
  3. Fuel for Growth completion, end-2027. Well-founded on the record. CHF 3.0bn gross; white-collar CHF 1.0bn run-rate. Moderate novelty if a post-2027 programme is announced ("no ceiling").
  4. FY27 results, February 2028. Could show the margin at about 16.7–17.0% and leverage at about 2.5–2.7x. That opens the buyback question, which would be novel and is not in consensus.
  5. Possible strategy or capital markets day. Not dated in the sources; plausible under a new CEO and Chair.

Months 18 to 36 (2028–2029).

  1. Delivery against the medium-term framework. 4%+ organic with RIG of at least 2%, margin 17%+. Moderately founded; needs volume above recent history.
  2. Resumption of share buybacks once leverage is near or below 2.5x. Novel; moderately founded on the debt path.
  3. Growth platforms at scale. CHF 27bn growing high single digit; GLP-1 and protein ranges; quick commerce in emerging markets. Low novelty; incremental.
  4. L'Oréal stake monetisation. Highly novel, poorly founded: no signal from board or management. It would transform leverage and capital returns.

Offsetting negative steps.

  • Commodity costs returning to the P&L in H2-27 (MS).
  • Continued franc strength.
  • GLP-1 drag.
  • Euro election risk-off in 2027.
