Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5849th percentile

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5849th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nestlé holds number one or two positions in concentrated, high-barrier categories: petcare, coffee systems, infant and medical nutrition. These produce most of its profit. They sit inside a value chain where:

  • upstream suppliers are weak, and getting weaker as procurement AI spreads;
  • downstream retailers are strong, and getting stronger through data, media and premium private label.

Industry structure therefore protects Nestlé's margins at the category level and threatens them at the channel level. The binding constraint is retailers, not competitors.

The past three years show what that means:

  • pricing power that was really cost pass-through;
  • volume lost to private label and focused challengers;
  • a self-admitted multi-year share loss.

The recent stabilisation (the rate of share loss cut 60%, Q3-26 RIG 2.8%) fits the historical pattern of staples turnarounds that rebuild volume within two to three years. Unilever and Danone are the precedents.

Two predictable industry dynamics in 2027:

  • Cost-led price deflation in European coffee and confectionery, driven by retailers and short-hedged private labels, which caps the gross-margin benefit of commodity relief.
  • Continued slow drift of mass-market food toward own labels.

For the model: segment Nestlé into a protected core (pet, coffee systems, nutrition; about 60–70% of profit) growing about 4–6% at stable or rising margin, and a contested remainder (mainstream food, confectionery, instant coffee in developed markets) growing about 0–2% at flat or slightly lower margin.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5538th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4013th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance the route to market hinders Nestlé in developed markets and helps it in emerging markets and digital channels.

  • Developed-market grocery is a structurally adverse intermediary. Consolidated buyers, private label, retail media and delisting threats sit there.
  • Digital and emerging-market general trade are helpful and growing. They are now about 21.6% of sales through e-commerce, plus a large emerging-market base.

The direction is slowly favourable as the channel mix shifts. The fragility is that Amazon and quick commerce also carry higher trade-spend and lower-margin economics.

  • Hindering.
  • European alliances delisted 30 brands at Intermarché (early 2026), leaving H1-26 Zone Europe RIG at 0.5%.
  • Walmart and Tesco enforce price ceilings; expert calls report "deeper 52-week promotional calendars".
  • Retail media (Walmart Connect, Roundel) favours own labels.
  • Customer concentration is low: the top five are 14% of receivables and none is above 5%. That limits single-customer risk but not alliance risk.
  • Helping.
  • E-commerce: 21.6% of year-to-date sales, +15.3% organic in Q3-26.
  • Nespresso direct-to-consumer, "critical for margin preservation".
  • India: quick commerce at about 40% of e-commerce, replenishment every two days, "preferred partner of choice", 5.3 million outlets, over 216,000 villages.
  • Distributor destocking has ended: US pet safety stock was cut by four days in Q2, which cost about 80bp of pet growth, and is now normalised. China moved from distributor push to consumer pull.
  • Fragile points.
  • Quick commerce carries "structurally higher trade spend".
  • Migration to Amazon "dilutes gross margins".
  • Reverse factoring (CHF 6.21bn) depends on banks continuing to fund it.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F4021st percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Existing competitors are behaving in ways that hurt Nestlé at the margin. They are reinvesting harder in volume, marketing and capacity in exactly the categories where Nestlé is rebuilding. Nestlé's position is robust in petcare and coffee systems, where it holds scale and science leadership. It is fragile in instant coffee, creamers, US frozen, mainstream confectionery and China infant formula, where focused rivals out-execute it.

  • Petcare: Mars. Larger, private, digital-aggressive, stronger with Kellanova. Purina has nonetheless gained share since adding capacity. Royal Canin and Hill's lead the premium vet channel.
  • Coffee.
  • JDE Peet's leads European fresh roast. Lavazza and Keurig hardware compete against Vertuo. Ryze (adaptogens) takes niche share at a premium.
  • Starbucks is a licence partner (Global Coffee Alliance). Its price rises cost volume.
  • Dairy and creamers. Chobani (12% of creamers, USD 1.2bn plant); Danone recovering after its own recall.
  • Infant and medical nutrition.
  • Danone recovered faster after its recall, with a smaller footprint (6 countries against Nestlé's 60+).
  • Feihe self-produces 16 core ingredients and grew HMO products 20–30% month on month.
  • Abbott pays higher trade margins in emerging markets.
  • Danone's AI-led research and Kate Farms raise the bar in medical nutrition.
  • Snacks. Mondelez is volume-led and reinvests. Lindt, as a premium specialist, protects price.
  • Fragility. The 18 underperforming cells sit in these contested areas. In petcare and premium coffee, where profit concentrates (estimated at 55–60% of trading operating profit), Nestlé's position is not fragile.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D4429th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4542nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Disruption is acting on every link of Nestlé's value chain, but in different directions:

  • Upstream suppliers. Climate and AI-driven procurement shift power toward Nestlé. Helpful.
  • Retailers. Data, media and private label shift power away from Nestlé. Harmful.
  • Consumers. GLP-1, digital channels and value-seeking move demand toward health, convenience and price. Mixed.
  • Regulators. Health and packaging rules raise costs. Mildly harmful, scale-favouring.
  • Competitors. Digital natives and AI-enabled peers lower barriers in marketing. Harmful in the mass middle.

The net leaves Nestlé moderately fragile in the mass-market middle of its portfolio and robust at the premium and science-led ends.

  • Suppliers. AI e-auctions "commoditise raw inputs", giving buyers model-based pricing linked to indices. Three hubs manage over 70% of spend. Climate raises volatility: El Niño, coffee and cocoa deficits.
  • Retailers. Retail media (80% incremental margins at Tesco); first-party data used to launch copycats; agentic commerce routing orders on price. Wisdom Library: "Orchestrator or Participant: Find the Control Point". In developed grocery the retailer holds the control point.
  • Consumers. Polarised: premium trade-up alongside value trade-down. E-commerce at 21.6%; GLP-1 lowering calorie intake 20–30% among users.
  • Competitors. Social-media-native brands bypass mass marketing. Unilever runs over 40 digital twins (+8% equipment effectiveness); Danone uses AI formulation tools.
  • Nestlé's counters. Nespresso direct-to-consumer and the app; AI procurement; Arvin automation; quick commerce in India; 50% of social spend on micro-influencers.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B+8284th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Nestlé is number one or two in every category that drives its profit, and number one overall in food and beverages. Its weaker positions are in the categories it is shrinking or that are fragmented. For the model, the lead positions mean Nestlé has nothing to catch up. The share question is defence, not attack.

  • Group. World's largest food and beverage company: CHF 89.5bn of sales, 185 countries, about 271,000 employees, factories in 75 countries.
  • Coffee (29% of sales). Global number one, with three billion-franc brands (Nescafé, Nespresso, Starbucks at-home under licence).
  • In European fresh-roast and instant coffee it is number two, with about 17% share behind JDE Peet's (Jefferies).
  • Private label holds 29% of European coffee.
  • Petcare (21%).
  • Global number two (Purina 30% against Mars 33%), with Colgate's Hill's third.
  • Number one in cat food globally.
  • The US is a near-duopoly: Nestlé and Mars hold close to half of retail.
  • Purina's US online share is under 2%, against Mars 13% and Chewy 10%.
  • Nutrition (21%).
  • Global leader in infant formula and among the leaders in medical nutrition.
  • In China it trails Feihe (17.6% all-channel share) and is losing share to Yili and Danone; Danone's Aptamil leads international brands online with 22%.
  • Food & Snacks (29%). KitKat and Maggi (over CHF 5bn combined) lead locally. In global snacking the largest player has only 3.7% share. In confectionery Nestlé trails Mars, Mondelez, Ferrero and Hershey.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-7056th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Concentration is high and steep where Nestlé earns most of its profit (coffee systems, petcare, infant and medical nutrition) and low where it earns least (prepared food and snacking). The group average therefore hides two industry structures. For modelling, petcare and coffee margins can be treated as structurally protected, and Food & Snacks margins as contestable.

  • Petcare. The top two hold about 50% of US retail and about 63% globally (Mars 33%, Nestlé 30%). Steep: number three, Hill's, is far smaller. The top five clearly hold over 30%, and numbers one and two hold far more than five times the share of numbers six to ten.
  • Coffee. An oligopoly: Nestlé, JDE Peet's, Lavazza, Keurig Dr Pepper, plus Starbucks-licensed products. The top five exceed 30% globally. The main "sixth player" is private label at 29% of European coffee, a single, large, retailer-controlled competitor.
  • Infant and medical nutrition. High concentration: Nestlé, Danone, Abbott, Reckitt/Mead Johnson, Feihe.
  • Barriers are high: clinical trials, Chinese FSMP registration of 14–30 months, hospital recommendation.
  • Danone holds over 30% of Chinese adult enteral nutrition.
  • Food and snacking. Fragmented. The largest global player holds about 3.7%, and private label sits at 14% of US dollar sales and 18% to over 40% across Europe.
  • Downstream. Retail is more concentrated than the suppliers:
  • Norway has a 97% three-chain triopoly; Finland 33.5% in two groups; UK Tesco 28%.
  • The top five grocers in developed markets hold "multiple times" the share of leading brands (MS).
  • That imbalance, not supplier concentration, is the binding fact.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F337th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Shares are fairly stable in petcare and premium coffee. They are unstable and eroding in instant coffee, single-serve compatibles, US creamers, US frozen and China infant formula. The main "new entrant" by share gained has been private label, followed by niche challengers. Nestlé has been a net share loser for years. The recent improvement is a slowing of loss, not a reversal.

  • Nestlé's own admission. The company "had been losing overall market share for years, driven by eighteen underperforming business cells". The rate of value share loss has since been cut by about 60%.
  • Share movements.
  • Instant coffee. Nestlé was the "biggest value share loser over twelve months" (MS), losing 57bp of value share while J.M. Smucker held steady.
  • Nespresso Original. Compatible capsules now take over 60% of Original-machine consumption since the patents expired.
  • US creamers. Chobani rose from 4% to 12% share, taking most of the volume freed by Danone's 2025 recall.
  • China infant formula. E-commerce sales fell 22% as Wyeth weakened, while Feihe and Yili gained.
  • European scanner value growth. -1.9% in April 2026 and +1.7% in August 2026, against Danone +5.1% to +6.4% (Bernstein).
  • Petcare. Purina took share from General Mills (Blue Buffalo) after adding capacity.
  • Snacking. Confectionery share was lost to Mondelez.
  • Entrants.
  • Private label. US 13.7% to 14% dollar share; Europe up 0.6pp in Norway down-trading cycles; UK about 8bp a year.
  • Challengers. Ryze (adaptogen coffee), Chobani, Freshpet (over 95% of the fresh pet category), digital-native pet subscriptions.
  • No single new entrant holds more than low single digits of Nestlé's main categories.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D4526th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Share consolidates toward the most advantaged players, not toward the largest. The most advantaged are currently:

  • focused, volume-led branded players (Unilever's power brands, Danone's protein dairy, Mondelez);
  • premium specialists;
  • retailers' own labels.

Nestlé is the largest. It is among the advantaged only in petcare, premium coffee systems and medical nutrition. In mainstream food and instant coffee it has been a share donor. Scale alone has not pulled share toward Nestlé. Execution and focus decide.

  • Evidence for consolidation toward the advantaged.
  • Unilever: 25 power brands are 78% of turnover with a 7.1% three-year growth CAGR.
  • Danone: Oikos is over EUR 1bn and US Greek yogurt is about 55% of the yogurt market.
  • Tesco and Lidl gain while Asda loses.
  • Discounters grew 11.3% a year from 2016 to 2024, against grocery at 2.1% (Bernstein).
  • Where Nestlé is advantaged.
  • Petcare: capacity, science, vet channel, wet cat.

  • Medical nutrition.
  • Emerging-market distribution: India has 5.3 million outlets and 6,000 dark stores.
  • Where it is not.
  • US frozen, mainstream confectionery and creamers.
  • China infant formula.
  • Instant coffee in developed markets.
  • The strategic response is right. Concentrating A&P on 135–150 brands and giving 30% of sales to growth platforms is the Unilever playbook. Wisdom Library: "Thematic Dispersion Within Sub-Themes Is Where Alpha Lives."
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D4826th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The listed branded competitors are rational, putting margin and return first, though all have now turned to reinvesting for volume. Three groups are volume-first and willing to accept low margins:

  • private Mars;
  • Chobani;
  • retailers' own labels.

Industry prices do fall when costs fall, through retailer pressure. They do not fall when volume falls. Brands have instead held list price and lost volume, as in 2022–24. The 2027 risk is cost-led price deflation, not a volume-led price war.

  • Rational listed peers.
  • Unilever "rationally" uses productivity to fund brands.
  • Danone keeps a guidance-corridor discipline.
  • Mondelez reinvests gross margin rather than cutting price.
  • Tesco "rationally opting to avoid unprofitable sales" (MS).
  • Volume-first players.
  • Mars, which is private, deploys "strong digital media spend to actively steal share".
  • Chobani's promoted retail value is about 2.5 times Danone's in Greek yogurt.
  • Private label sits 20–30% below national brands and is premiumising.
  • In emerging-market infant formula, Danone and Abbott pay 15% trade margins against Nestlé's 9%.
  • Prices fall with costs.
  • Lindt cut its German RRP; Orkla Snacks cut pricing 1.5%.
  • Europe pricing for Nestlé was 0.2% in Q3-26.
  • Retailers "demand immediate retail price reductions" as cocoa and coffee fall (Bernstein, MS).
  • Unilever Thailand reset prices by 50% on 700 SKUs, a sign of what happens when a brand over-prices.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3824th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: unstable competitors, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: Competitor attitude has become less stable over the last 18 months, and a "shark attack" on pricing is plausible in one specific setting. As cocoa and coffee costs fall, short-hedged private labels and retailers may cut shelf prices faster than Nestlé's longer hedges allow it to follow. That would force Nestlé either to cut price ahead of its cost relief or to lose shelf space and face delistings. Elsewhere (petcare, premium coffee) behaviour is stable.

  • What changed.
  • 2022–24: everyone raised prices and volumes fell.
  • 2025–26: the industry pivoted to volume-led growth, with Unilever, Danone, Mondelez and Nestlé all reinvesting. That raises intensity at once.
  • Mars got bigger (Kellanova). Chobani added USD 1.2bn of capacity.
  • Retailers formalised cross-border delisting playbooks (MS, 12 November 2026).
  • The hedging asymmetry.
  • Nestlé's 6–18-month coffee and cocoa hedges "widened the price gap vs competitor brands" during deflation (MS).
  • "Shorter-hedged private label competitors are positioned to aggressively leverage falling cocoa prices to cut retail prices" (MS).
  • Lindt's hedges run up to 18 months.
  • Unpredictable players. Retailer buying alliances are the closest thing to a "Michael O'Leary" actor: delistings across countries at once, and should-cost modelling.
  • Model implication. Hold a scenario in which European coffee and confectionery pricing turns negative by 1–3% in H1-27 before hedge relief arrives. That would take about 10–20bp off group gross margin for one or two quarters.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5849th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Industry structure is moderately supportive overall. It is strongly supportive in petcare, coffee systems and medical and infant nutrition, which are concentrated, have high barriers and resist private label. It is unsupportive in mainstream food and snacks and in instant coffee in Europe. The support is fragile in one dimension: it rests on brands' bargaining power against consolidated retailers, which has eroded for a decade. The upstream position is very strong. The downstream position is the weak link.

  • Value-chain economics.
  • Brand owners earn about 45–55% gross and 15–25% EBIT margins.
  • Upstream processors earn 1.5–5% EBIT. Packaging earns about 7%. Grocers earn 3–6% (Kroger 3.4–3.5%; Tesco 4.3%).
  • Nestlé sits at the richest stage (45.6% gross, 16.1% EBIT) and is deliberately asset-light upstream.
  • Where the support is robust. Clinical and regulatory barriers in medical and infant nutrition, petcare science, a duopoly, and Nespresso's direct-to-consumer channel.
  • Where it is fragile.
  • Retailer data and media power (Roundel ad revenue up 51%).
  • Private label at 29% in coffee and premiumising.
  • Hard discounters growing about 11% a year.
  • Fresh-food tension: a grocery ceiling enforced by Walmart's everyday-low-price model.
  • Model implication. Assume margin parity at best in Food & Snacks and Europe, with structural protection in pet, coffee and nutrition. Segment mix toward growth platforms is the lever that improves group-level industry support over time.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4528th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Substitutes are a moderate and slowly rising threat, concentrated in four places:

  • Appetite suppression (GLP-1) as a substitute for snack and frozen calories. Rising.
  • Home-made and fresh food against packaged infant and prepared food. Stable.
  • Compatible capsules and out-of-home coffee against Nespresso Original. Mature.
  • Fresh, raw and vet-prescribed pet diets against kibble. Rising, but Purina participates.

Coffee itself has few substitutes and benefits from GLP-1 users seeking energy.

  • GLP-1.
  • Sweet bakery spending falls 9% six months after starting GLP-1 (Cornell).
  • About 70% of former users still eat less ice cream and chocolate (DB).
  • US sweet-category volumes are expected about 3% lower by 2035 (MS).
  • Offsets: protein, portion control, premium treats. Caffeine rises with GLP-1 (MS).
  • Coffee. Compatible capsules hold over 60% of Original consumption. Coffee-shop chains substitute for at-home coffee, which Nestlé partly captures via the Starbucks licence and Nespresso out-of-home.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

C-5652nd percentile8 answers
Judgement56C-4164C-4284C-4350C-4447C-4536C-4656C-4740
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

C-5652nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nestlé's competitive advantage is wide, real and mainly scale- and science-based. It is strongest where profit concentrates: petcare, coffee systems, medical and infant nutrition, and emerging-market distribution. Brand share of mind and pricing power in the mass-market middle have been narrowing for five years against private label and focused challengers.

The test for a genuine competitive advantage is sustained ROIC above rivals and stable share. Nestlé fails both on recent evidence:

  • ROIC of 12.5%, below Unilever (19%) and Hershey (23%);
  • a self-admitted multi-year share loss.

So Nestlé has strong barriers to entry and scale, but a weakening edge against existing rivals.

The new strategy has three elements: concentrating A&P on 135–150 brands, pricing below inflation, and building capacity in petcare and coffee. It is the right response, and early evidence is positive: the rate of share loss is down 60%, billionaire brands are share-neutral, and Q3 RIG was 2.8%. But it is "heroic" rather than gravitational. It costs money that would otherwise rebuild margin.

For modelling, the competitive position supports:

  • organic growth of about 3.5–4%;
  • margin stability to modest expansion in the core;
  • no real pricing tailwind;
  • continued price investment in the contested categories.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C+6471st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Nestlé holds a wide set of moats.

  • The durable ones are scale-based: procurement, distribution reach, R&D and science, and emerging-market route to market.
  • In the profit core, petcare science and medical and infant nutrition add clinical and regulatory barriers. These are strong.
  • Brand-based customer moats are mixed: strong in Pro Plan, Nespresso Vertuo, Nescafé in emerging markets, KitKat and Maggi, and weakening in instant coffee in developed markets, Nespresso Original, creamers and US frozen.
  • The culture and knowledge moat is weak and only now being rebuilt.

Overall the moat is wide but has been narrowing at the edges for five years. New management is trying to widen it by concentrating spend. Whether that is working is not yet proven.

  • Scale and procurement. Strong; stable to widening. Three hubs manage over 70% of spend and Nestrade centralises hedging. Shared services cut costs 30–75% in standardised processes. AI procurement and vendor consolidation are now being added.
  • Distribution. Strong; widening in emerging markets. 185 countries. India: 5.3 million outlets, 216,000+ villages and 6,000 dark stores. Low customer concentration: the top five are 14% of receivables. Category captaincy with retailers. More than 30 billion-franc brands.
  • Science, regulatory and clinical. Strong; at risk after the recall.
  • Medical nutrition needs double-blind trials; Chinese FSMP registration takes 14–30 months.
  • Purina's veterinary science and therapeutic diets.
  • The Nestlé Research Center; more than 100 million quality tests a year.
  • The recall and China share loss show the trust moat can be dented.
  • Ecosystems and switching costs. Mixed; narrowing in Original, holding in Vertuo. Nespresso Original owners use under 40% Nestlé capsules. Vertuo is patented, but on the flat part of the S-curve in Europe. Active consumers are growing again.
  • Brand share of mind. Mixed; narrowing in the mass middle.
  • Private label at 29% of European coffee; instant coffee the biggest value-share loser; younger consumers see instant as "unauthentic".
  • Against that, billionaire brands are share-neutral for the first time in a decade.
  • Wisdom Library warning: "Share of the Consumer Mind (and When Brand Is Just a Search Fee)". A mainstream brand's premium can dissolve when an AI shopping agent or a retailer app compares prices.
  • Culture and knowledge. Weak; being rebuilt. "Step-driven", "bureaucratic", "insular". It is now being pushed toward a performance culture.
  • Measured test (Towson). ROIC above WACC is sustained but falling: 12.5% in FY25, trailing Unilever's 19% and Hershey's 23%. Market share is not stable. That puts most of Nestlé's advantage at the "barriers to entry and scale" level rather than the "competitive fortress" level.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A8496th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Nestlé's scale is unmatched in packaged food. It is about 3.5 times Danone and about 2.5 times Unilever's post-Foods home and personal care business, with the industry's largest A&P budget and procurement spend. Scale confers four things:

  • lower input costs;
  • unrivalled distribution;
  • the capacity to absorb regulation and fund science;
  • low dependence on any single customer.

It does not confer pricing power over consolidated retailers, protection from focused challengers, or growth. Scale has proven necessary but not sufficient. Wisdom Library: "Scale Needs a Bounded Market".

  • Size against peers.
  • Nestlé: CHF 89.5bn of sales; 271,000 staff; about CHF 65bn of annual materials spend; A&P about CHF 7.7–8.0bn (8.6–8.9% of sales).
  • Danone: EUR 27.3bn of sales.
  • Unilever: about EUR 39bn pro forma (HPC only).
  • Orkla: NOK 71.5bn (about CHF 5.5bn).
  • Customers are as large or larger: Tesco GBP 73.7bn; Walmart far larger.
  • What scale confers.
  • Procurement leverage: AI across 200,000 orders; e-auctions.
  • Lowest-cost compliance with fragmented packaging and health rules, which "favour larger manufacturers".
  • The ability to build capacity: USD 550m Batavia; CHF 520m Italy.
  • Emerging-market reach and quick-commerce partnerships, where Nestlé is "partner of choice".
  • Diversification, which lowers aggregate earnings volatility.
  • What it does not confer.
  • Relative growth: revenue CAGR -0.6% FY19–25 reported; organic lagged Danone and Unilever in 2024–26.
  • Margin leadership: 16.1% against Unilever 20%, Colgate 21%, Hershey 22%.
  • Agility: three CEOs in 13 months; a "bank that sells food".
  • Shelf-price control in Europe.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F479th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Nestlé's offerings are demonstrably not inferior in its profit core: petcare, coffee systems and soluble coffee in emerging markets, and medical nutrition. Recent share gains and positive RIG prove this. In the contested middle it has been demonstrably inferior on value for money over 2022–25, to private label and focused challengers:

  • instant coffee in developed markets;
  • creamers;
  • US frozen;
  • mainstream confectionery in Europe;
  • China infant formula.

The current turnaround consists of restoring utility there through price restraint, price-pack architecture and reformulation.

  • Disproving inferiority (positive evidence).
  • Coffee grew 9.3% (Q1-26) and 7.5% (H1-26), against category growth of about 4%. Nescafé grew double digit in Mexico, Brazil and India.
  • Purina took share from General Mills.
  • Wet cat brands (Fancy Feast, Felix) grew at mid-to-high single-digit RIG.
  • US Nielsen to May 2026: Nestlé volume +1.1% and value +3.5%, outperforming packaged-food averages.
  • KitKat RIG positive.

  • Evidence of inferiority (negative).

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D3642nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Nestlé has demonstrable pass-through power. It recovered cost inflation through price in 2022–25. It does not have demonstrable real pricing power. Each round of price rises cost volume and share (RIG -0.3% in FY23; Starbucks +8% price, -4% volume and mix), so utility eroded. It is now deliberately pricing below inflation to rebuild volume. Real pricing power exists in pockets (premium coffee, Pro Plan, medical nutrition) but is not being applied. That is the correct choice, and it means price should not be modelled as a margin lever.

  • Applied, with erosion.
  • 2022–23: about 8% pricing, then volume fell and private label gained.
  • FY25: 2.8% pricing on coffee, but gross margin still fell 110bp because pricing lagged cost.
  • Europe: retailers delisted rather than accept list-price rises (H1-26 Europe RIG 0.5%).
  • Wisdom Library: "Pricing power breaks before demand does."
  • Applied with little erosion.
  • Coffee in Q1-26: 5.7% pricing with RIG still 3.5%.
  • Nespresso and soluble coffee in emerging markets.
  • Pro Plan and therapeutics.
  • India: masala premiums.
  • Not applied now. Q3-26 pricing 1.1% (Europe 0.2%). "Price increases only where consumers can tolerate them" (Manz). As commodities deflate, retailers will demand price give-backs.
  • Contrast. Lindt raised prices about 56% cumulatively (2022–26) and kept margins, but then lost European volume and cut its RRP. Even the strongest premium confectioner hit a ceiling.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B5681st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Historically Nestlé kept its scale economies, as margin ambition (17.5–18.5%), shareholder returns and the CHF 20bn buyback. It priced to protect margin. The new strategy shares them de facto, though not as an explicit value. Savings are reinvested in marketing, product superiority and price restraint to rebuild volume. This is closer to "scale economies shared" than at any time in a decade, but it is tactical, framed as reinvestment rather than a Costco-style promise. It will likely reverse if margin targets come under pressure.

  • Evidence of sharing now.
  • Fuel for Growth savings are explicitly reinvested in A&P (8.9% of sales) and CHF 600m of growth investment.
  • Pricing is kept below inflation (Q3-26 1.1%); price-pack architecture keeps entry price points low.
  • "Volume share losses reduced by 60%".
  • CEO: "consumer-centric marketing actions are working".
  • Evidence against a culture of sharing.
  • The margin target remains 17%+.
  • The 2022–23 pricing was set to protect margins.
  • Retailer disputes show Nestlé resisting give-backs ("defending brand equity outweighs short-term retail volume compromises").
  • Promotional depth, not list-price cuts, is the chosen tool: targeted coupons, buy-three-get-one.
  • Wisdom Library: "Scale Economies Shared". A durable moat requires sharing as a reflex. At Nestlé it is a phase.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F4021st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Few gravitational forces work in Nestlé's favour. The ones that do are:

  • Emerging-market demographics and urbanisation. Rising incomes in AOA, Latin America and India, plus a channel shift to modern and digital trade where Nestlé is the partner of choice.
  • Pet humanisation and premiumisation.
  • Compliance complexity, which favours scale.

Several gravitational forces work against it:

  • retailer consolidation and own-label premiumisation;
  • developed-market demographic and GLP-1 volume erosion;
  • the Swiss franc's long-run appreciation;
  • falling births in infant nutrition.

Net, Nestlé's relative position does not improve automatically with time. The current improvement depends on "heroic" management action: cost cuts, reinvestment, portfolio pruning. That is less durable than gravity.

  • Pulling Nestlé forward.
  • Emerging markets are about 41% of sales and drove 71% of organic growth in 2010–19 (Redburn). AOA grew 6.4% in Q3-26; India's digital commerce is growing quickly.
  • Pet: 4–5% structural growth; wet cat capacity.
  • Medical nutrition: ageing.
  • Pulling it back.
  • Private label and discounters: 11.3% a year growth (2016–24).
  • CHF: Redburn models a structural -2% a year translation drag.
  • China births down 17% in 2025.
  • GLP-1: 0.7% a year volume drag on North American packaged food (DB).
  • Retail media taking A&P dollars.
  • Mechanical, company-specific.
  • Portfolio mix improves mechanically as low-margin units leave: Waters +30bp.
  • Capex falling below depreciation growth (capex 4.8% of sales) mechanically lifts FCF.
  • These are one-off steps, not compounding.

D4526th percentile

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D5531st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: The strategy is right but not bold. Its priorities are well chosen and consistent with what has worked for staples turnarounds (Unilever and Danone):

  • volume over price;
  • focus on four categories;
  • more and better-targeted A&P
  • shrinking the perimeter;
  • a cost programme funding reinvestment;
  • local P&L accountability.

It widens the moat where Nestlé is advantaged (pet, coffee, medical nutrition). It is not dynamic in capital terms. It does not use the balance sheet or the L'Oréal stake, does not address the dividend's absorption of cash, and does not exit the structurally challenged middle of the portfolio (US frozen, mainstream confectionery, China infant formula). It manages them instead.

  • Smart elements.
  • Growth platforms at 30% of sales with a defined funding pot (CHF 600m).
  • Marketing return discipline: 135 brands; non-working spend below 20%.
  • Capacity first in petcare.
  • A RIG gatekeeper in bonuses to lock incentives to the strategy.
  • Exiting Waters before EU packaging deposit rules arrive.
  • Wisdom Library: "Name the Marathon, Then Find the Moat". The marathon here is cost and volume execution.
  • Missing or timid elements.
  • L'Oréal, about CHF 42bn: "passive".
  • No buyback or capital-return ambition.
  • US frozen and Gerber "challenged" but kept.
  • China nutrition model "re-engineered" but not re-thought.
  • Morgan Stanley: dividend policy "severely limits its ability to reinvest in restoring top-line health".
  • Dynamic range.
  • Good in a disinflation and volume-recovery environment.
  • Weaker if commodities re-inflate in H2-27, because the strategy has just conceded price.
  • Weaker if the CHF jumps, because there are no buyback levers to defend EPS.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C+5568th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Governance structure is shareholder-friendly and prudent: one share, one vote; no controlling holder; a fully independent board; demanding executive shareholding; falling pay. Governance outcomes over the past decade have been less shareholder-friendly:

  • capital allocation built leverage to fund buybacks at higher prices and acquisitions that were later written down;
  • two CEO removals in 13 months showed oversight failure;
  • disclosure has gaps (the pension credit).

The current board under Isla looks designed to fix exactly these failures. The evidence supports "shareholders first" in design, with a mixed record in practice.

  • Structure.
  • All 13 directors independent; Lead Independent Director; 12-year terms; 72-year age limit.
  • 50% of director fees paid in shares blocked three years.
  • CEO must hold 5x salary; at least 50% of bonus in blocked shares.
  • PSU vesting on constant-currency EPS, relative TSR (against STOXX Global Food & Beverage), ROIC and GHG.
  • Clawback and malus.
  • No dual class, no poison pill. The 5% voting cap is the only defensive feature.
  • Pay. Executive board total CHF 77.0m (2024), CHF 50.7m (2025); proposed CHF 55m for 11 members in 2027. No open-market selling by insiders in the last 12 months.
  • Outcomes.
  • The CHF 20bn buyback (completed 2024), executed when Nestlé's forward P/E was mostly above today's 17.6x. The five-year range is 14.9–24.9x; this is my inference from the multiple history, as purchase prices are not in the files.
  • Leverage up from 0.8x to about 3x.
  • Write-downs: Bountiful CHF 1.3bn; Aimmune USD 2.1bn.
  • The Freixe conduct dismissal.
  • The Chair stepping down early after calls for his resignation.
  • The undisclosed pension credit.
  • The 66-year dividend record: shareholder-friendly, but rigid.
  • Why. The pre-2025 insider board (Bulcke, ex-CEO, as Chair) tolerated strategic drift. The outsider Chair and new committees are the corrective.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F2813th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Nestlé has not been an enlightened capital allocator over the past decade, and it is not a dynamic repurchaser now. Its allocation history:

  • Buybacks. It levered up to buy back about CHF 20bn of shares at multiples well above today's.
  • Acquisitions. It bought growth assets at peak valuations (Bountiful, Aimmune, Blue Bottle) that were written down.
  • L'Oréal. It held a large non-core stake passively.

Its genuine value-creating moves were joint-venture structures (Froneri, which paid a CHF 2bn extraordinary distribution; Cereal Partners; Galderma historically) and the 2018 US confectionery sale. The current team is allocating sensibly within tight limits: disposals of low-return units, capacity in high-return categories, debt reduction. It has neither the balance sheet nor, apparently, the inclination to be dynamic.

  • Repurchases. Shares fell from 2,880m (FY19) to 2,572m (FY24). FY24 buyback CHF 4.7bn; FY25 CHF 0.2bn; none since. Buybacks were paused to protect the dividend and de-lever.
  • Acquisitions. CHF 7.0bn (FY21), 2.3bn (FY22), 0.9bn (FY24), 1.3bn (FY25). Value outcomes poor (C-51).
  • Joint ventures. Froneri (the ice cream JV with PAI) paid a CHF 1.98bn distribution in 2025. Peranel follows the same template.
  • L'Oréal. About CHF 42bn, 20% held, "passive". It is the largest single source of under-used capital. Selling it would retire most of Nestlé's net debt.
  • My judgement on the sequence. At a P/E of about 17.6x and with leverage at 3x, the highest-return use of disposal cash is debt reduction now. Buybacks become sensible once leverage nears 2.5x (FY28 at the earliest), unless the L'Oréal stake is monetised earlier.
4c

Financial profile

D4845th percentile8 answers
Judgement48C-4927C-5040C-5162C-5248C-5356C-5430C-7640
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D4845th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nestlé's economic engine is solid but has stopped compounding:

  • Margins: high (gross 46%, operating about 16%).
  • Returns: adequate (ROIC about 12.5%).
  • Cash: strong (FCF CHF 9–10bn).

But six years of falling CHF EPS show the cost:

  • gross-margin erosion (49.6% in FY19 to 45.6% in FY25);
  • currency translation;
  • rising interest costs;
  • a recurring stream of restructuring and impairment charges;
  • buybacks halted by leverage.

The levers for the next 18 months to four years are now mostly operating ones:

  • coffee margin recovery (up to about 60–70bp of group margin);
  • net Fuel for Growth savings;
  • portfolio mix;
  • modest operating leverage from RIG of about 2%.

Together they support constant-currency EPS growth of about 5–7%. Financial leverage gives no further boost: no buybacks, net interest flat at best.

For the model:

  1. Build margin by segment, with coffee the swing line.
  2. Treat consensus's falling finance cost (about CHF 0.56bn over FY26–28) as unproven.
  3. Treat consensus revenue as perimeter-inconsistent.
  4. Model reported-to-underlying adjustments of CHF 1–2bn a year through 2027.
  5. Keep FCF at about 80–85% of underlying net income, with the dividend absorbing most of it.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F2722nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Nestlé is generating negative reported EPS growth. EPS has fallen for six years in Swiss francs. It is generating only low-single-digit constant-currency EPS growth on organic revenue growth of about 3.5%. Consensus expects about +2%, +4.5% and +6% in FY26–28. That would be the best three-year run since before 2020, but it is still modest. Its durability depends on margin recovery that is not yet visible.

  • Reported EPS history.
  • Brokers' basis: CHF 4.80 (FY23), 4.77 (FY24, -0.6%), 4.42 (FY25, -7.3%).
  • FactSet adjusted diluted: 5.10 (FY19) to 3.94 (FY25), about -4.2% a year. This happened despite shares falling 10.7% through buybacks.
  • Underlying EPS at constant currency: +3.3% (FY24) and +4% (H1-26). At actual rates H1-26 was -2.4% (CHF 2.22 against 2.27).
  • Revenue history.
  • Organic growth: 7.2% (FY23), 2.2% (FY24), 3.5% (FY25), about 3.7% (9M-26).
  • Reported growth: -1.5%, -1.8%, -2.0% and -2.5% (H1-26).
  • The gap between organic and reported growth was about 4–9pp a year, from currency and disposals.
  • The transmission problem.
  • Organic growth of 3–4% has not produced EPS growth because margin fell: EBIT margin went from 17.2% (FY24) to 15.9% (FY25, FactSet adjusted).
  • Currency removed about 5pp a year.
  • Buybacks have stopped, so share count no longer helps.
  • Consensus, like for like from 4.42. +2.1% (FY26, 4.51), +4.5% (FY27, 4.72), +6.3% (FY28, 5.02), +5.3% (FY29, 5.28; three brokers only).
  • Revenue growth is only +1.0%, +1.4% and +2.5%, so consensus EPS growth relies on margin and on lower finance costs, not revenue.
  • Durability. For FY27–28, constant-currency EPS growth of about 5–7% is achievable if margin recovers about 30–50bp a year. Reported CHF growth is likely 2–4 points lower if the franc keeps strengthening.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4023rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The scope to deploy capital at high returns is limited. High-return outlets exist:

  • capacity in petcare and coffee;
  • emerging-market distribution;
  • A&P behind growth platforms.

They absorb perhaps CHF 1–2bn a year above maintenance. The binding constraint is that about 87% of FCF already goes to the dividend and leverage is about 3x, so there is no surplus capital to compound. Nestlé is a distributor of cash, not a compounder. Its fair multiple is that of a mature, low-reinvestment staple: mid-to-high teens P/E, not the 20x-plus of 2019–21.

  • M&A. Constrained and with a poor return record: Bountiful written down; Aimmune USD 2.1bn impaired.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C6262nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns are adequate but mediocre for a branded staple, and falling.

  • Group ROIC: about 12.5% (FY25), comfortably above any reasonable cost of capital (about 6–7%, my estimate) but well below best-in-class peers.
  • Return on recent acquisitions: poor to negative.
  • Return on tangible capital: high, because the asset base is dominated by acquired goodwill and brands.

The returns that matter for the model (incremental returns on organic capex and A&P) are probably good in petcare and coffee and poor in the contested categories.

  • Group ROIC. 12.5% (FY25), down as operating profit fell (Business Foundations report).
  • Peers: Unilever 19.0% (top third of the sector; executive LTI target 18.5–19.5%); Hershey 23.0%; Danone 10.7% (FY25); Orkla ROCE 12.3%.

  • Bountiful: USD 5.75bn, then a CHF 1.3bn write-down plus a USD 1.0bn sale price for the mainstream part.
  • Aimmune: USD 2.1bn impairment. Blue Bottle: target missed, sold.
  • FY25 asset impairments of CHF 923m.
  • The 2019–22 acquisition cohort destroyed value.
  • Organic returns. Probably high on capacity-constrained petcare and coffee (sales responded immediately when capacity opened). Wisdom Library: "Capacity to Suffer Shows Up in the Accounts".
  • Tangible capital. Not quantified in the files. Returns are qualitatively high: brand-owner gross margins of 45%+ and an asset-light upstream, with no farms and outsourced commodity origination.
  • Model implication. Hold ROIC at about 12.5–13.5% through FY28. Margin recovery and divestments lift it slowly; no acquisitions dilute it.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

D4847th percentile

Judgement: Cash conversion is good on reported net income (about 100%) but only moderate on underlying net income (about 80–87%). It is flattered by a large supply-chain finance programme.   • Conversion.
  • Company FCF: CHF 10.67bn (FY24), 9.15bn (FY25; guidance was "at least 8.0bn"); H1-26 CHF 3.38bn, +46%; FY26 guide above CHF 9bn.
  • Against underlying net income (brokers'-basis EPS times about 2,572m shares): about 87% (FY24) and about 80% (FY25).
  • Against reported net income (CHF 9.03bn in FY25): about 101%.
  • Consensus implies about 81–84% of underlying net income in FY26–28.
  • Trap: the peer table's 0.2–0.4x history is after dividends (see the reader's note); the consensus 0.9x is before.
  • Working capital.
  • Average working capital fell to 1.3% of sales (2024) from 3.2% (2023).
  • Reverse factoring expanded CHF 1.5bn to CHF 6.21bn (6.8% of sales, over 37% of payables).
  • FactSet year-end net working capital rose from CHF 0.9bn (FY24) to CHF 5.5bn (FY25), about 6% of sales. Causes: payables settled (MS cites a CHF 2.7bn outflow) and expensive coffee inventory (a CHF 605m inventory outflow).
  • At June 2026 it was CHF 4.7bn (5.3% of LTM sales) against CHF 5.9bn a year earlier: improving.
  • Distributor stock compression (India 12–15 down to 7–10 days) and standardised working-capital KPIs help further.
  • Drags.
  • Restructuring cash of about CHF 2.0bn for the 16,000 roles; restructuring and litigation charges of CHF 1.7bn in FY25.
  • Lease payments.
  • Capex is now near a floor (below 5% of sales).
  • Risk. If banks withdraw reverse factoring, payables would contract by billions in one go.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

C5660th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Margins are high in absolute terms (gross about 46%, underlying operating about 16%) but have fallen materially since 2019. They appear to be bottoming, but the bottom is not yet confirmed. The gross-margin loss since 2019 is the root of Nestlé's earnings stagnation. About half looks cyclical (coffee and cocoa) and about half structural (mix, private label, pricing lag, reinvestment). Consensus expects slow recovery. Management's 17%+ is above consensus.

  • Gross margin. 49.6% (FY19), 47.8% (FY21), 45.2% (FY22), 45.9% (FY23), 46.7% (FY24), 45.6% (FY25), 46.4% (H1-26, -20bp year on year).

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D3039th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings, do one-offs or capitalised costs flatter it, and does tax or other leakage erode EPS?

Judgement: The earnings base is not clean, and the gap between adjusted and reported EPS has widened.

  • Brokers'-basis EPS exceeded reported basic EPS by about 13% in FY23 and FY24 and by 26% in FY25 (CHF 4.42 against 3.51).
  • H1-26 reported net income was 39% below underlying (CHF 3.5bn against 5.7bn).
  • Recurring "non-recurring" charges (restructuring, impairment, litigation) have been CHF 1–2bn a year.
  • A non-operational pension credit flattered the H1-26 margin.

On the other side: development costs are expensed, so there is no capitalisation flattery.

  • Items adjusted out.
  • Restructuring and other trading items: CHF 1.1bn (FY24), 1.7bn (FY25), about CHF 800m (H1-26).
  • Impairments: CHF 923m (FY25) and CHF 1.3bn (H1-26, VMS and ice cream).
  • Recall: CHF 75m of operating profit and CHF 110m of inventory write-offs (FY25).
  • These will continue through 2027 (about CHF 2bn of restructuring cash). A possible Russia write-off of up to CHF 1bn.
  • Items inside "underlying" that flatter.
  • The 30bp H1-26 pension credit (about CHF 130m), not communicated; ex-credit H1 margin would have missed consensus (MS, Redburn).
  • Adjusted net income includes the 20% equity share of L'Oréal's earnings, but only dividends are received in cash (MS).
  • Consensus inconsistency. The FactSet model's consensus net income (CHF 10.29bn for FY26) is about CHF 1.3bn below EPS times shares (4.51 × 2,572m = CHF 11.6bn). This suggests brokers differ on whether associate income is included. Check before building net income.
  • Tax. Reported rate 21.0% (FY19), 24.2% (FY20), 20.9% (FY21), 24.2% (FY22), 18.2% (FY23), 25.0% (FY24), 24.6% (FY25). Volatile and rising toward 24–25%.
  • Interest. Net interest CHF 1.0bn (FY19) rising to CHF 1.69–1.70bn (FY24–25), about 12% of EBIT. A rising leak, now stabilising.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F4016th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Financial leverage is high for Nestlé's history and its peer group: about 2.85–3.2x net debt to EBITDA, up from 0.8x before 2017. It is comfortable for solvency: interest cover is about 8.5x, 80% of debt is fixed and the average cost is about 2.6%. It amplifies EPS only modestly: a 10% EBIT change moves pre-tax profit by about 11–12%. Its real cost is strategic and in valuation:

  • it rules out buybacks;
  • it squeezes the dividend's room to grow;

  • it makes Nestlé the "leveraged defensive" in a rates-led de-rating.

  • Levels.

  • Company net debt: CHF 51.4bn (FY25, 2.85x); CHF 56.3bn (June 2026; about 3.1x per MS).
  • FactSet, including leases: CHF 56.7bn and 3.22x.
  • Target range 2.0–3.0x; management aims for the middle.
  • Peers: Danone 1.7x, Orkla 2.2x, Unilever 2.5x (falling to 1.3x after Foods), Tesco 2.0x.
  • Cost.
  • Net interest CHF 1.69bn (FY25), about 12% of EBIT.
  • +100bp on rates adds only about CHF 17m a year, because so much of the debt is fixed.
  • Refinancing over time at higher EUR and USD coupons is a slow leak. CHF funding stays cheap (SNB at 0%).
  • Consensus below the line.
  • The gap between EBIT and pre-tax profit narrows from about CHF 1.56bn (FY26) to CHF 1.32bn (FY27) and CHF 1.00bn (FY28), a swing of about CHF 0.56bn, worth about 4% of pre-tax profit.
  • This is a material part of consensus EPS growth. It is hard to reconcile with about CHF 4bn of disposal proceeds and CHF 1–2bn a year of retained FCF, which save only about CHF 0.15–0.2bn of interest, plus the loss of Russian interest income.
  • It probably reflects associate income or definitional differences.
  • This is a specific item for the second set to test.
  • De-leveraging path. Peranel CHF 2.8bn (-0.1x), VMS about USD 1.0bn and ice cream proceeds reduce leverage to about 2.5x by FY28 only if EBITDA grows as consensus assumes. Consensus total debt: CHF 56.3bn (FY26) to CHF 47.0bn (FY28).
