Economic leverage

5

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

F4022nd percentile

Memo topic 5, Pricing power, leverage, cost control, feedback loops: the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1733C-4536P-0442
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?F338th percentilefull answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D3642nd percentilefull answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D4246th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Price will contribute little to revenue and nothing to margin over the cycle. At Nestlé, pricing is a cost pass-through mechanism with a lag of 6 to 12 months, set by the length of its coffee and cocoa hedges (Morgan Stanley: Nestlé's cover runs 9 to 12 months, private-label suppliers' 3 to 6). Its margin effect is a timing effect:

  • positive when input costs fall faster than retailers can claw back price, as in H2-26 to H2-27;
  • negative when costs rise before price can follow, as in 2025 (gross margin -110bp) and probably 2028.

My base case for pricing:

  • FY26: about 1.7% (H1 2.1%, Q3 1.1%).
  • FY27: about 1.0% to 1.2%.
  • H1 at about 0.7%. Europe goes slightly negative, at about -0.5% to -1.0%, as retailers and short-hedged private label force coffee and chocolate give-backs. North America runs at about +0.5%, emerging markets at about +3% (price rises announced for Q4-26).
  • H2 at about 1.3%.
  • FY28 to FY30: about 1.5% a year, as commodity re-inflation is passed through and emerging-market inflation persists.

That adds CHF 0.9bn to 1.3bn of revenue a year, about a third of organic growth, against about two-thirds in 2022 to 2025.

On margin, the price-cost spread adds about +30bp to +40bp net in FY27 (I assume Nestlé keeps about half of the coffee and cocoa relief) and costs about -15bp in FY28. Over the cycle it is roughly zero. The forecast should therefore carry no structural price-led margin gain. Management's 4%+ organic target, if it implies about 2% pricing, is the part of the medium-term framework least supported by the evidence.

Evidence that pricing power is pass-through, not real.

  • 2022–2023: about 8% pricing protected margin only partly (UTOP 17.1% to 17.3%), and volume fell to -0.3% RIG.
  • 2025: 2.8% pricing on coffee still left gross margin down 110bp. The price lagged the cost.
  • Peers repeat the pattern:
  • Mondelez took nearly 30% chocolate price in Europe and lost 7.4% of volume.
  • Lindt took 19%, lost 6.6% volume, then cut guidance and is cutting price in Germany and Switzerland.
  • JDE Peet's booked 19.5% pricing in 2025, then passed lower coffee costs through and saw sales fall in some regions.
  • Keurig Dr Pepper's US coffee price fell 4.2% in Q3-26.
  • Westrock Coffee's sales fell 15.4% on pass-through of lower green coffee.

The sector pattern is that coffee and cocoa pass through in both directions within about a year.

Where real pricing power exists, by category.

Category Pricing outlook Evidence
Emerging-market soluble coffee Real power; continued positive price Low elasticity; shoppers traded down into Nescafé soluble in 2024–25; EM pricing 3.3% in 9M-26 with RIG 2.7%
Nespresso (direct to consumer) Moderate power; low single-digit price Margin fell only 360bp in 2025 against 540bp for Nescafé and Starbucks at-home; pricing 1.4% in 2026
Starbucks at-home, US creamers Weak; volume elastic +8% price brought -4% volume and mix (Jefferies); Chobani took creamers share from 4% to 12%
Petcare (US) Flat pricing; mix-led gains US pricing flat after double-digit rises; 62% share of premium wet cat; dog-food shopper incidence -4% (Morgan Stanley)
Medical nutrition Moderate power outside China tenders Prescriber-led; China volume-based procurement cuts contract values 20% to 30%
Infant formula Weak near term Recall cohort lost; formula sales about 10% below pre-recall levels; China online -22%
Confectionery, Europe Negative in H1-27 Lindt's German RRP cut from €2.69 to €2.19; Orkla Snacks -1.5%; Morgan Stanley (12 November 2026): short-hedged private label will cut chocolate prices
Mainstream food (US frozen, Maggi in mature markets) Weak; promotion-led "Deeper 52-week promotional calendars"; Kraft Heinz volume and mix -2.6%

Europe is the battleground of 2027. It is about 20% of sales. Pricing was 0.2% in Q3-26 against consensus of 1.3%. Intermarché delisted about 30 brands in early 2026, resolved by Q3. The Edeka–Mars dispute lasted about 18 months, and only half of the 400 products returned. Cross-border alliances (Concordis, Eurelec) now coordinate delistings. Coffee and chocolate are where commodity relief is largest and therefore where give-back demands will be loudest, during the H1-27 negotiation round. I assume about half the relief is given back. That is between the JDE Peet's model (most of it given back, quickly) and Nestlé's own 2015 experience (a CHF 300m commodity tailwind delayed by inventory and hedging, then mostly retained).

How price connects to the other dynamics. The pricing rule of thumb that matters for the model: a fall in commodity prices reaches Nestlé's margin about two to three quarters later, and a rise reaches it with the same lag. With coffee forecast to fall about 20% in 2026 and about 11% in 2027 (Jefferies, November 2026) and inflation returning from H2-27 (Morgan Stanley), the gross-margin window is H2-26 to about Q3-27. FY27 is the best year of the cycle for the price-cost spread and FY28 gives some of it back. The pricing that lifts organic growth in FY28 (about 1.5%) does not lift margin. It pays for the higher costs.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7547P-0144
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4739th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage is modest, typical of a diversified staple.

  • Volume. Each 1pp of volume (RIG) moves EBIT by about 2–2.5% (about CHF 0.3–0.35bn).
  • Margin. Each 50bp of margin moves EPS by about 3%.
  • Price. 1pp of net price with costs unchanged would move EBIT by about 6%, but price is rarely "costless".

The 18-month cone of operating outcomes is therefore narrow on volume. It is wider on the commodity, price and give-back balance. It is widest on currency, which is not operating leverage at all. Most of the range in reported EPS comes from the Swiss franc and from margin mix, not from volume.

  • Cost structure (my estimates from disclosed lines).
  • COGS about 54% of sales, of which raw and packaging materials about 33% (variable) and conversion about 21% (partly fixed).
  • Distribution about 8.4% (mostly variable).
  • A&P about 8.6–8.9% (discretionary).
  • Administration, R&D and other about 12–13% (largely fixed).
  • Fixed and semi-fixed costs total about 25–30% of sales. The contribution margin on an incremental unit is about 35–40% before any A&P scaling.
  • Disclosed sensitivities (Change Forces report).
  • Revenue ±1pp: EPS ±CHF 0.05 (±1.0%).
  • Volume ±2pp: EPS ±CHF 0.16 (±3.6%), that is, about 1.8% per point.
  • EBIT margin ±50bp: EPS ±CHF 0.14 (±3.1%).
  • Currency ±5%: EPS ±CHF 0.18 (±4.0%).
  • Translation margin effect: a 5% CHF move costs 10–15bp of margin.
  • Precedent. Volume losses in FY23 (RIG -0.3%) and FY24–25 (0.8%) coincided with margin falling only about 110bp in FY25. Most of that fall came from commodity cost, not deleverage. Operating deleverage from volume is not Nestlé's main risk.
  • Cone for 18 months (illustrative). RIG 1–3% gives EBIT about ±2–3% around the base. Net commodity and pricing ±50–100bp gives EBIT ±3–6%. Currency ±5% gives EPS ±4%.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F4421st percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Nestlé carries real fixed-cost operating leverage, but management is deliberately spending most of it. The gross profit from each extra unit of volume is largely recycled into A&P and growth platforms rather than dropped to profit. Over the next four years, volume growth of about 2.2% to 2.4% a year will therefore add only about 10bp to 15bp a year to the margin. That is far less than the arithmetic of a 35% to 40% contribution margin implies.

The history confirms that Nestlé's margin follows the price-cost spread, not volume:

  • 2021: volume growth (RIG) of 5.5% came with a 30bp fall in margin.
  • 2017: volume growth with only 0.3% pricing came with a margin rise, because savings and low inflation coincided.
  • 2025: volume growth of 0.8% came with a 110bp fall, from the coffee and cocoa spike.

Operating leverage will therefore shape the line items far less than three other things: the commodity cycle, the perimeter changes, and a new piece of reverse leverage from the shrinking perimeter. That reverse leverage is stranded central costs, which hit as transitional service agreements expire in 2028. The cone of 18-month outcomes from volume alone is narrow: RIG of 1% to 3% moves EBIT by about ±1.5%, or ±10bp of margin.

The cost structure and what is actually fixed. These are estimates from disclosed lines, as no fixed and variable split is disclosed (AlphaSense, 17 November 2026: "analysts cannot precisely model margin expansion from volume recovery").

Cost line Share of sales Behaviour
Raw and packaging materials About 33% Variable
Factory conversion About 21% Roughly half fixed
Distribution 8.4% (H1-26, up from 8.2% in FY25) Mostly variable, with fixed warehousing
A&P 8.9% (H1-26) Set as a ratio to sales and rising by policy, so it behaves as variable
Administration and other About 20% (20.2% in H1-26, flattered 30bp by the pension credit) Largely fixed
R&D 1.8% Largely fixed

Truly fixed costs are about 25% to 30% of sales. Once A&P is treated as a variable cost (management holds it at a ratio and is raising it), the incremental margin on a unit of volume is about 22% to 27%, not 35% to 40%. Each 1pp of RIG adds about CHF 0.9bn of sales and about CHF 0.2bn to 0.25bn of UTOP: about +1.5% of EBIT and +10bp of margin, before cost inflation on the fixed base. The first set's figure of 2% to 2.5% of EBIT per point (C-75) is the ceiling, reached only if A&P does not rise with sales.

Why operating leverage will be weaker than the volume recovery suggests.

  1. Fixed costs inflate. At about 2.5% to 3% a year on a fixed base of about CHF 23bn to 27bn, inflation adds CHF 0.6bn to 0.8bn of cost a year, roughly the same as the extra gross profit from 2.3% RIG. Volume growth only "absorbs" fixed costs if fixed costs are held flat in money terms. That is what Fuel for Growth is for, so operating leverage and the savings programme are the same lever counted once, not twice (P-03).
  2. A&P is rising by policy. It went from 8.1% to 8.6% (FY25) and 8.9% (H1-26), and is heading for over 9%. Jefferies estimates A&P on the growth platforms could reach 14% of their sales. That spends about 20bp to 30bp of margin over FY26 to FY28.
  3. The perimeter shrinks faster than central costs. About CHF 6bn of sales (Waters, mainstream vitamins, ice cream) and CHF 2bn (Russia) leave over 2026 to 2027. Central costs allocated to them stay unless cut. Unilever's precedent is gross stranded costs of €400m to €500m on its ice cream separation, and €250m a year after Spreads, which took three years to remove. On a similar ratio, Nestlé faces gross stranded costs of about CHF 0.15bn to 0.3bn (20bp to 35bp). Transitional service income will offset part of this until about 2028. I carry a net -5bp to -10bp in FY27 to FY28.
  4. Depreciation creeps up. Capex (about 4.8% to 5.0% of sales including intangibles) exceeds D&A (about 4.0%) by about CHF 0.8bn to 0.9bn a year, so D&A on the continuing perimeter rises about 2% to 3% a year (Jefferies: depreciation about 70bp of sales below capex). Morgan Stanley's "362bp headwind if fully normalised" (June 2024) is not a credible modelling input: it would require capex to be expensed at once. The real effect is about -3bp to -5bp a year on margin, which I include.

How it shapes the line items, against the last four to six years.

Line FY19–FY25 FY26–FY30 base case Why
Gross margin 49.6% (FY19), 45.2% (FY22), 46.7% (FY24), 45.6% (FY25) 46.6% (FY26), 47.3% (FY27), 47.2% (FY28), 47.4% (FY29), 47.5% (FY30) Coffee and cocoa relief through 2027, mix from the exits, partial relapse in 2028. It stays 2pp below FY19 because about half the loss since 2019 is structural (mix, private label, price investment)
A&P / sales 8.1% to 8.6% About 9.0% to 9.2% Policy
Distribution / sales 8.2% to 8.4% About 8.2% Automation (Arvin) against freight
UTOP margin 17.3% (FY23), 17.2% (FY24), 16.1% (FY25) 16.35%, 16.6%, 16.8%, 17.0%, 17.1% About +20bp a year, below the +40bp a year of 2016–2020
D&A CHF 3.4bn to 3.7bn CHF 3.58bn to 3.72bn (down in FY27–FY28 as Waters and Russia leave, then rising) Capex above D&A

The 18-month cone attributable to operating leverage. It is the narrowest of the cones in this case.

Driver Range EBIT effect Margin effect
RIG over H2-26 to H2-27 1.5% to 3.0% About -1.3% to +1.0% around my base ±10bp
Commodity price and give-back balance ±50bp to 100bp of gross margin ±3% to 6% —
Currency ±5% — About ±4% on EPS

The Consumer should therefore treat volume as the most predictable driver and the price-cost spread and the franc as the wide ones.

Peer evidence.

  • Danone improved its operations by 77bp in 2025 but reinvested 59bp in A&P and product superiority, netting +18bp before other items. Its CFO says, "expanding manufacturing volumes is the best way to get leverage". Specialized Nutrition widened its margin on 2.7% volume growth.
  • Orkla discloses a contribution ratio of 39.8% (Q3-26). It shows how much of a branded food cost base is variable, and why its margin follows volume.
  • Lindt expanded its margin through a 6.6% volume fall with 19% price. That is a premium route Nestlé's mass-market mix cannot copy.
  • Nestlé India absorbed fixed costs on strong local volume (employee costs at 9.4% of sales). It is the emerging-market proof that Nestlé's leverage works where volume is strong and price is not contested.
5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7640P-0235
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F4016th percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F358th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Financial leverage will not amplify EPS growth over the forecast period. It will hold EPS back, in three ways:

  1. Net finance costs stay at about CHF 1.4bn to 1.5bn a year. Consensus assumes a fall to about CHF 1.0bn by FY28. That assumption is worth about CHF 0.12 to 0.14 of FY28 EPS (about 2.5% to 3%), and I do not find it supported.
  2. The share count is flat. It adds nothing to EPS growth for the first time in a decade; over FY19 to FY24 it added about 2% a year.
  3. The balance sheet de-levers in one step and then stalls. Disposal cash takes net debt to about CHF 46bn and leverage to about 2.5x by end-FY27. After that, free cash flow after leases (about CHF 8.8bn to 9.7bn) roughly equals the dividend (CHF 8.1bn to 8.5bn), so leverage falls only as EBITDA grows: to about 2.4x by FY30.

The leverage is comfortable for solvency (interest cover about 10x UTOP; about 80% of debt fixed). It is uncomfortable for capital returns. Buybacks can restart only in token size (I assume CHF 0.5bn in FY29 and CHF 1.0bn in FY30, adding 0.1% to 0.4% to EPS), unless the L'Oréal stake is monetised. This is the single most important balance-sheet conclusion for the Consumer: management's "middle of the 2.0x to 3.0x range" is reachable in FY27, but reaching it does not release cash. The dividend absorbs the cash that deleveraging would otherwise release.

The net debt path (company basis, approximately; leverage on UTOP plus D&A).

End of Net debt Leverage What moves it
FY25 (actual) CHF 51.4bn 2.85x —
June 2026 (actual) CHF 56.3bn About 3.1x The April dividend
FY26 About CHF 51.4bn About 2.8x H2 FCF after leases of about CHF 6.0bn, offset by CHF 0.9bn of Russian cash leaving the group
FY27 About CHF 46.3bn About 2.5x FCF after leases of CHF 8.8bn, less the CHF 8.1bn dividend, plus about CHF 4.3bn of net disposal cash (Peranel CHF 2.8bn, mainstream vitamins about CHF 0.8bn, ice cream about CHF 1.0bn, less bolt-ons)
FY28 About CHF 45.8bn About 2.5x —
FY29 About CHF 45.9bn About 2.4x Includes the CHF 0.5bn buyback
FY30 About CHF 46.1bn About 2.4x Includes the CHF 1.0bn buyback

FactSet's consensus total debt (gross, including leases) runs from CHF 56.3bn in FY26 to CHF 47.0bn in FY28. That is consistent with my path in direction. The difference is that consensus reaches CHF 47bn with higher FCF and still funds no buyback.

Why net finance costs will not fall as consensus assumes.

  • The base. Net financial expense was CHF 688m in H1-26 (CHF 759m in H1-25). FactSet's FY25 figure is CHF 1,686m, which includes currency transaction losses of about CHF 250m. I take FY26 at about CHF 1.47bn.
  • Savings. Lower average net debt (about -CHF 4.7bn in FY27 and -CHF 2.8bn in FY28) saves about CHF 0.12bn and 0.07bn at a 2.5% to 2.6% average cost.
  • Costs. About 46% of debt refinances over five years at a weighted new coupon of about 4.0%, against an effective 2.3% (Rothschild & Co Redburn). That adds about CHF 0.09bn a year. Russian interest income (CHF 0.9bn of cash at double-digit local rates) is lost: about CHF 0.08bn more in FY27 than in FY26. Morgan Stanley (8 October 2026) flags CHF 16.3bn of short-term debt maturing within 12 months, to be replaced at higher cost.
  • Net path. About CHF 1.47bn (FY26), 1.45bn (FY27), 1.40bn (FY28), 1.40bn (FY29, with a small buyback) and 1.42bn (FY30).
  • Against consensus. Consensus pre-tax profit implies CHF 1.56bn, 1.32bn and 1.00bn for FY26 to FY28. Consensus pre-tax profit excludes associates, so the narrowing is a genuine assumption of lower costs. The CFO said in October 2026 that the Russian deconsolidation will not materially change how analysts should forecast interest. That supports a flat line, not a falling one.

How it shapes EPS.

  • Sensitivities. A 10% change in UTOP moves pre-tax profit (before associates) by about 11%. Associates dilute that to about 9.5% on EPS. A 100bp rise in rates costs only about CHF 17m a year on the floating portion (company disclosure), so rate sensitivity is negligible inside 18 months. The slow repricing of maturing debt is the real cost, at about -0.6% of EPS a year, offset by deleveraging in FY27.
  • Against the past. Net interest rose from CHF 0.8bn (FY20–21) to CHF 1.7bn (FY24–25), costing about CHF 0.30 a share. Buybacks cut the share count from 2,880m to 2,572m (-10.7%), adding about CHF 0.50 a share. Over the next four years both levers are close to neutral. EPS growth must come from operating profit and associates alone.

What would change this.

  • L'Oréal. The stake is worth about CHF 42bn, about 19% of Nestlé's market value, and management treats it as passive. How the proceeds of any sale are used decides whether it helps EPS:
  • Into buybacks: selling CHF 10bn of L'Oréal at L'Oréal's own earnings multiple (roughly 30 times, my assumption; not in the files) gives up about CHF 0.33bn of associate income. Buying back CHF 10bn of Nestlé at 17.6 times earnings adds about CHF 0.57bn, so EPS rises about +2%.
  • Into debt repayment: repaying debt at about 2.5% to 4% pre-tax saves less than the associate income lost, so EPS falls. Morgan Stanley estimates -5.5% to adjusted net income for a full sell-down.
  • Thresholds: keeping the stake above 15% preserves equity accounting (Rothschild & Co Redburn). Jefferies sizes a 5% stake sale at CHF 6bn to 10bn.
  • Signals: the board has given none.
  • Reverse factoring. CHF 6.21bn (about 7% of sales; Unilever about 3.5%) is quasi-debt. Counting it adds about 0.35x to leverage. If banks withdrew it, the multi-billion cash outflow would push leverage back toward 2.9x to 3.0x and end any buyback discussion.
  • Ratings. Rating-agency comfort at about 2.5x to 3.0x is not discussed in the files. That is a gap worth closing (research requests).
5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7768P-0360
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

B+6887th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: A meaningful but finite part of the cost base is cuttable. Fuel for Growth's CHF 3.0bn gross (about 3.4% of sales) is credible and ahead of schedule. A further, smaller post-2027 wave from procurement AI, shared services and automation is plausible.

  • Most cuttable: indirect procurement, administration and overhead, and factory footprint.
  • Partly cuttable: distribution.
  • Not cuttable, by strategy: A&P, which is being raised.
  • Least controllable: raw materials (about 33% of sales), set by commodity markets.

The question for the model is not whether the cuts happen but how much survives reinvestment. Jefferies estimates about CHF 2bn net.

  • Programme composition.
  • About three-quarters (other sources say half) from procurement and spend consolidation: about CHF 65bn of annual spend; e-auctions; vendor counts cut (7,900 to 8 distributors in some categories).
  • White-collar: 12,000 roles, CHF 1.0bn a year by end-2027; 20% already delivered.
  • 4,000 factory roles through automation.
  • Shared services expanded threefold; nine end-to-end processes standardised.
  • Brands supported cut from over 400 to 135–150, with non-working marketing below 20%.
  • Delivery so far. CHF 1.1bn (FY25, against CHF 700m planned); CHF 1.7bn cumulative (H1-26, about 150bp of gross-margin support per DB); CHF 2.0bn targeted for FY26.
  • Costs to achieve. About CHF 2.0bn of cash; FY25 restructuring of CHF 1.7bn (including litigation).
  • Peer precedent. Unilever EUR 800m of productivity delivered (EUR 750m by Q1-26). Danone about EUR 1bn reinvested since 2022. Tesco "Save to Invest" CHF-equivalent of about GBP 500m a year, over-delivered. Large staples programmes typically hit about 85–95% of target; Nestlé is running above 100%.
  • What is left after 2027.
  • Navratil: "no ceiling".
  • Jefferies notes Nestlé's staff cost per head is already lower than peers, which limits further people savings.
  • Distribution (8.4% of sales) is a target for automation (Arvin).
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

B+6084th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Fuel for Growth will deliver its CHF 3.0bn of gross savings by end-2027, and probably more: delivery runs at 157% of the FY25 plan and the 2016–2020 programme delivered 155% of its first target. Only about a quarter of the gross will be visible as margin. I estimate about CHF 0.7bn, or about +80bp of UTOP margin, by FY28 relative to FY25. It will arrive in three steps: about +25bp in FY26, +30bp in FY27 and +25bp in FY28, the last including the start of a post-2027 wave.

The rest of the CHF 3.0bn is spent, not lost:

  • about CHF 1.2bn to 1.5bn offsets ordinary inflation on wages, energy, freight and overheads;
  • about CHF 0.3bn to 0.5bn funds the rise in A&P
  • about CHF 0.3bn to 0.5bn funds price investment and the CHF 600m of growth-platform spending.

The most important timing fact is a coincidence at the end of 2027. The programme's last tranche lands just as commodity relief ends (Morgan Stanley expects commodity inflation back in the P&L from H2-27) and as stranded costs from the exits surface. FY28 is the first year in which savings must fund reinvestment without help from falling coffee and cocoa costs. The first clean margin test is H1-27 (results late July 2027), when savings, commodity relief and the absence of the pension credit coincide.

What has been promised and delivered.

Item Detail
Gross savings CHF 1.1bn in FY25 (against CHF 0.7bn planned); CHF 1.7bn cumulative at H1-26 (CHF 0.6bn in H1-26); CHF 2.0bn cumulative targeted for FY26; CHF 3.0bn by end-2027
Composition Procurement and spend consolidation: about half to three-quarters (about CHF 65bn of annual spend; e-auctions; AI across 200,000 purchase orders). White-collar: 12,000 roles for CHF 1.0bn a year by end-2027, 20% delivered in 2025. Factory: 4,000 roles through automation
Cost to achieve About CHF 2.0bn of cash for the white-collar programme. Reported restructuring and net other trading items: CHF 1.7bn (FY25, including litigation) and CHF 0.8bn (H1-26)
My cash phasing (restructuring) About CHF 1.0bn (FY26), 0.8bn (FY27), 0.4bn (FY28), then about 0.3bn a year of recurring restructuring. These sit below UTOP, so they reduce reported EPS and FCF but not underlying EPS

Why only about a quarter reaches the margin. Three independent lines of evidence point the same way.

  1. Arithmetic. Nestlé's non-commodity cost base is about CHF 30bn (conversion, distribution, administration, R&D). At 2.5% to 3% inflation it rises by about CHF 0.75bn to 0.9bn a year. Pricing of about 1% to 1.5% a year (P-04) recovers about CHF 0.9bn to 1.3bn of revenue, but in 2027 part of that price is handed back on coffee and confectionery in Europe. Gross savings of about CHF 1.0bn a year are therefore roughly matched by inflation plus deliberate reinvestment. The visible margin gain is the remainder.
  2. Peer precedents. - Danone: 77bp of operational improvement in 2025 against 59bp reinvested, so about a quarter retained. - JDE Peet's: reinvests 50% of its €500m programme. - Unilever: delivered €670m of its €800m by end-2025. Overheads improved 50bp, but the savings were used to offset ice cream stranded costs. - Kraft Heinz is the reverse case: deep cuts without reinvestment, then a USD 600m to 700m reinvestment in 2026 to stop share loss. - Bernstein (February 2025): Nestlé needed more than CHF 1.0bn of net savings just to hold a 16% margin under that year's cost pressure.
  3. Nestlé's own 2016–2020 programme. It delivered CHF 2.8bn of savings against CHF 1.8bn promised. The margin rose 160bp to 17.7% over 2016 to 2020, about half the gross savings (3.1% of sales). That happened with inflation near 1% and A&P not rising. Today inflation is higher and A&P is rising, so a drop-through of about a quarter to a third is the right adjustment.

What reaches each line, and when.

Line FY26 FY27 FY28 FY29–FY30
UTOP margin from net savings About +25bp About +30bp About +25bp (includes the post-2027 wave and automation) About +15bp to 20bp a year ("no ceiling"; Arvin; shared services)
Restructuring cash below UTOP About CHF -1.0bn About -0.8bn About -0.4bn About -0.3bn
FCF effect, net of restructuring cash Neutral Positive about CHF 0.4bn Positive about CHF 0.8bn Positive

What could put more on the line. Staff cost per head is already below peers' (Jefferies), which limits further people savings. But three larger pools are untouched or early: distribution at 8.4% of sales (automation), the 400-to-150 brand tail (complexity cost), and a structural review of the "contested middle" (US frozen, mainstream confectionery). If management announced a second programme at FY26 results (18 February 2027), it would be the main upside to my FY28 to FY30 margins.

What could take it away. Organisational stress from cutting about 6% of the workforce in two years. Nestlé's prospectus of 29 May 2026 warns of "distraction for managers and employees", and expert calls describe talent leaving for Danone. Volume momentum (Q3-26 RIG of 2.8%) shows no execution drag yet. The risk is concentrated in 2027, when most of the white-collar cuts land.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4740P-0550
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F4021st percentilefull answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D5044th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Two reinforcing loops are running and will dominate revenue over the next 18 months:

  • The volume-reinvestment loop. Lower pricing and higher A&P bring more volume, and more volume funds more A&P.
  • The petcare capacity loop. New capacity brings share gains, which bring scale.

Both are real and both are young. Three balancing loops will dominate margin and reported earnings:

  • The retailer give-back loop. It claims part of the commodity relief in 2027.
  • The currency loop. It cuts reported earnings every year.
  • The balance-sheet loop. The dividend absorbs free cash flow, so there are no buybacks and the multiple has less support.

The most important insight is how the loops interact over time. In FY27, part of the volume loop's fuel is commodity relief, which is temporary, and the savings programme, which ends in 2027. In FY28 that fuel shrinks at the same moment that commodity costs turn up and stranded costs surface. FY28 is therefore the year the volume loop must run on savings alone. It is also when the balance-sheet loop starts to bind: with the dividend absorbing almost all free cash flow, any shock forces a choice between A&P and the dividend. If that choice falls on A&P, the reinforcing volume loop turns vicious, as at Kraft Heinz after 2017. My base case has the volume loop surviving at reduced strength (RIG about 2.2% in FY28 to FY30, margin +15bp to 20bp a year). The cone is widest in FY28, not FY27.

The loops one by one: mechanism, current evidence, strength, and what would flip it.

  1. Volume-reinvestment loop (reinforcing; revenue and later margin). - Mechanism. Pricing below inflation and A&P at 8.9% or more lift RIG. Higher RIG absorbs fixed costs, which funds further A&P and keeps the RIG bonus gatekeeper satisfied. - Evidence.
    • RIG rose 1.2%, 1.8% and 2.8% across Q1 to Q3 of 2026, positive in all zones in Q3.
    • The rate of value share loss is down about 60%, and billionaire brands are share-neutral.
    • US Nielsen volume was +1.1% to May 2026.
    • Strength. Moderate. About 0.2pp to 0.3pp of Q3's 2.8% came from the Waters heatwave and confectionery phasing.
    • Warning signs already present. Bernstein's Western European scanner (30 October 2026) showed Nestlé value -0.3% with volume slowing to +1.2%, against Danone +2.7%. North American confectionery volume fell 11% in Q3-26 (Morgan Stanley).
    • Peer precedents. Unilever's loop has run about three years (volume 4.2% in H1-26, margin 19.4% to 20.0%). Danone's Renew ran 2022 to 2025 and then slowed in China. Kraft Heinz shows the reverse loop: cuts led to distribution losses, which forced a USD 600m to 700m reinvestment.
    • What flips it. RIG falling below about 1.5% excluding the recall lap, or A&P cut to protect margin.
  2. Brand concentration (reinforcing for priority brands; balancing for the tail). - Mechanism. Supported brands were cut from over 400 to about 135 to 150. Priority brands and growth platforms (30% of sales, 7% organic growth in H1-26) gain. The tail declines. - Expected net. About +0.2pp to 0.3pp of mix in RIG, with the tail at about -1% to -2% a year. - Precedent. Unilever went from 370 to 180 brands, with 25 power brands now 78% to 80% of turnover, and cut SKUs 40%. - Risk. Shelf-space loss in coffee if variants are cut too far (expert call).
  3. Retailer give-back loop (balancing; gross margin in H1-27). - Mechanism. Falling coffee and cocoa costs trigger retailer demands. Delistings or price cuts follow, which lower brand margin and leave less to reinvest. - Evidence.
    • Morgan Stanley, 12 November 2026: "Retailers vs Suppliers: a new playbook?".
    • Europe pricing was 0.2% in Q3-26.
    • The Intermarché delisting ran from early 2026 to its resolution in Q3. The Edeka–Mars dispute lasted 18 months.
    • Strength. High in H1-27, in Europe (20% of sales), in coffee and confectionery.
    • Limit. Retailers cannot keep number-one and number-two brands off shelf for long. Nestlé holds number-one or number-two positions in 80% to 85% of its markets.
    • Modelling. About half the relief is lost.
  4. Currency loop (balancing; reported revenue, margin and EPS). - Mechanism. Franc strength cuts reported earnings, which pressures pricing or reinvestment, which costs volume. - Evidence. The 2015 precedent: franc strength took 7.4% off reported sales, and pricing fell to 1% in Q4-15 under a Swiss cost base that had to be cut. In 2022 to 2025, FX took 7.8%, 3.7% and 5.7% off sales and underlying EPS fell 7.3% at actual rates in FY25. - Current state. The loop is acting on reported numbers only. Management has not cut A&P in response, which is the change from 2015. - Modelling. -1.0% to -1.5% FX a year, about -0.8% to -1.2% of EPS.
  5. Balance-sheet loop (balancing; EPS growth, capital returns, multiple). - Mechanism. Leverage at 2.5x to 3x and a dividend absorbing about 87% to 92% of FCF after leases mean no buybacks. EPS growth then rests only on operations, which means less support for the multiple. - Easing. It eases once, in FY27, with about CHF 4.3bn of net disposal cash, and then stalls (P-02). - The hidden twist. The disposals that relieve the balance sheet also remove EBITDA: about CHF 0.75bn, from Russia, Waters, mainstream vitamins and ice cream. The leverage ratio therefore falls by less than the cash suggests.
  6. Organisational loop (balancing risk; volume execution in 2027). - Mechanism. Cutting 16,000 roles alongside a performance-culture push brings talent loss and execution drag, which slow volume. - Evidence so far. None in the numbers: Q3-26 RIG was 2.8%. Expert calls cite leaders leaving for Danone, and Nestlé's own prospectus names the risk. - Timing. Peak exposure is 2027, the year most white-collar cuts land.
  7. Petcare capacity loop (reinforcing; Petcare revenue and margin). - Mechanism. New capacity (Batavia, Ohio, opened August 2026 for over USD 550m; plus Italy and Thailand) lets Purina serve unmet demand. Share gains follow, and with them scale economics. - Evidence. Q3-26 Petcare organic growth was 5.3% with RIG 4.3%. Sell-in matched sell-out, and share was taken from Blue Buffalo. Jefferies models 4% Petcare volume for 2027. - Counterweight. US pet market growth slowed to about 1% (Bernstein, October 2026). Dog-food shoppers are trading down to private label, which gained 300bp of US dog volume share in 2025. - Strength. Moderate to strong in cat, weak in dog.

How the loops shape the line items.

Line Next 18 months FY28–FY30 Dominant loops
Organic revenue 3.6% to 3.8% 3.7% to 3.8% Volume and petcare loops lift RIG to about 2.2% to 2.4%; give-backs hold pricing to about 1% in FY27
Gross margin +70bp in FY27 Flat in FY28 Commodity window versus give-backs
UTOP margin +25bp a year +15bp to 20bp a year Savings fund the volume loop
Reported revenue and EPS Flat — Currency and perimeter
Capital returns None Token Balance-sheet loop

Dated indicators that would show the volume loop stalling or reversing.

  • 18 February 2027 (FY26 results): an FY27 organic guide below 3% to 4%; any cut to A&P intent; Q4-26 RIG below about 1.8% (Q4 laps a tougher H2-25 RIG of about 1.4%).
  • Late April 2027 (Q1-27 sales): RIG below about 1.5% after stripping out the roughly 0.9pp recall lap; Zone Europe RIG negative; North American confectionery still down double digits.
  • Late July 2027 (H1-27 results): the A&P ratio below the 8.9% of H1-26, or UTOP below about 16.4% despite commodity relief. Either would mean the reinvestment is not producing operating leverage.
  • Monthly: Bernstein's Western European and US scanner readings for Nestlé, against Danone and private label.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

D4243rd percentile

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Four uncommon features affect how the Consumer should read Nestlé's guidance:

  1. The company publishes its own compiled consensus before results (for example "Company-compiled consensus: pre-Q3 results 2026"). Beats and misses are measured against a figure the company has seen.
  2. Currency guidance is unusually large and volatile. It moved from "-3% to -6%" to about -3% to about -2% within the year. Reported-currency outcomes are as much a franc call as an operating one.
  3. Reporting structure keeps changing. Five zones became three; Nestlé Health Science was folded in; segments went from six to five; product categories were re-cut into four pillars; Waters is to be deconsolidated. Year-on-year comparisons and long histories are harder to build.
  4. Disclosure lapses. The 30bp pension credit was revealed only after analysts asked. Margin phasing language was softened ("broadly similar").

Also: sales-only quarterly updates (Q1 and Q3) mean margin and cash evidence arrives only twice a year. The next margin data point is FY26 on 18 February 2027.

  • Positives. The new IR head David Hancock and CFO Manz provide clearer cumulative trackers (savings, recall drag). Growth-platform disclosure is new. The Q3 call and transcript are detailed.

  • The margin ambition was rebased at the November 2024 CMD from 17.5%+ to "at or above 16%" for FY25. It was then delivered at 16.1%.
  • FY25 absorbed the reinvestment, the commodity spike and heavy charges: impairments CHF 923m; restructuring and litigation CHF 1.7bn.
  • H1-26 took the CHF 1.3bn VMS and ice cream write-down.
  • Consensus EPS went through a three-year downgrade cycle. It is now stable to slightly down: FY27 -0.8% over one month, -0.5% over three, +1.0% over six, -2.3% over twelve. Revision breadth is narrowly negative (EPS -1, EBITDA -1, sales -1 over three months).
  • The rating mix is improving: buy share 44%, +8pp.

This is close to a "cleared base". The remaining estimate risks are specific:

  • Russia, not yet fully modelled by all brokers;
  • the franc;
  • commodity re-inflation in FY28.

None is a generic over-optimism.

  • Dispersion is narrow. FY27 EPS spans CHF 4.55–4.92 (about ±4%). Morgan Stanley (CHF 4.60, Sell) is at the low end. Restricted and CIC brokers are at the top (CHF 4.83–4.92). There is no outlier bear forecast that would suggest an unrecognised risk.
  • Not sandbagged in the Wisdom Library sense. Management targets are honest, not padded. Kitchen-sinking happened through charges (impairments, restructuring), not guidance padding. The H2-26 "broadly similar" margin wording is the only recent soft-pedal.
  • Wisdom Library: "Composite Signal Masks Component Deterioration". The dashboard's sell-side momentum D masks a positive target-price and buy-share trend alongside slightly negative EPS, EBITDA and sales revisions. The profit lines are still drifting down. This is not an upgrade cycle yet.

Case simplicity

Group judgement

Risks

C-5552nd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The seven answers describe a company whose operations are easy to forecast and whose reported earnings and share price are not, for reasons that lie mostly outside it.

  • What is predictable. The earlier sets hang together: the same causal chain runs through both, and the points where they jar are arithmetic and move FY27 EPS by less than 1% (D-01). The base case asks for little and draws on strengths Nestlé has shown: savings delivered twice, volume of about 2% once pricing eased and capacity opened, exits structured as joint ventures (D-02). FY26 is nearly fixed. For FY27, organic growth, the margin and constant-currency EPS fall in narrow ranges: about 2.9% to 4.0%, 16.3% to 17.0% and ±2.4% (D-04). The negative items in the FY27 bridge (Russia, the pension credit, the exits, together about 4.8% of EPS) are known to within half a point (D-03).
  • What is not. Reported EPS for FY27 has a range of about ±4.6%, and three-quarters of that is the franc (D-04). The improvement in the EPS trajectory against the last six years comes from the franc and the commodity cycle, not from the turnaround (D-03). The largest uncertain operating item, how much coffee and cocoa relief Nestlé keeps and when the relief reverses, depends on two things the company does not disclose (D-05, D-06). About half of the share price's movement is the sector's, and Nestlé's beta to the sector is at a two-year high (D-07).
  • The pattern that joins these. The variable the share price has followed, quarterly RIG, is the one to which EPS is least sensitive: a full point is about 1.4% of EPS. The variables to which EPS is most sensitive, the franc, the perimeter and the price-cost spread, are the ones the market has looked through. The case is therefore predictable in a way that can mislead. Our forecast can be right on every operating line and the reported result can still disappoint a market that has not adjusted for the perimeter. Equally, reported EPS can be flat for two years and the shares can hold, if RIG stays near 2% and the sector is firm.
  • The sequence of prints matters as much as their level. Reported EPS growth runs about +8%, 0% and -1% to -2% over the three half-years, while the engine grows about 5% at constant currency and perimeter. The most favourable print is the next one. The test the earlier sets identified, the H1-27 margin, will show about +10bp in the headline against about +40bp underneath. The window ends with FY28 guidance in February 2028, where the cone is more than twice as wide.

For the Consumer, the steer is to describe predictability in two parts and not to average them. The operating case deserves a high rating for predictability: diversified, slow-moving, well disclosed on the top line, with a delivery record on savings and a low bar. The reported and share-price case deserves a middling to low one: it is concentrated in the franc, the commodity cycle and the sector's rating, none of which Nestlé controls. The dated tests are 18 February 2027 (Q4 RIG, H2-26 gross margin, FY27 guidance and the perimeter), the end of the European negotiating round in March 2027, late April 2027 (Q1 RIG without the recall lap) and late July 2027 (the H1-27 margin).

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

F184th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The perimeter is extremely wide:

  • 185 countries;
  • four category pillars, plus Nespresso and Waters as separate segments;
  • three zones;
  • 75 factory countries;
  • more than 30 billion-franc brands.

It is narrowing by about 10% of sales over 2026–27. Width cuts both ways for predictability:

  • Aggregate organic growth is unusually stable (2–4% outside the inflation years), because diversification averages out category and country shocks.
  • Reported EPS is unusually hard to predict, because currency, scope changes and many simultaneous moving parts compound.

The 2026–27 narrowing raises underlying quality but temporarily lowers forecastability, because the scope changes are many, overlapping and inconsistently modelled by brokers.

  • Width. About 41% of sales in emerging markets. USD about 32% of revenue, EUR about 20%, CNY about 5.5%, BRL about 4.5%, MXN about 4%, GBP about 4%, CHF about 1%. More than 2,000 brands are implied by "400 to 135–150 supported".
  • Narrowing. Waters (about 4%) to a JV, VMS mainstream (about 1.2%), ice cream (about 1.1%), Russia (about 2%), Blue Bottle; yfood added.
  • Predictability signals.
  • Organic growth over FY23–Q3-26: 7.2%, 2.2%, 3.5%, then a quarterly range of 3.5–4.0%.
  • Reported revenue growth was negative every year.
  • Guidance has been cut mid-year (FY24).
  • Consensus FY27 revenue spans CHF 84.5–94.3bn across brokers.
  • The Change Forces report's simplicity test fails. Four fulcrum drivers (savings, coffee price and volume, commodities, currency). Six-plus key drivers. Six live headwinds.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C+7873rd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The base case is plausible mainly because it asks for little, and what it asks for rests on strengths Nestlé has shown. By FY28 it requires Nestlé to recover 70bp of margin, less than two-thirds of the 110bp it lost in 2025 alone, with a savings programme larger in relation to sales (3.4%) than the 2016 to 2020 programme (3.1%) that accompanied a 160bp rise. It requires RIG of about 2%, which the business reached in each of the last two quarters once pricing fell and petcare capacity opened. It does not require real pricing power, bargaining power over European retailers, a changed culture or a leading digital position. Those are the strengths Nestlé would need to acquire, and they are required only by management's own ambition of 4%+ organic growth and a 17%+ margin, which the base case does not reach until FY29. The thesis as modelled relies on demonstrated strengths; the thesis as management states it does not.

The expected change, set against the record.

What the base case expects The record Strength it draws on Demonstrated or to be acquired Plausibility
Gross savings of CHF 3.0bn by end-2027 CHF 1.1bn in FY25 against CHF 0.7bn planned; CHF 1.7bn by H1-26; the 2016 to 2020 programme delivered CHF 2.8bn against CHF 1.8bn Procurement scale (about CHF 65bn of spend through three hubs), shared services Demonstrated, twice About 90%
RIG of 2.4% in FY27 (about 2.05% excluding the recall lap), then 2.2% to 2.3% -0.3%, 0.8% and 0.8% in FY23 to FY25; 1.2%, 1.8% and 2.8% in Q1 to Q3 of 2026. From my own knowledge of the company's results, RIG ran at about 1.6% to 3.0% in 2016 to 2019, with pricing of about 0.5% to 0.8%, the same regime as the one forecast Petcare capacity, emerging-market distribution, advertising funded by savings Demonstrated in petcare and emerging markets; bought, not earned, in the contested middle About 60% to 65% for 2% or more in FY27
Margin +25bp in FY27 and +20bp in FY28 H1-26 flat once the pension credit is removed; +40bp a year in 2016 to 2020 with lower inflation and flat advertising Savings, the commodity lag, the low-margin exits Demonstrated or contractual. No pricing power is needed beyond keeping half the coffee and cocoa relief About 60% for 16.5% or more in FY27
The exits close in 2027 and net about CHF 4.3bn Froneri (2016 onward), Cereal Partners and Galderma are the precedents; the ice cream sale is still under negotiation Joint-venture structuring Demonstrated About 85% by end-2027
16,000 roles removed without damage to sales No precedent at Nestlé on this scale. The risk is named in its own prospectus (29 May 2026) A performance culture To be acquired About 70% that no damage shows in RIG
Half of the commodity relief kept against European retailers Europe pricing of 0.2% in Q3-26; Intermarché delisted about 30 brands in early 2026 and the dispute was settled by Q3 Brand strength against buying alliances Partly demonstrated. Nestlé settles disputes within months, but by conceding About 55%
Management's ambition: organic growth of 4%+ and margin of 17%+ Organic growth of 4% last reached on pricing; margin targets missed since 2017 Real pricing power, European bargaining power, culture, digital To be acquired About 15% to 25% by FY28

What the peer precedents add (AlphaSense, 18 November 2026).

  • Volume-led turnarounds funded by productivity do work, and they take two to four years. Unilever and Colgate are the successes. Both put volume first and reinvested the gross savings. Colgate's programme delivered 290bp of savings in Q3-26, almost exactly the 300bp of raw-material inflation it faced, while advertising reached 14.4% of sales. That is the second set's "a quarter reaches the margin" in a peer's accounts.
  • The failure is the opposite choice. Kraft Heinz dropped its cuts to profit, lost distribution and brand strength, and now holds a reinvestment base of USD 700m simply to stabilise, with little improvement in tracked sales (Bernstein, November 2026). Nestlé's rule that savings fund advertising, and its bonus gate on RIG, are designed to prevent this. The risk returns only if the margin target is defended by cutting advertising.
  • Commodity relief is shared, not kept. Mondelez lifted its operating margin by 190bp in Q3-26 on lower raw materials while cutting European prices by 3.7%, and said it will reinvest a meaningful part. JDE Peet's passed lower coffee costs through, missed revenue by 5% and held profit. Keurig Dr Pepper cut United States coffee prices by 4.2%. The pattern supports "keep about half", and it shows that give-backs reduce revenue more than profit.
  • New capacity helps when it is aimed at premium formats, and it can stumble. Hill's grew 5.7% on wet-cat capacity and therapeutic diets. Mars's North American dog food volumes fell by as much as 10%. Danone's eight expansion projects in United States yogurt caused service failures that cut volume. Purina's Batavia plant (opened August 2026) is aimed at wet cat, the right format, and Q3-26 petcare RIG of 4.3% with sell-in matching sell-out shows no disruption so far. The ramp-up curve is undisclosed.

Where the present delivery is weakest. Profit has not yet moved. The only half reported under the new chief executive (H1-26) shows a margin of 16.1% excluding the pension credit, against 16.5% a year earlier, with gross margin down 20bp. The base case's H2-26 implies a gross margin of about 46.8% against 44.6% in H2-25 (FactSet basis), a rise of about 220bp, but a UTOP margin up only about 60bp (16.3% against 15.7%). If the February 2027 results show that pattern, it will be the first reported evidence both that the commodity relief is real and that roughly three-quarters of it is being spent. That is the delivery the forecasts for 2027 depend on, and it has not yet been seen.

The strengths the thesis does not need, which protects its plausibility. The base case assumes no buyback, no sale of L'Oréal shares, no acquisition, no real price increase, no margin above 17% before FY29 and no organic growth above 3.8%. Each of these would be upside. The one acquired strength the base case does need is organisational: removing about 6% of the workforce in two years while holding volume. Peak exposure is 2027.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

A6892nd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The cone is narrow for what the business does and moderately wide for what the accounts will report. FY26 is almost fixed: nine months of sales and a half of profit are known. For FY27, organic growth should fall within about 2.9% to 4.0%, the margin within about 16.3% to 17.0% and constant-currency EPS within about ±2.4% of the base case. Reported EPS should fall within about ±4.6% (CHF 4.32 to 4.74), with the franc about three-quarters of the variance. The skew is mildly negative, by about 1% on the mean. It is negative on organic growth, the franc and the second-half margin, and positive on the first-half margin and FY26 cash flow. The case is therefore predictable in direction and in its operating lines, and only moderately predictable in reported EPS. The cone widens sharply for FY28, which is first seen when guidance is given in February 2028, at the end of the window.

The cone by line (plausible ranges, which I intend to hold roughly four outcomes in five).

Line Base case Plausible range Skew Main reasons
FY26 organic growth 3.7% 3.5% to 3.8% Slightly down Nine months at 3.7%. Q4 began with Western European volume of -3.2% (Bernstein scanner, 27 October 2026); the Q3 heatwave and confectionery phasing reverse
FY26 margin 16.35% 16.2% to 16.5% Neutral H1 at 16.4%; guidance of H2 "broadly similar"
FY26 EPS CHF 4.55 CHF 4.48 to 4.62 Neutral H1 at CHF 2.22
FY26 free cash flow, company definition CHF 9.85bn CHF 9.3bn to 10.3bn Up H2-25 alone gave CHF 6.8bn; guidance is "above CHF 9bn"
FY27 RIG 2.4% 1.6% to 3.0% Slightly down Recall lap of about 0.35 points is mechanical; Europe is soft
FY27 organic growth 3.6% 2.9% to 4.0% Down Pass-through of lower coffee and cocoa costs cuts pricing; consensus is about 3.2%
FY27 margin 16.6% 16.3% to 17.0% Up in H1, down in H2 Commodity relief peaks in H1; cost inflation returns in H2
FY27 reported revenue growth -2.3% About -7% to +3% Down Currency of ±5 points dominates; closing dates add ±0.7
FY27 EPS CHF 4.53 CHF 4.32 to 4.74 Slightly down See the decomposition below
FY27 free cash flow, company definition CHF 9.66bn CHF 9.0bn to 10.2bn Neutral Working capital and restructuring timing

What makes up the FY27 EPS cone.

Source Range Effect on EPS Share of variance
Franc ±5% on the average rate About ±4.0% About 75%
Margin ±30bp About ±1.9% About 17%
RIG ±0.75 points About ±1.1% About 6%
Finance cost and associates ±CHF 0.1bn About ±0.7% About 2%
Closing dates of the exits ± one quarter About ±0.4% Under 1%
Combined, if independent About ±4.6%
Combined without the franc About ±2.4%
  • The franc. The sensitivity is the company's and the brokers': a 5% move is about 4% of EPS and 10bp to 15bp of margin (company aide-mémoire, 28 September 2026). On my reading of the quarterly effects, the franc weakened a little through the second half of 2026, so at today's rates FY27 translation would be about zero. The base case's -1.0% is a drift assumption, not a mark to market.
  • The margin. ±30bp is the error in the bridge (D-01). It is not wider because two of its parts offset. When retailers claim a larger share of the commodity relief, pricing and revenue fall but the cost base has fallen too. JDE Peet's missed revenue by 5% in Q3-26 on pass-through and held its profit (Morgan Stanley, 4 November 2026). For Nestlé this means that organic growth and margin tend to miss in opposite directions. The combined effect on EPS is smaller than either alone, which narrows the EPS cone and widens the organic-growth cone.
  • Volume. One point of RIG is about 1.5% of operating profit (second set, P-01). Even the first set's bear case of 1% RIG would cost only about 2% of EPS.
  • Probabilities that follow. FY27 EPS at or above today's consensus of CHF 4.72: about 15%. FY27 EPS below FY25's CHF 4.42, so that EPS has fallen over two years: about 25% to 30%. FY27 EPS within CHF 4.45 to 4.60: about 50%.

The skew, driver by driver.

  • Negative: organic growth. The base case's 3.6% is about 0.4 points above consensus and rests on underlying RIG of about 2.05% and pricing of 1.2%, almost all from emerging markets. European pricing is likely to be negative in H1-27. Mondelez cut European prices by 3.7% in Q3-26, and Deutsche Bank expects its negative European pricing to persist for several quarters; Lindt targets single-digit wholesale price cuts in 2027. My central expectation is about 3.3% to 3.4%.
  • Positive: the H1-27 margin. Chocolate pipeline costs are expected to fall 40% in H2-26 and 48% in H1-27 (Morgan Stanley, 4 November 2026), and coffee about 20% in 2026 (Jefferies). Morgan Stanley expects 2027 margins to be weighted to the first half.
  • Negative: the H2-27 margin. The base case has H2-27 UTOP (16.7%) above H1-27 (16.5%) because the low-margin businesses leave. If Nestlé's cover is at the short end of the range in the sources (three to six months), the cost increases expected from a strong El Niño reach the P&L in H2-27 and not in 2028. NOAA puts the probability of very strong El Niño conditions persisting into early 2027 above 83%, and Ivory Coast and Ghana cocoa output is forecast down 17% and 18% to 38% (AlphaSense, 18 November 2026). Against that, the futures curves for arabica, robusta and cocoa slope down through 2028, and the industry holds more than ten months of cocoa cover (Barry Callebaut, 4 November 2026).
  • Negative: the franc's tail. The centre is near zero, but large moves have been in one direction. Forecasters cited in the first set expect gentle strengthening, and the franc gains in risk-off markets.
  • Negative, small: timing. If Peranel closes at the end of Q1-27 and not at mid-year, FY27 loses a further quarter of Waters (about -0.4% of EPS, with cash arriving sooner). Jefferies assumed part of the ice cream sale would complete in FY26.
  • Positive: FY26 cash flow. The base case of CHF 9.85bn implies H2-26 below H2-25. An outcome of CHF 10bn or more is as likely as one below CHF 9.6bn.
  • Tail events outside the cone. A second food-safety event; coordinated delistings by a European alliance (the Edeka and Mars dispute lasted about 18 months); withdrawal of supplier finance (about 7% of sales); a jump in the franc of 10% or more. Each is individually unlikely within 18 months. The first set's point holds that each would push Nestlé back toward price or cost cuts that undo the volume strategy.

The path of reported EPS through the window. This is the pattern the market will see, and I do not find it stated in either earlier set.

Half-year Underlying EPS Change on a year earlier Reported
H2-25 (actual) CHF 2.15
H1-26 (actual) CHF 2.22 -2.4% July 2026
H2-26 About CHF 2.33 About +8% 18 February 2027
H1-27 About CHF 2.21 to 2.25 About 0% Late July 2027
H2-27 About CHF 2.28 to 2.32 About -1% to -2% February 2028
  • H2-26 is the best print of the window. It compares with a half in which the margin was 15.7% and translation was heavily negative. The base case has a margin of 16.3%, revenue up 4.3% and gross margin up about 220bp (46.8% against 44.6%, FactSet basis). FY26 EPS will be the first to rise in francs since FY22. The same results will carry reported net income of about CHF 5.5bn to 6bn and the first full statement of the perimeter.
  • H1-27 will look flat. The underlying improvement of about 40bp in margin is offset in the headline by the pension credit in the comparison and by Russia. The headline margin will read about 16.5% against 16.4%.
  • H2-27 will look down. Revenue falls about 5% as Waters, vitamins and ice cream leave, and commodity relief fades.
  • Consequence for predictability. The direction of each print is predictable. What is not predictable is whether the market reads the sequence as "+8%, 0%, -2%" or as "about +5% a year at constant currency and perimeter". The company's presentation at the February 2027 results, and whether it restates a pro-forma base, will decide much of that.

Beyond the window. The second set's cone for FY28 is CHF 4.1 to 5.2 around CHF 4.59, about ±12%, more than twice the FY27 cone. Three things widen it: commodity inflation returning as savings end, stranded costs as transitional service agreements expire (peer precedents put these at up to 1% of sales and 24 to 36 months to remove; AlphaSense, 18 November 2026), and a second year of currency drift. FY28 guidance in February 2028 is the event inside the window that carries this risk.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

D5046th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Disclosure is helpful for tracking operating momentum and only partly helpful for tracking the drivers that decide the case. In the next six months Nestlé will give two dated readings: full-year results with FY27 guidance on 18 February 2027, and Q1-27 sales in late April 2027. Together they give a full view of volume, pricing, the gross margin, advertising, savings and cash, and a first view of the perimeter. They do not give the reading on which the 18-month case turns, the H1-27 margin, which arrives in late July 2027, outside the six months. Nor does Nestlé disclose the items that would let an outsider predict it: net savings after reinvestment, commodity cover, and the outcome of the European negotiations. Against that, the three drivers of our difference from consensus (the franc, the perimeter and finance costs) can be tracked from outside the company more easily than the operating thesis can.

What the company will disclose in the next six months, and what each item tells us.

Date Disclosure What to read from it Helpfulness
About two to three weeks before each event Aide-mémoire and company-compiled consensus The currency effect, the bar the result will be judged against, and how far brokers have removed Russia and the exits High. Uncommon among peers
18 February 2027 Q4-26 organic growth, RIG and pricing by zone and category Whether RIG holds near 2% without the heatwave and phasing; whether European pricing has turned negative High
18 February 2027 H2-26 gross margin, UTOP margin by zone and category, advertising ratio Whether commodity relief has arrived (gross margin of about 46.8% in H2 on the base case) and how much is being spent High, though only twice a year
18 February 2027 Cumulative savings (target CHF 2.0bn) and any new target Delivery, and whether a second programme is announced High on the gross figure; nothing on the net
18 February 2027 Free cash flow, net debt, restructuring, supplier-finance balance in the notes FY26 cash flow against "above CHF 9bn"; leverage against the range of 2.0x to 3.0x High
18 February 2027 Russia: balance-sheet detail promised for the full-year report The final charges, tax effect and lost interest income Medium to high
18 February 2027 FY27 guidance and scope Organic growth, margin direction and cash flow; management has said margin detail on the exits comes only after closing Medium. Outputs, not a bridge
Late April 2027 Q1-27 sales by zone and category RIG after removing the recall lap of about 0.9 points; the first pricing reading after the European round High for volume and price; no profit data
By mid-2027 Closing announcements for Peranel, vitamins and ice cream The dates that set the perimeter High, but timing is not in Nestlé's hands

What is not disclosed, and why it matters.

  • Net savings. Nestlé reports gross savings only. The fraction that reaches margin is the largest judgement in the base case (about a quarter) and cannot be observed directly, only inferred from the gap between gross margin and UTOP margin twice a year.
  • Commodity cover and hedge prices. These are undisclosed, and estimates of cover in the sources range from three to twelve months. The timing of both the relief and its reversal therefore cannot be confirmed in advance.
  • European contract outcomes. These are confidential. France's statutory deadline at the end of February 2027 falls after the full-year results, so the February guidance will be given before the outcome is known to the market.
  • A pro-forma base. Nestlé has not yet restated its accounts without Russia, Waters, vitamins and ice cream. Until it does, brokers will keep modelling different perimeters (FY27 revenue estimates run from CHF 84.5bn to 94.3bn).
  • Stranded costs and transitional service terms. The chief financial officer has said that supply agreements cover most stranded costs. No amount, fee or duration has been given.
  • Profit between half-years. Q1 and Q3 are sales-only. In the next six months there is one profit reading, and it is distorted by the absence of the pension credit and one quarter without Russia.
  • Lapses. The 30bp pension credit in H1-26 emerged only after analysts asked, and the H2-26 margin wording was softened without emphasis. The Russian disclosure of 22 October 2026 was the opposite: prompt and quantified (sales, UTOP, cash flow, both charges and the effect on net debt).

What can be tracked from outside, monthly or daily.

  • Volume and price in developed markets. Bernstein's Western European scanner and the Nielsen data for the United States, monthly, for Nestlé against Danone and private label. These led the Q4-26 concern (European volume of -3.2% at the start of the quarter).
  • The price-cost spread. Arabica, robusta and cocoa futures daily; Morgan Stanley's chocolate pipeline-cost tracker and Jefferies' coffee and commodity trackers monthly.
  • Retailer disputes. Delistings are reported in the press and collected by brokers (Morgan Stanley, 12 November 2026).
  • The franc. Daily, against the dollar and the euro. About ±4% of EPS for each 5%.
  • Finance costs. Nestlé's bond issues are public, with coupons (4.20% to 4.80% on dollar issues in H1-26).
  • Peers who report first. Lindt, Danone, Unilever and Mondelez give January and February readings on European pricing and volume before or alongside Nestlé.

A watch-list for the six months, with the levels that would change my view.

Indicator Supports the base case Would lower it
Q4-26 RIG (18 February 2027) 1.8% or more Below 1.5%
Q4-26 pricing About 1.0% or more, Europe near zero Group below 0.7%, Europe below -1%
H2-26 gross margin About 46.5% or more Below 46%
H2-26 UTOP margin 16.2% or more Below 16%
Advertising ratio 9% or more Below 8.9%, which would mean margin is being protected at the cost of volume

Comparison with peers. Nestlé discloses more on the top line than Danone or Unilever (RIG and pricing separately each quarter, by zone and category) and publishes its own consensus. It discloses less on profit, which Unilever and Danone also report half-yearly but with clearer statements of reinvestment: Danone showed 77bp of operational improvement against 59bp reinvested in 2025. Nestlé gives no equivalent.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D4732nd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The trace is clear at both ends and opaque in the middle. What Nestlé promises on volume, gross savings, the exits, cash flow and leverage maps onto named, dated and published measures. What it promises on margin does not: there is no stated net saving, no bridge to 17% and no date. Between UTOP and EPS the company guides only the tax rate, although that stretch is mechanical and can be modelled. The external transmission, from coffee, cocoa, the franc, retailers and interest rates to EPS, is clearer in mechanism than the internal one. Each link has a usable rule of thumb. It is unclear in timing and in share, because the two facts that set them, the length of commodity cover and the outcome of retailer negotiations, are not disclosed. The result is a case in which an outsider can say with confidence what will move EPS and by how much for each unit of change, but not when the largest operating item, the price-cost spread, will arrive or how much of it Nestlé will keep.

The internal trace: from what the company says to EPS.

What the company says it will achieve Operational driver Line it lands on What is published Clarity
RIG of at least 2%; organic growth of 4%+ Advertising above 9% of sales on 135 to 150 brands; growth platforms at 30% of sales; petcare capacity Revenue RIG and pricing each quarter by zone and category High
Fuel for Growth: CHF 3.0bn gross by end-2027 Procurement (about CHF 65bn of spend); 16,000 roles, of which white-collar savings of CHF 1.0bn Cost of goods, distribution, administration Cumulative gross savings twice a year High for gross, none for net
"Savings fund growth" Reinvestment in advertising, price and growth platforms (CHF 600m in 2026) Advertising ratio; pricing Advertising ratio twice a year Medium. The split between kept and spent is never stated
Margin to improve in FY26; 17%+ in the medium term The sum of the above, plus commodity costs and mix UTOP margin Margin twice a year, by zone and category Low. No bridge and no date
Exits of Waters, mainstream vitamins and ice cream Joint venture and sales Revenue, margin, associates, net debt Prices and proceeds; expected closing in H1 2027 High on terms; medium on dates; margin and EPS effects come only from brokers
Free cash flow above CHF 9bn; capital spending below 5% of sales Working-capital measures; capital spending discipline Cash flow Twice a year High
Leverage toward the middle of 2.0x to 3.0x Disposal proceeds; retained cash Net debt Twice a year High
Tax rate of about 22% underlying Tax Guided High
Net finance cost; associates Below UTOP Not guided. The chief financial officer said only that Russian interest income is "not material" Low on guidance, but mechanical
  • Where the trace breaks. It breaks between gross savings and margin. CHF 3.0bn is 3.4% of sales. The base case has about 0.8% reaching the margin by FY28. Jefferies says about CHF 2bn is "net". Morgan Stanley models 10bp of margin gain in FY27. All three readings are consistent with the same disclosures, which shows how little the disclosures constrain the answer.
  • The measures that do work as milestones. Seven published measures carry almost the whole operating case, and each can be checked against a number: RIG (2% or more), pricing (about 1%), gross margin (46.6% in FY26, 47.3% in FY27 on the base case), the advertising ratio (9% or more), UTOP margin (16.35%, then 16.6%), cumulative savings (CHF 2.0bn, then CHF 3.0bn) and free cash flow (above CHF 9bn). Two half-yearly comparisons do the work that a net-savings disclosure would do: gross margin against UTOP margin (how much relief is spent) and the advertising ratio against RIG (what the spending buys).
  • From UTOP to EPS. A change of 10bp in margin is about CHF 0.028 of EPS (0.6%). Net finance cost of about CHF 1.4bn to 1.5bn, associates less minorities of about CHF 1.3bn to 1.4bn and tax of 22% complete the calculation. Share count is flat. Because there is no buyback and no large acquisition, this stretch is simpler than at any time in the last decade.

The external trace: from value-chain dynamics to EPS.

Dynamic Mechanism Rule of thumb Lag Clarity
Coffee prices Green coffee is about 5% of sales A 20% fall is about 100bp of sales before pass-through About nine months from spot to P&L (sources range from three to twelve) Mechanism high; timing and share kept medium to low
Cocoa prices Cocoa is about 1% of sales A 45% fall in pipeline cost is about 45bp before pass-through As above As above
Retailers' claim on the relief Price give-backs and funded promotions in annual negotiations The base case keeps about half Six to twelve months after the cost falls; Europe's round is early 2027 Low. Confidential
Other inputs (milk, sugar, wheat, palm oil, packaging, freight) About 27% of sales The World Bank expects its agricultural index to rise 5% in 2027 Shorter Medium
The franc Translation, unhedged A 5% move is about 4% of EPS and 10bp to 15bp of margin Immediate High
Interest rates Refinancing of maturing debt at about 4% against 2.3% About CHF 0.09bn a year (base case); +100bp on the CHF 16.3bn maturing within a year is CHF 163m, or 1.3% of pre-tax profit (Morgan Stanley, 20 October 2026) One to five years High
Consumer demand Volume One point of RIG is about 1.5% of operating profit Immediate High
L'Oréal Equity-accounted share of profit About 10% of net income; only about 60% is received as dividend Immediate High
  • Pass-through differs sharply by product, which is why "about half" is reasonable for Nestlé and not for a coffee specialist. In European roast and ground coffee, retail prices follow green coffee costs six months earlier with a correlation of about 90% (Rothschild & Co Redburn, cited by AlphaSense, 18 November 2026): relief there passes almost wholly to the shopper. Roast and ground is about 5% of Nestlé's sales against 23% of JDE Peet's (Jefferies, June 2025). Most of Nestlé's coffee is soluble and capsules, where the margin fell less in the upswing (Nespresso -360bp, other coffee -540bp) and where the direct channel leaves no retailer to claim the relief. In chocolate, retail prices have followed cocoa with a correlation of only about 30% over 25 years (Redburn, July 2026), but retailers are now more forceful: Rewe has publicly attacked manufacturers, and Mondelez and Lindt are cutting prices.
  • The asymmetry that the sell side rarely joins up. Private-label suppliers renew contracts quarterly or half-yearly and reprice first when costs fall. Branded suppliers with longer cover are pressed to match before their own costs have fallen. If costs then rise again (El Niño; Morgan Stanley expects commodity inflation in the P&L from H2-27), the brand has conceded price in H1-27 and absorbs higher costs in H2-27 and 2028. Morgan Stanley and Bernstein each describe one half of this. AlphaSense's narrative report (18 November 2026) finds that the two halves are rarely linked. This double squeeze is the mechanism behind the base case's flat underlying margin in FY28.
  • The franc is the clearest link and the least predictable input. Nothing about the mechanism is uncertain. Only the exchange rate is.
  • Two different interest-rate sensitivities are quoted and both are right. The company's CHF 17m for a rise of 100bp measures only floating-rate debt. Morgan Stanley's CHF 163m measures the refinancing of everything due within a year. The first is why finance costs cannot jump inside 18 months. The second is why they cannot fall as consensus implies.

Peers. Unilever and Danone give a clearer internal trace on one point: both state what they reinvest. JDE Peet's has the clearest external trace, because it has said it passes coffee costs through, and its revenue and profit behave accordingly. Nestlé's external trace is blurred by its breadth, which is also what makes its aggregate result steadier.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

B+3885th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: Sentiment risk is now elevated because the share price has run ahead of earnings:

  • the price is at its 52-week high (CHF 87.72 against a high of 87.75);
  • it sits 10.8% above the 200-day average;
  • the 24-month-forward P/E re-rated from about 16.5x to 17.6x while FY27 EPS fell 2.3% over 12 months;
  • the consensus target price (CHF 88.62) equals the price.

The main sentiment shocks that could hit regardless of fundamentals:

  1. A rates-led staples de-rating. The US 10-year is near 5%. Defensive leadership is fading: FfF notes "defensives turned negative against cyclicals at one week and twelve months". Leveraged defensives de-rate first (Wisdom Library), and Nestlé is the most leveraged large staple.
  2. A sector rotation into cyclicals if FfF breadth triggers fire (breadth at 65%, the three-month defensive lead below +2 points).
  3. GLP-1 headline shocks, such as an oral approval, Lilly data or survey data, hitting all packaged food.
  4. Peer blow-ups. Danone's China slowdown; Orkla's volume miss (shares -15% post-print); Lindt; and Unilever's new 4–6% algorithm as a comparison that makes Nestlé look slow.
  5. Earnings-quality headlines (pension-credit-type stories; the Russia write-off).
  6. A "turnaround fatigue" reaction if Q4-26 RIG falls back below 2% after Q3's weather-assisted 2.8%.

The downside reaction function is visible: Nestlé fell 8% in five days after Q2-25 and suffered a 9% consensus cut after the 3Q24 miss. Options in July priced an 11.3% chance of the stock falling below CHF 60.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D3632nd percentile

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Nestlé has no recent record of upgrading guidance.

  • In FY24 it cut twice: from about 4% to about 3% (July), then to about 2% (October).
  • FY25 guidance was qualitative ("improving") and met.
  • FY26 guidance was narrowed, not raised. The margin phasing was trimmed (H2 flat on H1).
  • The only upgrades have been cost-savings targets: Fuel for Growth from CHF 2.5bn to 3.0bn; FY26 savings to CHF 2.0bn after FY25's CHF 1.1bn against CHF 0.7bn.

Navratil's behaviour so far is to hold guidance and beat it on the top line. Raising it is not his pattern. This is consistent with an "honest tracker" posture after the credibility damage of 2024. It means upside will show up as beats rather than guidance raises.

  • Archetype. The Change Forces report classifies Nestlé as a "reactive tracker": late-year downward revisions; delivery on revised rather than initial targets; an over-promising bias during macro inflections.
  • Margin targets were walked down repeatedly. 17.5–18.5% (2017), then an 18.5% ambition, then "at or above 16%" for 2025 at the November 2024 CMD, then 17%+ medium term.
  • Wisdom Library contrast: "Sandbagging Precision Management Archetype". Nestlé is not a sandbagger. Its guidance has been accurate at best and optimistic at worst.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

D4544th percentile

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement:

  • One year: a clean run of beats on the top line and cash, with lower-quality beats on margin.
  • Three years: mixed to poor, including one major guidance failure (FY24).
  • Five years: poor on margin and EPS against what was promised.

In the comparable environment (the 2022–23 inflation shock and its unwind), Nestlé protected nominal growth through price but lost volume and then margin. It performed worse than Unilever in the recovery phase. The current beat streak starts from a deliberately low bar and is helped by easy comparisons.

  • Last 12 months.
  • Q4-25: organic 4.0% against 3.4% consensus; RIG 1.3% against 0.9–1.0%, though flattered by about 20bp of pre-buying.
  • Q1-26: 3.5% against 2.2–2.4%; RIG 1.2% against 0.1%.

  • H1-26: margin 16.4% against 16.2% consensus, but ex-pension it was a miss; EPS CHF 2.22 against 2.16.
  • Q3-26: 4.0% against 3.3%; RIG 2.8% against 1.5%; pricing 1.1% against 1.8% (a miss); sales 1.2% ahead.

  • FY23: 7.2% against 7–8% guidance; missed the 7.5% consensus midpoint.

  • FY25: met or beat (3.5% against 3.4%).
  • Five years.

  • EPS fell from 5.10 to 3.94 (FactSet adjusted, FY19–25).
  • A prolonged three-year downgrade cycle (Redburn).
