P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D4431st percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The forecasts deserve different weights by line, because Nestlé's record differs sharply by line:

Line Weight Lean Why
Organic growth High Slightly below management's 4%+ ambition, to about 3.6% to 3.8% The steadiest variable in the case
Savings High Upward The best-delivered promise
Free cash flow Moderately high Upward for FY26 only —
Margin Moderate for FY26–FY27; low for 17%+ by "the medium term" Neutral to slightly up against consensus for FY27; down against management's 17%+ ambition for FY28 onward Margin promises have been missed for nine years
Reported revenue and EPS Low Down against consensus Currency has been negative every year and perimeter changes are mis-modelled

A clear piece of evidence for leaning EPS down comes from consensus itself. Out-year consensus has decayed every year: FY27 EPS from a high of CHF 5.28 to 4.74 today (-10%), FY28 from 5.62 to 5.02 (-11%), FY26 from 4.91 to 4.51 (-8%). A forecast that has fallen 8% to 11% from its peak without a recession is the signature of a structural drag (the franc and perimeter) that analysts add back to their models each year and remove again later.

The record, by line.

  • Organic growth (high weight).
  • The quarterly organic range was 3.5% to 4.0% across four quarters (Q4-25 to Q3-26), with four consecutive beats against consensus: Q4-25 4.0% against 3.4%; Q1-26 3.5% against 2.2% to 2.4%; Q2-26 3.7% against 3.5%; Q3-26 4.0% against 3.3%.
  • The one large failure, FY24 (guided about 4%, delivered 2.2%), came under the previous CEO and in a pricing-led regime.
  • Diversification across 185 countries keeps aggregate organic growth stable even when categories swing.
  • I lean slightly below management because the 4%+ ambition needs pricing of about 2%, which the commodity cycle will not provide in 2027 (P-04).
  • Volume (moderately high weight). RIG of at least 2% looks achievable for FY27 (Q3-26 2.8%; a mechanical recall tailwind of about 0.9pp in Q1-27). Former insiders call sub-2% RIG "the long-term norm", and FY23 to FY25 delivered -0.3% to 0.8%. I put 2.4% for FY27 and 2.2% to 2.3% thereafter, and would lean down if Q1-27 RIG, excluding the recall lap, falls below 1.5%.
  • Margin (moderate to low weight).
  • The record of missed targets: 17.5% to 18.5% (set 2017; reached 17.7% in 2020 and then lost); the 18.5% ambition (reset at the November 2024 CMD); "at or above 16%" for 2025 (met at 16.1%).
  • Smaller recent signals: H2-26 guidance was softened to "broadly similar" to H1, and the H1-26 margin beat contained an undisclosed 30bp pension credit.
  • Lean: I sit 20bp above consensus for FY27 (16.6% against 16.4%) because the commodity window, the savings and the low-margin exits coincide in that year. I reach 17.0% only in FY29, not "the medium term". Management's target is a direction, not a forecast.
  • Savings (high weight; lean up). FY25 delivered CHF 1.1bn against CHF 0.7bn planned. The 2016–2020 programme delivered CHF 2.8bn against CHF 1.8bn first promised. A further programme is plausible ("no ceiling").
  • FCF (moderately high weight; lean up for FY26).
  • FY25 came in at CHF 9.15bn against guidance of "at least CHF 8.0bn" and the sell-side's CHF 8.3bn. H1-26 FCF rose 46% to CHF 3.38bn. H2-25 alone delivered CHF 6.8bn.
  • FY26 FCF of about CHF 9.9bn looks more likely than consensus's CHF 9.38bn.
  • The lean reverses for FY27 to FY28, because the perimeter removes cash-generating businesses (Russia, Waters).
  • Reported EPS (low weight; lean down). FactSet adjusted diluted EPS fell from CHF 5.10 (FY19) to CHF 3.94 (FY25) despite a 10.7% fall in the share count. Currency was negative every year from 2023 to 2025. The consensus decay pattern is set out above.

The adjustment the Consumer should apply. For the third set's predictability judgement, treat the FY27 organic growth and RIG forecasts as narrow-cone, high-confidence numbers. Treat the FY27 margin as medium-confidence, depending on one dated event (H1-27). Treat reported revenue and EPS as wide-cone and biased low against consensus, because the bias comes from known mechanics (Russia, perimeter, the franc, finance costs), not from operating uncertainty.

Forecasts and upgrades

7

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

D3215th percentile

Memo topic 6, Forecast financials: the Rater's score

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement30C-4927P-0847P-1028
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F3015th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nestlé's financial forecasts for the next 18 months to four years describe an operating recovery that is genuine but lodged inside a shrinking, currency-eroded, cash-constrained wrapper. The operating engine is credible:

  • volume-led organic growth of about 3.6% to 3.8%;
  • CHF 3.0bn of gross savings, of which about a quarter reaches the margin;
  • coffee relief in 2027;
  • petcare capacity;
  • a margin rising about 20bp a year to 17.0% by FY29.

That is close to what consensus implies at constant currency.

Three mechanical forces mean reported revenue falls in FY27 and FY28 and underlying EPS is flat at about CHF 4.53 to 4.59, against consensus's CHF 4.72 and 5.02:

  • the forced exit of a high-margin Russia;
  • the deliberate exit of about 6% of low-margin sales;
  • a franc that drifts stronger.

Financial leverage, which helped EPS through buybacks until 2024, now helps not at all. The dividend absorbs about 90% of FCF after leases, net finance costs are flat rather than falling, and the deleveraging from disposals is a one-time step to about 2.5x that does not release buyback capacity.

The feedback loops make FY28, not FY27, the year of greatest uncertainty. In FY28 the volume-reinvestment loop must run without commodity relief, after the savings programme ends, and while stranded costs surface.

For the Consumer, the predictability judgement should separate the narrow cone (organic growth, RIG, savings delivery, FY26 FCF) from the wide one (reported EPS, the FY28 margin, the franc). The direction of expected consensus revisions over the next 18 months is down for FY27 to FY28 reported EPS and revenue. The most likely triggers are the FY27 guidance and the scope disclosures on 18 February 2027, and the first full half of Russia's absence in H1-27. That is not because the business disappoints, but because the perimeter and the franc are not yet fully modelled.

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

D4747th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Over FY19 to FY25 the items below operating profit were a net drag of about CHF 0.30 a share from rising interest, offset by about CHF 0.50 from buybacks. Over FY26 to FY30 they are close to neutral. Interest is flat at about CHF 1.4bn to 1.5bn, the share count is flat until a token buyback in FY29, the underlying tax rate is stable at about 22%, and associates (chiefly L'Oréal) grow about 3% a year. Underlying EPS growth will therefore equal operating profit growth plus about 0.3pp from associates.

Three below-the-line issues matter for the Consumer:

  1. Consensus finance costs. Consensus assumes net finance costs fall by about CHF 0.56bn by FY28, worth about CHF 0.17 a share. I find support for only about CHF 0.07bn (P-02), a gap of about CHF 0.12 a share (-2.5%) in FY28.
  2. Associates are large and non-cash. Associates less minorities are about CHF 1.33bn in FY26 (about 11% of underlying net income and CHF 0.52 a share). Only about CHF 0.8bn to 0.9bn arrives as dividends, so underlying cash conversion is lower than the headline suggests.
  3. Reported earnings will be very weak in FY26 and recover in FY27. The reported-to-underlying gap is extreme in FY26: - the CHF 1.3bn held-for-sale write-down (H1); - the Russia write-off of CHF 1.6bn, plus a possible CHF 1.4bn non-cash recycling of currency translation losses, which may overlap; - about CHF 1.6bn of restructuring; - a reported tax rate of 28.7% in H1.

Reported net income for FY26 is likely to be about CHF 5bn to 7bn (reported EPS about CHF 2.0 to 2.8), against CHF 9.0bn (CHF 3.51) in FY25 and CHF 4.55 underlying. That is the lowest reported figure in the seven years on file, and a headline risk at the 18 February 2027 results. It is mostly non-cash.

Each item.

  • Tax.

  • Associates and joint ventures.

  • Base case: about CHF 1.33bn (FY26) rising to about CHF 1.49bn (FY30), net of minorities. That is L'Oréal's earnings growth of about 4% to 5%, less euro translation into francs, plus about CHF 0.05bn a year from Peranel.

  • Disagreement on Peranel: Redburn treats Peranel as EPS-neutral in its first year: CHF 300m of operating income lost, offset by lower interest and associate income. I find the offsets smaller:

    • the 50% share of a leveraged joint venture's profit is about CHF 0.05bn.

The CFO: no excess cash for buybacks until leverage reaches the middle of the range. Notably, the CHF 20bn programme was executed at about CHF 107 a share, 22% above today's price, which is a lesson in buying back at a high multiple with borrowed money.

    • restructuring cash fades after FY27.
  • What does not change: about 87% to 92% of FCF after leases goes to the dividend.
  • The supplier-finance risk: reverse factoring of CHF 6.21bn (7% of sales) has little room to grow further. If it shrank, it would reverse FCF by billions in a year.

Versus the last four to six years, in one line. EPS was previously helped by buybacks and hurt by rising interest. It will now be helped by neither. Net income will grow only with operating profit and L'Oréal. FCF will stay at about CHF 9bn to 10.5bn, as it was, while the business shrinks by about 6% of sales.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F2812th percentile

Judgement: My base case is a successful but unspectacular volume-led turnaround:

  • RIG of about 2.2% to 2.4% a year, pricing of about 1% to 1.5%, organic growth of about 3.6% to 3.8%;
  • the UTOP margin rising from 16.1% (FY25) to 16.35% (FY26), 16.6% (FY27), 16.8% (FY28), 17.0% (FY29) and 17.1% (FY30);

The base case is about 1% above consensus EPS for FY26, 4% below for FY27 and 9% below for FY28. Almost all of the gap is Russia, the franc and finance costs. The base case rests on three foundations, each tested at a dated event:

  1. FY27 organic guidance and Q4-26 RIG at FY26 results on 18 February 2027. These test the volume loop.
  2. The H1-27 margin at the results in late July 2027. It must show UTOP of about 16.5% with no pension credit, which tests net savings and coffee relief.
  3. The FY27 results in February 2028. These test whether the margin holds as commodity relief ends and stranded costs surface.

The operational drivers.

C-53How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?C5660th percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D6034th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: There is real scope beyond cost cuts, concentrated in three or four big areas. All are congruent with strategy, and peer precedents show the outcome is achievable within two to three years:

  1. Coffee margin recovery as the 2024–25 bean spike unwinds.
  2. Portfolio mix from the exits.
  3. Petcare and growth-platform volume on new capacity.
  4. Working capital and capex discipline feeding FCF.

Coffee is the swing factor. It explains most of the FY25 margin fall and could restore much of it.

  • Coffee. Coffee, including Nespresso (about CHF 6.6bn of sales), is about CHF 25–26bn, or 29% of sales. FactSet's Powdered & Liquid Beverages line was CHF 25.1bn in FY25.
  • Margins fell about 540bp (P&L Beverages ex-Nespresso, about CHF 19bn) and about 360bp (Nespresso) in 2025.
  • That is roughly CHF 1.2–1.3bn of lost profit, about 140bp of group margin (my estimate).
  • Recovering half of it as hedges roll onto lower bean prices would add about 60–70bp to group margin. That is comparable to, or larger than, net Fuel for Growth savings left after reinvestment, but exposed to retailer give-backs and to bean prices rising again in H2-27.
  • Mix.
  • Waters out: +30bp (Redburn).
  • VMS and ice cream out: low-margin, accretive.
  • Growth platforms (30% of sales) growing high single digit.
  • Russia out: dilutive (about 4% of profit on 2% of sales).
  • Net mix: about +20–40bp by FY28.
  • Volume. RIG of 2% plus would add about 0.5–1% a year to EBIT through operating leverage.
  • Cash. Working-capital release as coffee inventory reprices; capex kept below 5%.
  • Peer precedents. Unilever lifted margin from 19.4% to 20.0% in a year while volume grew 4.2% (H1-26). Danone moved from 12.2% to 13.4% over FY22–25 (about 40bp a year) with volume turning positive.
  • Model implication. A margin path of about 16.4% (FY26), 16.7–17.0% (FY27) and 17.0–17.3% (FY28) is achievable: above consensus in FY27 and in line with Jefferies' 17.0%. It needs coffee recovery and net savings to coincide, with no commodity relapse before H2-27.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D5444th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: The core growth engine has not decelerated in total over the last twelve months. Organic growth ran 3.5–4.0% each quarter (Q4-25 4.0%, Q1-26 3.5%, Q2-26 3.7%, Q3-26 4.0%). That is slightly below Nestlé's own "normal" of 4%+ and well below the inflation-era 7–8%. The real risk is in the composition:

  • The input force that carried nominal growth (pricing) is slowing fast: 2.8%, then 2.1%, then 1.1%.
  • The replacement force (volume) is accelerating: 0.8%, then 1.5%, then 2.8%.

If pricing reaches zero or turns negative in 2027 (commodity give-backs) before RIG settles above 2.5%, organic growth falls toward 2.5–3%, below normal.

  • By category.
  • Coffee decelerated in Q3 after 9.3% (Q1) and 7.5% (H1); DB says coffee and nutrition slowed sequentially. Nespresso grew 2.5% in Q3 against 4.3% in H1.
  • Petcare accelerated from 2.7% (H1) to about 5% (Q3).
  • Nutrition is recovering from the recall.
  • Waters spiked to 12.3% on weather and leaves the group in 2027.
  • By zone. Europe 1.2% in Q3 with pricing at 0.2%. AOA 6.4%. Americas 3.3%.
  • Signals to watch.
  • Q4-26 (reported 18 February 2027) laps H2-25 RIG of about 1.4%, a tougher comparison than H1's 0.2%.
  • The Change Forces report's warning thresholds: RIG below 1.2% (bear), US PetCare below 3%, Nespresso margin below 19.5%.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

F3615th percentile

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: Consensus forecasts a clean break from the last five years. Six years of falling CHF revenue and EPS give way to:

  • modest revenue growth;
  • margin recovery back to FY23–24 levels by FY27–28;
  • accelerating EPS growth;
  • falling debt.

The margin and EPS path is credible, even modest, given commodity relief, savings and mix. Three parts of the build are not credible as they stand:

  • reported revenue is perimeter-inconsistent and probably too high for FY27–28;
  • the fall in costs between EBIT and pre-tax profit (about CHF 0.56bn over FY26–28) is unexplained;
  • the implied currency path is not stated.

The EPS outcome is close to right. The route to it is wrong in places, so the Consumer should rebuild it line by line.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F2711th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The next four years look like a quieter and smaller version of the last six:

  • Faster volume, slower price. RIG of about 2.3% against an average of about 1.5% to 2% over FY19 to FY25. Pricing of about 1.3% against about 3%.
  • Lower organic growth. About 3.7% against about 5.1%, because pricing was inflated in FY21 to FY23.
  • Reported revenue about flat. About +0.5% a year from FY25 to FY30, against -0.6% a year from FY19 to FY25. The shrinking perimeter and the franc consume almost all organic growth until FY29.
  • The margin returns to the FY22–FY24 band (17.1% to 17.3%) only by FY29–FY30.
  • Underlying EPS grows about 2% a year (CHF 4.42 to 4.92, FY25 to FY30). It is flat in FY27 to FY28 and accelerates only once the perimeter stops shrinking. The FY23 level of CHF 4.80 is regained only in FY30.
  • FCF is flat at about CHF 10bn.
  • Leverage falls from 2.85x to about 2.4x.

The net impact is positive on quality and negative on growth. The business that remains in FY28 is smaller, more volume-led, higher-margin on its continuing perimeter (16.85% after the exits in FY27), more concentrated in coffee, petcare and nutrition, and less exposed to water and Russia. It is not a business whose reported earnings grow faster than inflation before FY29.

What the Consumer should take from this. The difference between my numbers and consensus does not lie in the operating engine. My constant-currency, constant-perimeter EPS growth averages about 4.5% a year: about 5% in FY27 before the pension credit, about 3.5% in FY28 when commodity costs turn, and about 5% in FY29 to FY30. That is close to consensus's reported growth of 4.5% to 6.3%. It lies in three mechanical items that consensus appears to have left in:

That makes the predictability of the next 18 months a question about the accounting and the franc, not about Nestlé's ability to sell more coffee and cat food.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

F2623rd percentile

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F3220th percentile

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Our assessment is not more favourable than what guidance, estimates and the narrative already reflect. On operations it is about the same, and slightly better for FY26 cash flow and the FY27 margin. On reported earnings it is clearly worse: 4% below consensus for FY27 and 9% for FY28. On valuation it is worse again, because the multiple the market believes it is paying (17.6 times) is 19.3 times on our earnings. The positives are fully known and priced at a 52-week high. The negatives are known in pieces, are not in the numbers, and will enter them through estimates, not through guidance. The gap is therefore unfavourable in size. It is more benign in kind than a normal gap of this size: it is mechanical, largely exogenous and already visible to the careful half of the sell side, and the market has so far looked through such items while RIG holds.

The three channels together.

Channel What it reflects now Our assessment The gap When it closes
Guidance FY26: organic growth of 3% to 4%, margin above 16.1%, cash flow above CHF 9bn. Medium term: 4%+ growth, 17%+ margin FY26 met, cash flow beaten. FY27 guidance in line with the base case. Medium term late on margin and short on growth Small, slightly unfavourable. No conservatism to release 18 February 2027
Sell-side estimates FY27 and FY28 EPS of CHF 4.72 and 5.02; revenue rising; finance costs falling CHF 4.53 and 4.59; revenue falling; finance costs flat Unfavourable: -4% and -9% About three-quarters of FY27 by April 2027; FY28 through 2027 and February 2028
Narrative Volume-led turnaround; margin recovery next; L'Oréal as an option The same engine, inside a shrinking, currency-eroded, cash-constrained wrapper Unfavourable: what is missing from the narrative is negative Once the company restates its base; the franc part only if the franc moves

Timing: the next six months, and months 6 to 18.

  • Next six months. The February 2027 results are the most favourable print of the window (H2-26 EPS up about 8%, gross margin up about 220bp, cash flow above guidance, FY27 guidance in line). They are also the trigger for the re-basing of FY27 revenue, EBIT and EPS and for the first full view of reported earnings (about CHF 5.5bn to 6bn). Q1-27 sales in April will carry a mechanical recall lap. On balance this period can support the narrative even as EPS estimates fall.
  • Months 6 to 18. The prints weaken (H1-27 EPS about flat, H2-27 down 1% to 2%), reported revenue falls about 5% in H2-27, the commodity relief fades and FY28 guidance arrives. If estimates have not finished falling by then, this is where the gap is most likely to matter for the shares.

What the gap means for the return the model shows. The model gives +16.2% to its 18-month column (31 December 2027): +7.2% from dividends, +4.5% from the multiple and +4.3% from earnings.

  • Dividends (+7.2%). Two payments, CHF 3.15 and CHF 3.20. The second is paid in April 2028, after the column's date but inside 18 months from today. This is the most dependable part. It is covered about 1.08 times by free cash flow after leases on the cross-check, so it is safe but leaves nothing over.
  • Earnings (+4.3%). Two-year-forward EPS moves from about CHF 4.56 today to CHF 4.76 (FY29). The gain comes from the perimeter ceasing to shrink in FY29, not from the two years in between, which are flat.
  • The multiple (+4.5%). From 19.3 times to the guideline 20.17 times, on our earnings. The ten-year median is 19.5 times.
  • Three readings of the same forecast. If the market pays today's screen multiple of 17.6 times on our FY29 EPS, the price is about CHF 84 and the return about +2%. If it pays today's multiple on our earnings, 19.3 times, the price is about CHF 92 and the return about +12%. At the guideline multiple the price is about CHF 96 and the return +16%. On today's consensus for FY29 (CHF 5.28, three brokers only), CHF 96 would be 18.2 times.
  • What has to be true for the guideline return. Consensus EPS falls by about 8% to 9% toward ours while the share price rises by about 9%. Put in the terms an investor sees, the multiple on consensus rises from 17.6 times to about 20 times through a downgrade cycle. The evidence for it: in 2026 the multiple rose about 15% while FY27 estimates fell 2.3%, and the shares rose on 22 October 2026. The evidence against: the last six months' re-rating was the sector's move times Nestlé's beta, not Nestlé's own (D-07); the multiple's five-year range runs from 24.9 times down to 14.9 times, and the low came in mid-2025 at the end of a three-year downgrade cycle; and the first set's precedent that a re-rating which runs ahead of earnings needs upgrades to follow.
  • My view of the fair multiple. The guideline sets it and the memo judges it. My steer is that the stock is turning up in operations while its estimates have not yet stopped falling, which by the guideline's own logic argues for the lower part of the historical range, and that 20.17 times on our earnings is above the ten-year median for a business whose EPS is flat for two years. I would treat the multiple's +4.5% as the least dependable third of the return, and I would present the return as about +12% without it.

Where the stock is in its lifecycle, on this evidence. Operations: turning up. Narrative and share price: turning up and beginning to broaden, on volume alone. Estimates: still bottoming, with one more mechanical leg down. Guidance: stable. The combination is unusual. It is neither a clean early-stage case, where estimates rise, nor a stalling one, because the engine is improving. The most favourable reading is that the estimate reset is brief (February to April 2027) and is followed by a period in which constant-perimeter growth of about 5% becomes visible and, from FY29, shows in reported EPS.

What would turn the gap favourable. A franc that weakens; commodity relief that is largely kept through the European round, shown by an H1-27 margin near 17%; a second savings programme with a stated net benefit; any step on capital returns, which today means L'Oréal. None is in the base case, and only the second and third are in management's hands.

For the Consumer. State plainly that our forecasts are below consensus, say why in the three mechanical terms, and separate that from the judgement on the business, which is positive. Do not describe the shares as trading at their five-year median multiple without adding that this rests on consensus EPS we expect to fall. Take free cash flow and leverage from the second set's cross-check and debt path, not from the model's lines. Use 18 February 2027 as the first test of both halves of the case: the engine (Q4 RIG, H2-26 gross margin) and the wrapper (scope, FY27 guidance, the re-collected consensus).

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

D4537th percentile

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: Guidance is most likely to be met at each of the six events and unlikely to be raised. FY26 guidance is close to certain to be met, with free cash flow the one line likely to be beaten. FY27 guidance, to be given on 18 February 2027, is most likely to be set where the base case already is: organic growth of about 3% to 4%, a margin that improves, and free cash flow above CHF 9bn. Nestlé's pattern under this management is to set a range around the run-rate, hold it, narrow it at mid-year and beat it on sales. The larger point is structural. Nestlé guides in measures that our three mechanical drags barely touch: organic growth excludes currency and perimeter, the margin percentage is raised by the exits, and only free cash flow shows them. Guidance can therefore be met all the way through a period in which reported EPS is flat and consensus EPS falls. The risk of a reduction is concentrated late in the window: the softening of second-half margin language in July 2027, and FY28 guidance in February 2028. The medium-term ambition (4%+ organic growth, RIG of at least 2%, a 17%+ margin) is likely to be kept and not met within three years on organic growth, and met late on margin.

Each event.

Event What is tested Raised or beaten Met or held Reduced or missed Comment
18 February 2027: FY26 against guidance Organic growth of 3% to 4%; margin above 16.1%; cash flow above CHF 9bn Cash flow above consensus (CHF 9.4bn to 9.6bn): about 65% All three met: about 90% Any one missed: about 10% Base case: 3.7%, 16.35%, CHF 9.85bn
18 February 2027: new FY27 guidance against expectations Organic growth, margin direction, cash flow, scope Better than expected: about 15% In line: about 60% Worse: about 25% See below

April 2028: Q1-28 sales Reaffirmation About 5% About 85% About 10%

What FY27 guidance is likely to say, and why it will not alarm.

  • Organic growth. Most likely "3% to 4%" or similar, RIG-led. The base case is 3.6% and my lean is 3.3% to 3.4%; consensus is about 3.2%. A range of 3% to 4% would sit at or slightly above what brokers expect.
  • Margin. Most likely "further improvement". Consensus has the FY27 margin flat at 16.4%. The base case has 16.6%, and the exits alone add about 11bp net of Russia in FY27. A statement of improvement would be at or above consensus.
  • Free cash flow. Most likely "above CHF 9bn" again. The base case is CHF 9.66bn on the company's definition. Consensus is CHF 10.2bn (FactSet) and CHF 10.06bn (company-compiled). This is the one guided line on which the perimeter shows: Russia gave about 3% to 4% of group cash flow, and Jefferies puts the cost of the Waters and vitamins exits at about CHF 250m a year. A repeat of "above CHF 9bn" would sit about 10% below consensus.
  • Scope. The company will have to state how it treats Russia, Waters, vitamins and ice cream in its guidance and in the consensus it compiles. This disclosure, more than the guidance figures, is what will move estimates (I-02).
  • Peers set a low bar for 2027. Kraft Heinz has indicated flat organic sales and lower operating income. General Mills guides operating profit down 8% to 13%. Lindt cut its 2026 growth guidance from 4% to 6% to 0% to 2%. Unilever raised its 2026 outlook. A Nestlé guide of 3% to 4% with an improving margin would be among the firmer statements in food.

What would drive each outcome.

  • A raise or a beat. Q4-26 and Q1-27 RIG of 2.5% or more with Europe stable; an H2-26 gross margin near or above 47%, showing that relief is larger or better kept than assumed; the European round settled without delistings; a second savings programme. Navratil has said there is "no ceiling" on savings and has raised only cost targets so far. I put the chance of a new or extended programme at about 35% in February 2027 and about 55% by February 2028. It would be the most likely form of any "raise".
  • Met. The base case: RIG near 2%, pricing near 1%, about half the relief kept, savings on plan.
  • A reduction. European pricing below -1% with delistings that last beyond a quarter; a commodity rebound reaching the P&L in H2-27; execution slippage as most of the white-collar cuts land in 2027; a second food-safety event. The franc does not reduce guidance as given, but it reduces what guidance is worth in reported terms.

Nestlé's record and what it implies. FY24 guidance was cut twice. FY25 was met. FY26 was narrowed, not raised, and its margin phasing was softened. The only targets raised have been savings. AlphaSense's review (18 November 2026) concludes that the company is "more likely to meet than to cut or raise". The practical consequence is that upside appears as beats on quarterly sales, not as higher guidance, and that the half-year results are when wording changes.

The medium term (18 months to three years).

Target Raised Kept Reduced or re-expressed Delivered on the base case
Margin of 17%+ About 10% (most plausibly restated for the new perimeter with a date, at a capital markets day) About 75% About 15% 17.0% in FY29. About 25% chance by FY28
Organic growth of 4%+ Under 5% About 75% About 20% 3.6% to 3.8%. About 15% to 20% chance of 4% or more in FY27
RIG of at least 2% Under 5% About 85% About 10% 2.2% to 2.4%. About 60% chance in FY27
Leverage toward the middle of 2.0x to 3.0x About 90% About 2.5x at end-FY27. About 75% chance
Free cash flow above CHF 9bn About 20% About 75% About 5% CHF 9.7bn to 10.6bn
Fuel for Growth of CHF 3.0bn by end-2027 About 55% by February 2028 About 40% About 5% Delivered in full
  • The exits make the margin ambition easier without any operating gain. The three low-margin exits add about 52bp and Russia takes about 25bp. A margin of 17% on the new perimeter is therefore equivalent to about 16.75% on the old one. Management could restate the ambition upward for the new perimeter and present it as a raise. I would read that as arithmetic.
  • The organic growth ambition is the one least supported. It needs pricing of about 2% or RIG of about 3%. The base case has neither.

How our assessment compares with what guidance reflects. For FY26 our assessment matches guidance, and is better on cash flow. For FY27 we expect guidance in line with the base case. For the medium term our forecasts are below the ambition on growth and later on margin. No guidance speaks to reported EPS, which is where we differ most from consensus. The gap between our assessment and guidance is therefore small and slightly unfavourable: there is no conservatism to be released, and there is a medium-term ambition we do not expect to be met on time.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

F2517th percentile

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: Consensus for FY27 and FY28 is likely to be cut on revenue, operating profit and EPS, held or nudged up for FY26, and raised on the margin percentage. I put the chance that FY27 consensus EPS is lower in six months than today at about 75% to 80%, and that FY28 is lower in 18 months at about 80%. My central expectations are FY27 EPS moving from CHF 4.72 to about CHF 4.60 by mid-2027, and FY28 from CHF 5.02 to about CHF 4.75 to 4.85 by early 2028. Net breadth will be negative, and it will be concentrated in two bursts: late January to April 2027, when brokers re-base for Russia and the exits, and around February 2028, when FY28 guidance meets a consensus that has the margin rising 30bp. The cause is not that the business will disappoint. It is that the consensus median still rests on brokers who have not removed Russia or the exits, and that the removal is arithmetic on figures the company has published. I do not expect consensus to fall all the way to the base case within 18 months: about 40% of our FY28 gap is near-certain arithmetic, and the rest depends on the franc drifting stronger and on finance costs that brokers revise slowly.

Where consensus stands, and how far it is from the base case.

  • The spread of brokers is the key fact for FY27. Seven houses are already at CHF 4.55 to 4.66 (Redburn, Morgan Stanley, Barclays, Bernstein, Santander, Jefferies and one unnamed). The other twelve that FactSet shows are at CHF 4.69 to 4.92. The low group includes the houses known to have rebuilt for Russia. The median of CHF 4.72 sits in the high group. As the high group rebuilds, the median moves toward the low group without any new information.
  • Other measures of "consensus" are higher still. The company-compiled consensus of 9 October 2026 has FY27 EPS of CHF 4.80, and Visible Alpha has CHF 4.80 and CHF 5.08 for FY27 and FY28 (cited by Redburn, 23 October 2026). Results are judged against the company-compiled figure. It will be collected again before the February results, and that collection is the first point at which every contributing broker must state a view on Russia.

  • For FY27, about three-quarters of the gap is near-certain. That gives the central expectation of about CHF 4.60, with our higher margin offsetting the currency and finance-cost parts if H1-27 confirms it.
  • For FY28, about 40% is near-certain, about 30% depends on the franc and up to about 30% on finance costs. At a flat franc I expect consensus of about CHF 4.80 to 4.85. With the franc 2% to 3% stronger, about CHF 4.70.
  • The base case for FY28 (CHF 4.59) is 3.6% below the lowest broker. I regard it as a low-end central estimate. If the franc holds at spot, a figure of about CHF 4.65 to 4.70 is equally defensible. The direction of consensus is not in doubt; the end-point is.

The net breadth of revisions.

  • Today. FY27 revisions over one, three, six and twelve months are -1%, -1%, +1% and -2% for EPS; -1%, -1%, -1% and -3% for EBITDA; and -1%, -1%, 0% and -3% for sales. Target prices are up 1% and the share of buy ratings is up 8 points to 44%. The dashboard's revision score is -1 and its sell-side momentum rating is D. This is a consensus that is drifting down slowly while opinion improves.
  • Late January to April 2027. I expect the score to turn more negative and the rating to stay at D or fall to F, as most brokers re-base revenue and EBIT together. Margin percentages will be revised up at the same time. AlphaSense's guidance report (18 November 2026) puts it the same way: reported revenue revisions skew lower while margin revisions skew higher, and the two should not be read as a change in demand.
  • July 2027. If the H1-27 margin prints at 16.5% or more against a consensus that may then be about 16.4% to 16.5% for the year, operating profit could see small upgrades. This is the one point in the window where breadth could turn positive on profit.
  • February 2028. The risk returns for FY28: commodity costs rising, stranded costs, the end of the savings programme and a second year of currency.
  • Target prices and ratings may keep rising through all of this. They did in 2026, on volume. Breadth of EPS revisions and breadth of opinion can diverge for several quarters.

What would make consensus rise instead. A franc 5% weaker (about +4%); RIG of about 3% with most of the commodity relief kept, taking the FY27 margin to about 17% (about +3%); a second savings programme with a stated net benefit; a sale of L'Oréal shares used for buybacks. I put the chance that FY27 consensus EPS is higher in twelve months than today at about 15%.

The record. Out-year consensus for Nestlé has fallen 8% to 11% from its peak in each of the last three forecast years (FY26 from CHF 4.91 to 4.51; FY27 from CHF 5.28 to 4.74; FY28 from CHF 5.62 to 5.02). Redburn calls it a lengthy downgrade cycle. The base case implies the cycle has one more leg, of a different kind: earlier legs came from volume and margin disappointments; this one comes from perimeter and currency.

Eighteen months to three years. Consensus is likely to be cut. FY28 revenue of CHF 93.9bn is 6.7% above the base case and cannot survive the perimeter. FY29 EPS of CHF 5.28 rests on three brokers and is about 10% above the base case's CHF 4.76. The longer-dated numbers also carry the flat-currency convention that Redburn criticises. The offset further out is that the perimeter stops shrinking in FY29, so growth rates recover (EPS +3.8% in FY29 on the base case) even though levels stay below today's consensus.

Does it matter for the shares? On 22 October 2026 Morgan Stanley cut FY27 EPS by 4% for Russia and the shares rose on the RIG print. Bernstein wrote that the negative revisions would be "more than offset by positive volume growth sentiment". One broker's cut is not the same as the median falling 2.5% to 5%, but the evidence so far is that mechanical downgrades are looked through while RIG holds. The danger is the coincidence of a weak RIG print with the re-basing, most plausibly at the February 2027 results if Q4 RIG is below about 1.5%.

C6666th percentileMemo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0744
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4441st percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: At present the share price moves with its peers to an unusual degree, and the re-rating of the last six months was almost wholly a sector move amplified by Nestlé's high sensitivity to it. Over the latest six-month window the peer basket explains 47% of Nestlé's daily price variance, against an average of 28% over the last 24 months and a low of 10%. Nestlé's beta to the basket is 1.26, and it reached 1.40 in October 2026, the highest of the period. Nestlé rose 10.1% in the window and the basket 7.6%. A beta of 1.26 on 7.6% is 9.6%. Only about half a point was Nestlé's own. Consensus EPS barely moved over the same months, so the change in price was a change in multiple, and the same split applies to the multiple. The level of the multiple against its own history is also an industry fact: Nestlé sits at the 23rd percentile of its ten-year range and the median food producer in the dashboard sits at the 30th. The multiple is therefore company-driven over a year, during which Nestlé clearly beat its large branded peers, but industry-driven over the last six months. Further re-rating would need either the sector to keep rising or company-specific upgrades, and I expect downgrades.

The evidence (co-movement table, computed 17 November 2026; prices only; the basket is 21 European food producers, equal-weighted).

Measure Latest six months 24-month range of month-end readings 24-month average
Correlation with the basket 0.69 0.32 to 0.70 About 0.5
Beta to the basket 1.26 0.68 to 1.40 About 1.0
Share of variance explained by the basket 47% 10% to 48% 28%
Idiosyncratic share 53% 52% to 90% 72%
  • The pattern over time. The sector's share falls to 10% to 20% in periods dominated by company events and rises above 40% when a common factor dominates. It was 14% to 15% from November 2025 to February 2026, the months of the change of chief executive, the announcement of 16,000 job cuts and the infant formula recall. It was 45% in August 2025 and has been 38% to 48% since July 2026.
  • The closest individual peers are the large and Swiss names: Danone (correlation 0.60), Associated British Foods (0.54), Barry Callebaut and Premier Foods (0.53), Orkla (0.52), Lindt and Cranswick (0.50). The pattern suggests that the common factor is the market's appetite for defensive, bond-like European consumer shares more than anything specific to food. The first set made the same point from the other direction: the sector trades at 18-year relative lows because of bond yields near 5%.
  • The beta. A beta above one to a basket of smaller companies is not what a large, diversified defensive would normally show. I read it as the combination the first set described: Nestlé is the most leveraged large staple, and it is the liquid vehicle through which investors buy or sell the sector (about USD 0.5bn to 0.6bn traded a day). It rose more than the basket in a rising sector and should fall more in a falling one. A 10% fall in the basket would imply about 12% to 13% for Nestlé before any company news.

The multiple.

  • Level against history: industry-driven. Nestlé's multiple on consensus (17.6 times) is at the 23rd percentile of its ten-year range. The 21 dashboard peers with data have a median percentile of 30, with Orkla at 30, Associated British Foods at 26, Danone at 48 and Lindt at zero. Nestlé's apparent cheapness against its own history is shared by the industry and is explained by the discount rate, not by Nestlé.
  • Change over twelve months: company-driven. Nestlé returned +12.4% over twelve months while Danone returned -7.1%, Lindt -29.0% and Associated British Foods -4.9%. Consensus FY27 EPS fell 2.3% over the same period, so the multiple rose by about 15%, from about 15.3 times to 17.6 times. Against the large branded peers that is Nestlé's own re-rating, earned by four top-line beats.
  • Change over the last six months: industry-driven. As set out above, the sector move and Nestlé's beta explain about 9.6 of the 10.1 points.
  • Position within the last two years. The multiple is at the 84th percentile of the last two years (median 16.4 times). On the base case's EPS it is 19.3 times.

What this means for predictability.

  • About half of the share price's movement over the next six months will come from a factor our forecasts do not address. The sector factor is driven by bond yields and by the rotation between defensive and cyclical shares. The first set recorded that defensive leadership was fading. With a beta of about 1.3, Nestlé is now among the more exposed members of its sector to that rotation, not a refuge from it.
  • The company-specific half is where our differences from consensus will act. Downgrades to EPS for the perimeter and the franc, and the quarterly RIG prints, will be Nestlé's own news. The record of October 2026 is that RIG outweighed a 4% cut to one broker's FY27 EPS on the day.
  • The basket is imperfect. It is equal-weighted and mostly small and mid-sized producers; Unilever, Mondelez and other global staples are not in it. A capitalisation-weighted basket of global staples might explain more. The direction of the conclusion (a rising sector share and a rising beta since mid-2026) does not depend on the basket.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

A6492nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The evidence points to five repeatable drivers of staples outperformance. Nestlé currently fits four of them, the first time in years:

  1. Volume-led growth with a rising A&P ratio. DB finds about a 500bp EPS premium for staples that raise marketing-to-sales.
  2. Portfolio carve-outs and simplification. AlphaSense calls them "most reliable" over 12–24 months: Unilever, Kerry, Henkel.
  3. Margin recovery as commodity costs fall. "A commodity's fall reaches a processor first, as cash, and a brand later, as margin."
  4. Self-help cost programmes that over-deliver. Tesco's Save to Invest; Unilever's EUR 800m.
  5. Buying a de-rated sector at a trough multiple. European staples sit at 18-year relative lows.

Nestlé fits drivers 1 to 4. On driver 5 it is no longer cheap: it is at its five-year median P/E and at its 52-week high.

The underperformance patterns are the mirror image, and Nestlé lived all of them in 2022–25:

  • price-led growth with negative volume (FY23 RIG -0.3%);
  • serial lowering of guidance and targets (FY24 cut from 4% to 2%; margin from 18.5% to 16%);
  • leverage in a rising-rate regime;
  • write-downs on acquisitions bought at the top (Bountiful, Aimmune);

  • for CHF reporters, currency drag (Lindt, Nestlé).

  • Share-price precedent.

  • Nestlé fell 8% in five days after Q2-25, rose about 17.7% in the six months to July 2026, and is now at 87.72, at its 52-week high.
  • The 24-month-forward P/E re-rated from about 16.5x (mid-2025, near a ten-year low) to 17.6x without EPS upgrades: FY27 EPS is -2.3% over 12 months.
  • Precedent (Wisdom Library: "Earnings Upgrade Cycle — Consensus-Gap Quantification") says a sustained re-rating needs upgrades to follow. "Turnarounds Take Longer Than You Think" warns that turnaround re-ratings often run ahead of earnings.
  • Peer precedents.
  • Unilever 2023–26. New CEO, power brands, volume-led growth (4.2% H1-26 volume), margin from 19.4% to 20.0%, carve-outs, EUR 6bn buybacks. It is the template Nestlé is following, but Nestlé lacks the buyback.
  • Danone 2022–25 ("Renew"). Margin from 12.2% to 13.4%, volume turned positive, guidance corridor met for three years; but then its China engine slowed and FCF missed.
  • Both show that a staples turnaround can rebuild volume within two to three years. Margin rebuilds slowly, at about 30–50bp a year.
  • Fit to the leveraged-defensive pattern. "The Market Chooses Its Defensives, and the Leveraged One De-Rates First" (Wisdom Library, November 2026). At about 3x net debt to EBITDA, Nestlé is the most leveraged large European staple. In a risk-off market with yields near 5%, it is the defensive most exposed to a rates-led de-rating.
  • Model implication. Use Unilever and Danone as the base rate: RIG rebuilt to about 2% within two years, margin about +30–50bp a year, EPS growth of 5–7% at constant currency. That is roughly what consensus already embeds for FY27–28.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B+6485th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: A fair trough multiple for Nestlé is about 14.5–15x 24-month-forward earnings. That is where the stock bottomed in mid-2025, near a ten-year low, at the end of a three-year downgrade cycle.

The realistic 12-month downside to a fair trough is about 15–25%, against upside of about 10–15% to about 20x (Jefferies' re-rating case: about CHF 99–100). The skew is roughly balanced to slightly negative at today's price.

  • Multiple history.
  • Five-year 24-month-forward P/E: range 14.9–24.9x, median 17.7x, 10th percentile 15.7x, 25th percentile 16.5x.
  • Ten-year dashboard bands: 10th percentile 16.4x ("crash"), median 19.5x.
  • Today 17.6x, the 48th percentile on five years and the 23rd on ten.
  • Morgan Stanley's bear case assumes a 20% discount to staples. Jefferies' downside also assumes margin progress above 17% is delayed.
  • Floor arguments.
  • The 3.5% dividend yield (CHF 3.10).
  • Ex-L'Oréal, the P/E is about 1.5 turns lower (14.5x when the headline was 16x; Jefferies, 2025).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4335th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market narrative has moved from "broken staple" (mid-2025, 24-month-forward P/E near 16.5x, ten-year low) to "turnaround showing volume traction" (now 17.6x, at the 52-week high, Jefferies Franchise Pick). It moved on volume evidence alone.

The next leg needs margin and capital-return evidence, and that evidence is dated:

  • FY26 results on 18 February 2027 provide guidance;
  • H1-27 results in July 2027 provide the first margin test;
  • FY27 results in February 2028 provide the leverage and buyback question.

Because the share price already discounts part of the volume recovery while estimates have not risen, the staircase must deliver upgrades. Margin above consensus in FY27 is the most plausible source. Without upgrades the re-rating stalls.

For the second and third sets:

  • the 18-month predictability question hinges on the H1-27 margin print;
  • the 18-to-36-month narrative hinges on whether net savings and coffee recovery survive a commodity re-inflation in H2-27, and on whether falling leverage reopens buybacks.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–64

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The two sets are highly congruent on how the business works and on the direction of every operating line. They jar on one large matter and several small ones. The large one is whether consensus EPS is "a sensible central case" (first set) or 4% to 9% too high for FY27 to FY28 (second set). I side with the second set on direction and on FY27, and with less confidence on the size for FY28. The small ones are arithmetic and definitional. They change no conclusion and move FY27 EPS by less than 1%. Evidence gathered after both sets were written supports the second set's FY27 numbers: seven broking houses already sit at CHF 4.55 to 4.66 for FY27. It leaves the second set alone for FY28, 3.6% below the lowest broker.

The causal chain on which both sets agree, and which holds together. Each link is supported in both sets and none contradicts another.

  1. Consolidated retailers, not rival brands, are the binding constraint (C-31, C-33). Nestlé's pricing power is therefore cost pass-through with a lag, not real pricing power (C-17, C-45, P-04).
  2. Volume must therefore be bought with price restraint and advertising (C-12, P-05). The money comes from Fuel for Growth, so most of the CHF 3.0bn is spent and not kept (C-77, P-03).
  3. The margin therefore rebuilds slowly, at about 20bp to 40bp a year, and the 17%+ ambition is late (C-13, C-71, P-01, P-06).
  4. The dividend absorbs about 90% of free cash flow after leases and leverage is about 3x. There are therefore no buybacks, and EPS grows only with operating profit and L'Oréal (C-19, C-64, P-02, P-08).
  5. With no financial lever, the mechanical items decide reported EPS: Russia, the exits, the franc and finance costs (C-04, C-07, C-15, P-09).

The first set's guidance to the modeller (build at constant currency, overlay the franc, remove Russia, segment the protected core from the contested middle, withhold credit for a margin above 16.5% to 16.8% until H1-27) was followed in the second set almost line by line. That is strong congruence of method.

Where the sets jar, and how I resolve each.

Matter First set Second set My resolution Effect
Consensus EPS "Sensible central case"; weaknesses "mechanical, not directional" (Sell-side group) 4% below consensus for FY27, 9% for FY28 Second set on direction. The mechanical items all point one way. FY27 is corroborated by brokers; FY28 is not The largest issue; see I-02
Net portfolio mix +20bp to +40bp by FY28 (C-78) "About zero to +10bp" First set. Arithmetic gives +27bp FY27 margin +10bp; FY28 unchanged
FY27 and FY28 margin 16.7% to 17.0%, then 17.0% to 17.3% 16.6%, then 16.8% Second set, with FY27 nearer 16.7%. The first set did not net the pension credit or Russia Under 1% of EPS
Net savings "CHF 1.5bn to 2bn net" (Jefferies) About CHF 0.7bn visible in margin Both can hold. Jefferies' figure is net of reinvestment; the second set's is also net of ordinary cost inflation. The second set's is the one that reaches EPS None
Cash conversion 80% to 87% of underlying net income 75% to 77% Second set. It deducts leases and the non-cash part of associate income None on EPS
Width of the volume cone Bear case of about 1% RIG "as plausible as" a bull case of about 3% (C-32) "The narrowest cone"; 1.5% to 3.0% Between the two. I use 1.6% to 3.0% for FY27 including the recall lap D-04
Russia's charges "Possible CHF 1bn cash write-off" CHF 1.6bn plus CHF 1.4bn, "may overlap" Company disclosure: distinct, non-cash, plus CHF 0.9bn on net debt Reported FY26 only
Length of commodity cover 6 to 12 months, and 6 to 18 months 9 to 12 months Unresolved. Sources give 3 to 6 months of cover (Maybank on Nestlé Malaysia, October 2026), a nine-month lag to the P&L (Jefferies, January 2025) and 9 to 12 months (Morgan Stanley). I use about nine months from spot to P&L Moves the margin window by up to half a year (D-06)
The H1-27 margin as the catalyst "The most important novel step": a margin inflection above consensus's +10bp (C-22) H1-27 UTOP of 16.5% against a reported 16.4% in H1-26 The step exists underneath (+40bp excluding the pension credit) but the headline will read +10bp. The catalyst is weaker as a narrative event than the first set implies I-03
Valuation 17.6 times, "at its five-year median" Not addressed 19.3 times on base-case EPS The fourth set as a whole

The looseness inside the second set's margin bridge. The bridge from 16.1% (FY25) to 16.8% (FY28) is not additive, and the Consumer should not quote its lines as measurements.

  • The three low-margin exits are entered at +32bp. On the second set's own margins they are worth +52bp.
  • Advertising is entered at -30bp, but the rise from 8.6% to about 9.2% of sales is 60bp, and P-03 says the net savings figure is already after "CHF 0.3bn to 0.5bn" that "funds the rise in A&P". Part of the advertising cost is therefore counted twice or half is missing, depending on the reading.
  • "Net savings and operating leverage" of +82bp is the balancing figure. It equals the +80bp of net savings and leaves nothing for the 10bp to 15bp a year of operating leverage that P-01 expects.

I rebuilt the path year by year from the second set's annual components with the correct perimeter arithmetic. FY27 comes to about 16.7% (net savings +30bp, commodity relief +35bp, pension credit -15bp, perimeter +11bp, advertising -10bp, and stranded costs, currency and depreciation together -14bp). FY28 comes to about 16.8% (net savings +25bp, commodity relapse -15bp, perimeter +24bp, advertising -10bp, and stranded costs, currency and depreciation together -17bp). The FY28 margin of 16.8% therefore survives as a judgement. Each component carries an error of 10bp to 20bp, so the margin is known to about ±30bp, not to the 5bp the bridge suggests.

Why I change no input. I tested five candidate changes against the base case.

Candidate change Evidence Effect on FY27 EPS
Russia's base of CHF 2.0bn of sales and CHF 0.53bn of UTOP, not CHF 1.8bn and CHF 0.49bn Company, nine months 2026 About -0.25%; revenue growth -2.5%, not -2.3%
The low-margin exits worth about 10bp more in FY27 Arithmetic on the second set's own margins; Jefferies About +0.6%
Organic growth of about 3.3% to 3.4%, not 3.6% European scanner volume of -3.2% at the start of Q4-26 (Bernstein, 27 October 2026); consensus organic growth of about 3.2% About -0.3%
Finance cost about CHF 0.05bn lower The chief financial officer called Russian interest income "not material" (October 2026) About +0.3%
Associate income slightly lower Froneri re-leveraged to about 6 times net debt to EBITDA; Morgan Stanley trimmed associate estimates About -0.2%
Net About +0.1% to +0.2%

The net effect is far inside the cone in D-04. Changing five inputs to move EPS by CHF 0.01 would add noise, not accuracy. I therefore leave the model as it is and carry these points as skews.

Congruence with the evidence gathered since.

  • Supports the second set. AlphaSense's report on the concentration of drivers (18 November 2026) calls financing costs "the clearest tension with consensus" and finds that Morgan Stanley believes consensus has not integrated the timing of the Waters and vitamins closings. Brokers who have rebuilt their models are already near the base case for FY27: Rothschild & Co Redburn CHF 4.56, Morgan Stanley CHF 4.60, Barclays CHF 4.61, Bernstein and Santander CHF 4.63, Jefferies CHF 4.66 and one unnamed broker CHF 4.55.
  • Does not yet support the second set. For FY28 the lowest brokers are Redburn (CHF 4.76), Morgan Stanley (CHF 4.79) and Santander (CHF 4.80). The base case is CHF 4.59. The low brokers grow FY28 EPS by 4% to 4.4%; the base case grows it 1.3%, because it takes a commodity relapse, a currency drift of -1.5% and flat finance costs together.
  • Cuts against both sets on the operating engine. AlphaSense's precedent report (18 November 2026) gives its highest confidence to a margin that plateaus at or just above 16% with RIG of 1.5% to 1.8% once commodity inflation returns, and only medium-high confidence to RIG above 2% with a margin of about 16.4% to 16.5%. The base case (underlying RIG of about 2.05% in FY27 excluding the recall lap, margin of 16.6%) is 10bp to 30bp and 0.25 to 0.5 points above that reading. On operations the base case is therefore slightly above consensus (organic growth 3.6% against about 3.2%; margin 16.6% against 16.4%) and on the optimistic side of the precedents, while on EPS it is below every broker. Both facts hold at once, and the memo should state both.

One further internal consistency that strengthens the second set. The base case's pricing of 1.2% to 1.5% a year comes almost wholly from emerging markets (pricing of about 3% on 41% of sales). Emerging-market pricing exists because of emerging-market inflation, which over time shows up as currency depreciation against the franc. A forecast that includes this pricing and a translation drag of 1% to 1.5% a year is coherent. A forecast that includes the pricing and assumes a flat currency after two years, which is how Redburn describes consensus (October 2024), counts the same inflation as growth and omits its cost.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F3010th percentile

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The narrative is complete on what is going well and thin on what will hold reported earnings back. The positive dynamics in our assessment (the volume recovery, the new chief executive's programme, petcare capacity, gross savings) are articulated by every house and are the reason the shares have re-rated. The negative mechanical dynamics are articulated in pieces by one or two houses each and are not joined up or carried into the consensus numbers: Russia's full-year cost, the fall in reported revenue, the franc beyond two years, finance costs, and the sequence in which retailers claim commodity relief before costs rise again. A few dynamics are almost absent: the dependence on supplier finance, the trough in reported earnings in FY26, and the fact that the multiple on rebuilt earnings is 19.3 times. On investor commentary I have little direct evidence. No source in my files reports social-media or buy-side commentary, and AlphaSense's narrative report reviewed four of about twenty covering houses. What the share price, positioning and ratings show is consistent with the sell-side picture: investors own the volume story and have not priced the wrapper.

Each dynamic, and how well known it is (sell-side evidence from AlphaSense's narrative report of 18 November 2026 unless stated).

Dynamic in our assessment Direction for the case Sell side How well known
Volume recovery; RIG of 1.2%, 1.8%, 2.8% Positive All houses; central since Q1-26 Fully
Turnaround under Navratil; gross savings of CHF 3.0bn Positive All houses Fully
Petcare capacity and wet-cat share gains Positive Jefferies ("Feline Better"), Morgan Stanley, Bernstein Fully
Dividend absorbs free cash flow; no buyback before 2028 Negative Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Redburn; central to the bear case Well
European retailer power and give-back demands Negative Morgan Stanley (12 November 2026) prominently; Redburn disputes the size Well, and contested
L'Oréal as an option Seen as positive Jefferies, Redburn, Morgan Stanley Well as an idea. Less known: a sale used for debt or buybacks is dilutive by about 5.5% (Morgan Stanley) to 7% (Jefferies), and management called itself a "happy holder" in February 2026
Russia's full-year cost to EPS Negative Sized at 1% (Redburn) to 4% (Morgan Stanley, Jefferies) Known as an event; not agreed in size; not in the median

The pension credit in H1-26 Negative Morgan Stanley, Redburn Partly. Brokers do not show consistently whether FY27 bases exclude it
Fall in reported revenue in FY27 and FY28 Negative No house gives a year-by-year path; commentary stresses the margin gain Poorly
The franc beyond a two-year horizon Negative Redburn alone (about -2% a year); Deutsche Bank treats currency as a near-term tailwind Poorly
The double squeeze: price conceded in H1-27, costs rising in H2-27 and 2028 Negative Each half appears separately Poorly

Supplier finance of about 7% of sales Tail risk Jefferies alone (August 2025) Hardly
The order of half-year EPS prints (+8%, 0%, -1% to -2%) Negative after February 2027 Not found Not articulated
The multiple on rebuilt EPS (19.3 times against 17.6 times) Negative Bernstein and Redburn say the valuation is full; none frames it this way Not articulated
FY26 cash flow above consensus; H2-26 gross margin up about 220bp Positive Not found as a forecast Partly. The only positives in our assessment not yet in the narrative

The pattern in the table. Rank the dynamics by how well they are known and the positives come first and the negatives last, with two exceptions at the bottom that are near-term positives. What is known is what Nestlé reports each quarter (RIG). What is not known is what must be assembled from several disclosures: the perimeter needs four transactions and their dates; the franc needs a view beyond the convention; the double squeeze needs the retailer note and the commodity note read together. The under-articulation is a consequence of complexity, not of concealment, and for that reason it will not persist once the company restates its base.

Investor commentary and positioning.

  • Direct evidence is missing. My files hold no report of social-media or buy-side commentary. I have requested one (research requests). The Consumer should treat what follows as inference.
  • What the price says. The shares are at a 52-week high (CHF 88.01 on 16 November 2026), 10.8% above the 200-day average, and have re-rated while FY27 EPS estimates fell 2.3% over twelve months. They rose on the day Russia's removal was announced. Investors are pricing the RIG trend.
  • What positioning says. Short interest is close to zero and hedge-fund net exposure was 0.2% (first set). There is no visible group of investors positioned for the negative dynamics. The mean target price (CHF 88.6) equals the share price, 44% of brokers rate the shares a buy, and Jefferies made Nestlé a franchise pick on 20 October 2026.
  • What the co-movement says. About half of recent daily price movement is the sector's (D-07). Part of the investor narrative is therefore about defensive shares and bond yields, not about Nestlé.

Eighteen months to three years.

  • Known. The 17%+ margin ambition and the debate about whether it arrives in FY27 (Jefferies) or much later (Morgan Stanley); GLP-1 and ultra-processed-food risk (Bernstein, 16 November 2026); the absence of buybacks until about 2028.
  • Not known. That leverage reaches about 2.5x in FY27 and then stalls, because the dividend takes the cash, so that buybacks from FY29 are token. That stranded costs surface as transitional agreements expire in 2028. That FY28 is the year the volume strategy must fund itself without commodity relief. That reported revenue does not regain its FY25 level until FY29 on the base case.

The H1-27 margin as a narrative event. The first set called a margin inflection at H1-27 the most important novel step. It is known to the sell side as the next test. What is not appreciated is how it will look: about 16.5% against a reported 16.4%, because the comparison includes the pension credit and Russia. An underlying gain of about 40bp will read as 10bp. The narrative benefit will depend on analysts making the adjustment, and the record on the pension credit is that they do, but only after the print.
