Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 16 November 2026, 14:17 to 14:43 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH): Set 1 answers, foundational components of the investment case (written 2026-11-16)

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's note: what the sources cover, and the data traps to avoid when building the model

  • Period coverage (the caution in the brief, checked). The "latest" Earnings Review does not cover the half to June 2026. It covers the third-quarter and nine-month sales update of 22 October 2026 (quarter to 30 September 2026). It is a sales-only print, with no profit, margin or cash figures. The "earlier" Earnings Review, labelled 2025 quarter 4, actually covers the full-year 2025 results (19 February 2026), the first-quarter 2026 sales (23 April 2026) and the first-half 2026 results (23 July 2026). FactSet's last reported period is therefore H1 2026, and the latest top-line evidence (Q3 2026) runs one quarter beyond it. The next results are FY2026 on 18 February 2027.
  • First-quarter 2026 figures conflict. Most sources give organic growth of 3.5%, real internal growth (RIG, i.e. volume and mix) of 1.2% and pricing of 2.3%. One table in the latest review gives 5.1%, 2.0% and 3.1%. I use 3.5% / 1.2% / 2.3%, which several independent sources repeat.
  • FactSet "free cash flow" in the financials block is after dividends. The block's line (for example CHF 3,528m in FY2025) equals operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex less cash dividends (15,904 − 4,527 − 7,849). The peer table's "FCF / net income" history (0.2x to 0.4x) inherits that basis, while its consensus columns (0.9x) are before dividends. Do not read the step from 0.4x to 0.9x as a cash-conversion improvement. The company's own FCF was CHF 10.67bn (FY24) and CHF 9.15bn (FY25).
  • Consensus revenue for FY2027 to FY2028 is not on one perimeter. Broker FY2027 revenue runs from CHF 84.5bn (Santander) to CHF 94.3bn. Some brokers have taken out Waters (50:50 Peranel JV, closing H1 2027), Russia (deconsolidated from end-September 2026, about 2% of sales), mainstream vitamins (sold to Yellow Wood, USD 1.2bn of sales) and the remaining ice cream (about CHF 1bn of sales, to Froneri). Others have not. The removals add up to about CHF 5–6bn, roughly 6% of sales. So the CHF 91.6bn median almost certainly overstates reported FY2027 revenue. EPS is affected much less, because these businesses carry low margins (Russia excepted).
  • Leverage figures differ by definition. Company net debt to EBITDA was 2.85x at end-FY25. FactSet's trailing figure, which includes leases, was 3.22x at June 2026. Morgan Stanley cites 3.1x at June 2026. Company net debt was CHF 56.3bn at June 2026; FactSet's was CHF 56.7bn.
  • EPS bases. The brokers'-basis EPS base for FY2025 is CHF 4.42. Reported basic EPS was CHF 3.51 and FactSet adjusted diluted EPS was CHF 3.94. Consensus growth must be measured from 4.42.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH). The world's largest branded food and beverage group, reporting in Swiss francs. It is organised around four core categories: Coffee (Nescafé, Nespresso, Starbucks at-home), Petcare (Purina), Nutrition (infant formula, medical nutrition, premium vitamins) and Food & Snacks (KitKat, Maggi, frozen and prepared foods). It reports through three geographic zones (Americas; Europe; Asia, Oceania and Africa) plus Nespresso and Waters & Premium Beverages, which is being moved into the Peranel joint venture.

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Unilever (ULVR-GB): the closest precedent of a large staples group running a productivity programme alongside volume-led reinvestment and portfolio separations.
  • Danone (BN-FR): the "Renew" cost and reinvestment programme in a similar food and nutrition mix.
  • Mondelez International: cocoa-exposed snacking with productivity funding reinvestment.
  • Kraft Heinz: the precedent of deep cost cutting followed by volume and brand erosion.
  • JDE Peet's: a coffee-focused cost base under the same green coffee cycle.
  • Lindt & Sprüngli: a Swiss-franc cost base with premium confectionery.
  • Orkla (ORK-NO): decentralised European branded food with a self-help cost target.
  • Mars: private, the main petcare and snacking competitor, for any public evidence on its cost behaviour.

Inputs:

  • What share of Nestlé's cost base is fixed and what share variable? Separate factory conversion, distribution (8.4% of sales in H1 2026, company disclosure), advertising and marketing (8.9% of sales in H1 2026), and administration and R&D.
  • How has Nestlé's underlying trading operating margin moved with volume (real internal growth) in past periods? Cover 2008–2010, 2015–2017, 2020–2021 and 2022–2025, with dated figures, and separate volume effects from commodity and currency effects.
  • Fuel for Growth: CHF 3.0bn of gross savings targeted by end-2027; CHF 1.1bn delivered in 2025; CHF 1.7bn cumulative by H1 2026 (company disclosures).
  • How much is being reinvested in advertising, price and growth platforms, and how much reaches operating margin?
  • What do management, auditors or the sell-side say about net savings?
  • The 16,000-role reduction (12,000 white-collar, 4,000 factory) targets CHF 1.0bn of annual white-collar savings by end-2027 at roughly CHF 2.0bn of cash cost (company, October 2025 and February 2026). What is the phasing of savings and cash cost by year? What do precedents at peers show about delivery against such targets and about disruption to sales?
  • Earlier Nestlé structural cost programmes (2016–2020): what was promised, what was delivered, and over what time?
  • What stranded overheads and dis-synergies are expected from deconsolidating Waters into the Peranel joint venture (H1 2027), selling mainstream vitamins to Yellow Wood Partners and transferring the remaining ice cream to Froneri? What does the Unilever ice cream and Foods separation precedent show?
  • What profit and interest income does Nestlé lose from deconsolidating Russia from end-September 2026, as disclosed or estimated by brokers?
  • Which cost lines proved cuttable at Nestlé and peers (procurement, shared services, factory footprint, marketing non-working spend, SKU count), which did not, and why?
  • How do depreciation and amortisation evolve as capex falls below 5% of sales (company guidance)?

Focus: How much of the CHF 3.0bn gross Fuel for Growth programme is likely to reach Nestlé's operating margin after reinvestment, stranded costs and the Russia loss, and by when. Also, how strongly does Nestlé's margin respond to a recovery in volume, judged on its own history and peer precedents?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • JDE Peet's: coffee pricing and hedging through the same bean cycle.
  • Keurig Dr Pepper: single-serve coffee systems and pod pricing against compatibles.
  • Lindt & Sprüngli: premium chocolate price rises and later retail price cuts in Germany.
  • Mondelez International: cocoa pass-through and price-pack architecture.
  • Danone (BN-FR): infant and medical nutrition pricing and creamers.
  • Unilever (ULVR-GB): volume-led recovery after over-pricing, including price resets in Thailand and Brazil.
  • Mars and General Mills (Blue Buffalo): US petcare pricing.
  • Private label suppliers and retailers such as Tesco (TSCO-GB), Aldi and Lidl: how own-label prices move against brands.

Operating conditions:

  • Falling coffee and cocoa costs, with European retailers demanding immediate price give-backs while Nestlé's hedges still carry older, higher costs.
  • Sharp coffee and cocoa cost inflation, such as 2024–2025.
  • Weak developed-market consumers with private label premiumising and discounters expanding.
  • Strong Swiss franc appreciation.
  • Rising GLP-1 weight-loss drug adoption in the US and UK.
  • Emerging-market inflation and currency devaluation.

Inputs:

  • Nestlé's pricing contribution by year and quarter from 2019 to Q3 2026, by zone and by category, against real internal growth. Company figures include pricing of 2.8% in 2025, 2.1% in H1 2026 and 1.1% in Q3 2026, with Zone Europe at 0.2% in Q3 2026.
  • Evidence of price increases held, and of price given back, in past periods of falling input costs, for example 2009–2010 and 2015–2016 in coffee and dairy. How long did the give-back take?
  • Coffee price elasticity: the reported 4% volume and mix decline after about 8% Starbucks at-home price rises (Jefferies, 2026). How does elasticity differ between Nescafé soluble in emerging markets, Nespresso, and Starbucks at-home?
  • Nespresso: the share of Original-machine owners' capsule consumption now taken by compatible capsules; Vertuo pricing and patent protection; direct-to-consumer pricing.
  • Hedging and cover: how long Nestlé's coffee and cocoa cover runs against private label and smaller competitors, and how that gap affected relative shelf prices in past deflation phases.
  • European retailer delistings in price negotiations (for example Intermarché, early 2026): frequency, duration and the eventual price outcome for Nestlé and peers.
  • US petcare pricing (reported by Jefferies as flat after double-digit increases at peak inflation) and the premium held by Pro Plan, wet cat and therapeutic diets against private label.
  • Infant formula and medical nutrition pricing in China and Europe after the 2026 cereulide recall, and the effect of volume-based procurement on enteral nutrition.
  • Trade spend and promotion intensity as a share of sales, and what retailers, competitors and the sell-side say about Nestlé's pricing now.

Focus: Over the next 18 months to four years, will Nestlé be able to keep pricing positive as coffee and cocoa costs fall and retailers demand give-backs, and how much of its commodity relief is likely to be passed to retailers and shoppers? Which of its categories have shown real pricing power in past cycles?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Nestlé's earnings path depends on whether a reinforcing loop outruns two balancing loops. The reinforcing loop: price restraint, then volume, then cost absorption, then savings reinvested in brands, then share. The balancing loops: commodity deflation and retailer give-backs; Swiss franc pressure and leverage limiting reinvestment.

Peers:

  • Unilever (ULVR-GB): the volume-led reinvestment loop after over-pricing.
  • Danone (BN-FR): the Renew productivity-to-reinvestment loop and its China slowdown.
  • Mondelez International: reinvesting gross margin gains in advertising.
  • Kraft Heinz: the loop running in reverse after deep cost cuts.
  • Lindt & Sprüngli: a premium price loop and its volume ceiling.
  • Orkla (ORK-NO): price-led growth reversing into volume declines against a concentrated retail triopoly.

Inputs:

  • The volume loop: as pricing falls and advertising rises (8.6% of sales in 2025 and 8.9% in H1 2026, company), does volume (real internal growth of 1.2%, 1.8% and 2.8% in Q1, Q2 and Q3 2026, company) keep rising? Does higher volume absorb fixed costs enough to fund further brand investment? What evidence shows this loop at Nestlé now, and at peers in past recoveries?
  • Brand concentration: media-supported brands are being cut from over 400 to about 135–150 (company). Does concentrated spend lift the share of priority brands while the tail declines? What happened at Unilever with its power brands?
  • The retailer give-back loop: falling commodity costs lead to retailer demands, then delistings or price cuts, then lower brand margin, then less reinvestment.
  • The currency loop: Swiss franc strength reduces reported earnings, which pressures pricing or reinvestment, which costs volume. Does this loop show in Nestlé's history, for example 2015 and 2022–2025?
  • The balance-sheet loop: net debt to EBITDA near the top of the 2.0x–3.0x target range (company) and a dividend absorbing most free cash flow limit buybacks and reinvestment, which slows growth and the multiple. Is it easing as disposal proceeds (Peranel CHF 2.8bn expected in H1 2027, company) arrive?
  • The organisational loop: does the 16,000-role reduction, alongside a performance-culture push, create talent loss and execution drag that slows volume recovery? What evidence is there at Nestlé and at peers' restructurings?
  • The capacity loop in petcare: new US wet cat capacity (Batavia, Ohio, 2026), then share gains, then a scale cost advantage.

Focus: Over the next 18 months to four years, which of these loops is likely to dominate Nestlé's revenue, margin and cash flow? What dated evidence or event would show that the volume-reinvestment loop is stalling or reversing?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Consensus EPS growth for Nestlé depends heavily on items below operating profit and on perimeter changes that brokers model inconsistently. The second set needs the inputs to the EPS bridge established separately from the operating forecast.

Peers:

  • Unilever (ULVR-GB): separations, stranded costs, buybacks and EPS bridges after disposals.
  • Danone (BN-FR): currency translation and associate and joint-venture accounting.
  • Lindt & Sprüngli: a Swiss-franc reporter's translation and transaction effects.
  • Orkla (ORK-NO): earnings dependence on an equity-accounted associate (Jotun) and currency translation.
  • Tesco (TSCO-GB): working-capital and supplier-finance effects on free cash flow.

Inputs:

  • Currency. The Swiss franc translation effect on Nestlé's reported sales, operating profit and EPS: company disclosures of -5.7% on 2025 sales, -6.2% in H1 2026 and -3.7% for the nine months to September 2026, and the company sensitivity that a 5% franc appreciation costs 10–15 basis points of margin. What currency paths do brokers assume for FY2026–FY2028, and how do they reconcile constant-currency and reported EPS?
  • Perimeter. The sales, operating profit and EPS effects of:
  • Waters & Premium Beverages into the Peranel 50:50 joint venture (expected H1 2027; CHF 2.8bn net proceeds per the company);
  • the sale of mainstream vitamins to Yellow Wood Partners (announced September 2026);
  • the transfer of remaining ice cream to Froneri;
  • the deconsolidation of Russia from end-September 2026;

  • the yfood acquisition. Which brokers include each in their FY2027 and FY2028 forecasts?

  • Net finance costs. Average cost and fixed share of debt (company disclosure: average cost of net debt about 2.6%, about 80% fixed); the refinancing schedule; the loss of interest income on Russian cash. How do brokers model the gap between EBIT and pre-tax profit for FY2026–FY2028, and why does it narrow in their forecasts?

  • Associates and joint ventures. Treatment of Nestlé's roughly 20% stake in L'Oréal and of Froneri, Cereal Partners and Peranel in underlying EPS. Do brokers include associate income consistently in net income and EPS?
  • Tax rate history and guidance; minority interests (for example Nestlé India).
  • One-offs. The H1 2026 non-cash pension credit (about 30 basis points, as reported by Morgan Stanley and Rothschild & Co Redburn) and whether it recurs. Expected restructuring and impairment charges through 2027. A possible Russia write-off.
  • Share count. The buyback was completed in 2024 with none since. What leverage level or event would management need to restart buybacks?
  • Free cash flow and working capital. The size and stability of supplier-finance (reverse factoring) programmes, and the cash effect of falling coffee and cocoa prices on inventories.

Focus: Which below-operating-profit, currency and perimeter inputs will decide whether Nestlé's reported EPS grows in line with consensus over FY2026–FY2028? Where do management guidance and the brokers' assumptions on these inputs differ most?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 17 November 2026, 17:26 to 17:49 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH): Set 2 answers, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years (written 2026-11-17)

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's note: the conventions used, the headline conclusion, and where I depart from the first set

Basis used throughout. Unless stated, "margin" means Nestlé's underlying trading operating profit (UTOP) margin. UTOP is the company's headline profit measure and is the same basis as the brokers' EBIT consensus (FY26 consensus EBIT CHF 14.74bn = 16.3% of sales, against company guidance of about 16.4%). "EPS" means underlying EPS, which equals the brokers' basis (CHF 4.80, 4.77 and 4.42 for FY23 to FY25). Underlying EPS includes Nestlé's share of the results of associates and joint ventures, chiefly the 20.1% stake in L'Oréal, net of minority interests. "FX" means currency translation on reported Swiss franc figures. Years are calendar fiscal years.

The headline conclusion, which the Consumer should test first. Nestlé's operating engine will do roughly what consensus expects. The wrapper around it will not. Consensus expects underlying EPS to grow +2.1%, +4.5% and +6.3% in FY26 to FY28, to CHF 4.51, 4.72 and 5.02. My base case is CHF 4.55, 4.53 and 4.59, then 4.76 (FY29) and 4.92 (FY30). FY26 is slightly above consensus and FY27 to FY28 are flat. The gap to consensus is -4% in FY27 and -9% in FY28. Three things hold reported EPS flat in FY27 and FY28:

  • Russia leaves. It is about 2% of sales but 3% to 4% of UTOP, plus high-yield interest income on CHF 0.9bn of local cash. On a full-year basis it costs about 4% of EPS; FY27 loses the 9 months still inside FY26.
  • Currency. I assume -1.0% in FY27 and -1.5% a year thereafter, against an implicit near-zero path in most broker numbers.
  • Net finance costs do not fall. Consensus has the gap between EBIT and pre-tax profit narrowing by about CHF 0.56bn by FY28. I find no mechanism for more than about CHF 0.07bn.

At constant currency and perimeter, my EPS grows about 4.5% a year, close to consensus's reported growth. On my numbers, underlying EPS does not regain its FY23 level of CHF 4.80 until FY30. Consensus has it at CHF 5.02 by FY28.

Where I disagree with the first set, and why.

  1. Consensus EPS as a central case (first set, Sell-side group and C-67). The first set judged consensus EPS "a sensible central case" and "close to right", with weaknesses that were "mechanical, not directional". I disagree on direction for FY27 to FY28. The mechanical problems the first set found (perimeter, finance costs, the franc) all point the same way, and together they are worth 4% to 9% of EPS. Evidence that consensus has not absorbed Russia: - FY27 consensus EPS moved only -0.8% in the month after the 22 October deconsolidation announcement. - Rothschild & Co Redburn cut only 1%. Morgan Stanley cut 4%, and its FY27 EPS of CHF 4.60 sits at the bottom of the range. - Consensus FY27 revenue of CHF 91.6bn implies +1.4% reported growth. That is incompatible with removing Russia, half of Waters, mainstream vitamins and the remaining ice cream (together about 4.7% of sales in FY27).
  2. The "CHF 1.3bn inconsistency" in consensus net income (first set, C-54). It is not an inconsistency. FactSet's Model-sheet net income (CHF 10.29bn for FY26) equals consensus pre-tax profit of CHF 13.18bn taxed at about 22%, so it excludes associates. The broker-sheet net income (CHF 11.69bn) and consensus EPS include them. The difference, about CHF 1.3bn (about CHF 0.51 a share), is the share of associates and joint ventures less minorities. Consensus pre-tax profit also excludes associates, so the EBIT-to-pre-tax gap of CHF 1.56bn in FY26 is essentially net finance cost. That is why its narrowing to CHF 1.00bn by FY28 is an EPS assumption, not a definitional artefact.
  3. Portfolio mix (first set, C-15, C-78). The first set credited +30bp to +50bp of margin from the exits by FY28. Net of Russia, the mix gain is about zero to +10bp. Leaving Russia (margin about 27%) costs about 24bp. That cancels most of the +30bp from Waters (margin about 9%) and the roughly +20bp from mainstream vitamins (about 6%) and ice cream (about 8%). The low-margin exits raise the margin. The one high-margin exit, which was forced, lowers it by almost as much.
  4. The FY27 margin (first set, C-78: 16.7% to 17.0%). I put FY27 at 16.6%, up about 25bp on FY26's 16.35%. Two items that the first set did not net out consume about 30bp: the 30bp pension credit in H1-26 does not recur (about 15bp on the full year), and Russia's loss costs about 15bp in FY27.
  5. Net savings (first set, C-12 and the Internal change forces group). The first set credited net savings of about CHF 1.5bn to 2bn by 2027, following Jefferies' "net" figure. I estimate that only about CHF 0.7bn of the CHF 3.0bn gross (about 80bp of sales) will be visible as margin by FY28. The rest pays for ordinary cost inflation on the non-commodity cost base, higher advertising and marketing (A&P), and price investment (P-03).
  6. Free cash flow (first set, C-52: consensus "plausible but not conservative"). The picture splits by year: - FY26 above consensus. I expect company-defined FCF of about CHF 9.9bn, against guidance of "above CHF 9bn" and consensus of CHF 9.38bn, because H2-25 alone produced CHF 6.8bn. - FY27 and FY28 below consensus. I expect CHF 9.7bn and 9.9bn, against CHF 10.2bn and 10.7bn, because the exits remove about CHF 0.75bn of EBITDA and the working-capital release fades.
  7. The consensus EBITDA growth figure. FactSet's FY25 "brokers'-basis" EBITDA actual of CHF 17,605m (19.7%) is not on the basis of the forecasts. Consensus FY26 EBITDA (CHF 18,376m) equals consensus EBIT plus D&A, and on that basis FY25 was CHF 18,041m (20.2%: UTOP 14,408 plus D&A 3,633). Consensus FY26 EBITDA growth is therefore +1.9% like for like, not +4.4%.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Two definitions the Financial Analyst must apply, because the form has no line for associates or minorities.

  1. "Net financial items" is a composite. It is net finance cost (company basis) plus Nestlé's post-tax share of the results of associates and joint ventures (chiefly the 20.1% L'Oréal stake, plus Froneri, Cereal Partners Worldwide and, from H2-27, Peranel), less minority interests (chiefly Nestlé India).
Component (CHF m) FY26 FY27 FY28 FY29 FY30
Net finance cost -1,470 -1,450 -1,400 -1,400 -1,420
Associates less minorities +1,330 +1,360 +1,410 +1,450 +1,490
Net financial items (input) -140 -90 +10 +50 +70
  1. The tax rate of 20.0% is an effective rate on that composite pre-tax figure. It is chosen so that the tax charge equals 22% (the company's underlying rate guidance) on pre-tax profit before associates. Associate income is already post-tax.

With these two conventions the model's EPS lands on the brokers' (company underlying) basis. One distortion remains in the model's cash flow. About CHF 0.45bn to 0.55bn a year of associate income is not received in cash (L'Oréal pays out only part of its earnings). Restructuring cash (about CHF 1.0bn in FY26, 0.8bn in FY27, 0.4bn in FY28 and 0.3bn a year thereafter) sits below the EBITDA line. Both are excluded from the model's inputs, so the model's free cash flow will exceed my cross-check by about CHF 1.4bn to 1.8bn in FY26 to FY27 and about CHF 0.9bn to 1.1bn from FY28. The cross-check figure is the correct one.

Why RIG and pricing take these values:

  • RIG. Volume is recovering (1.2%, 1.8% and 2.8% in Q1 to Q3 of 2026). Petcare capacity is unblocked (Batavia, August 2026). Q1-27 laps the 90bp infant formula recall drag. Waters' heatwave-boosted base leaves.
  • Pricing. Pricing is cost pass-through with a 6 to 12 month hedge lag. In H1-27, Europe gives back part of the coffee and cocoa relief.

Margin. The EBITDA margin is UTOP plus D&A, divided by revenue. The UTOP bridge from FY25 (16.1%) to FY28 (16.8%):

Item Effect
Russia out -24bp
Waters, vitamins and ice cream out +32bp
Commodity relief net of retailer give-backs and a partial relapse in 2028 +30bp
A&P up from 8.6% to about 9.2% -30bp
Stranded costs -10bp
Currency -10bp
Net Fuel for Growth savings and operating leverage +82bp (about CHF 0.7bn of the CHF 3.0bn gross)
Total +70bp

The FY27 margin is held back by the non-recurrence of the 30bp H1-26 pension credit (about -15bp on the year) and by Russia's absence (about -15bp). FY29 to FY30 add about +15bp to +20bp a year from continued savings and growth-platform mix.

D&A. Falls in FY27 to FY28 as the capital-intensive Waters business and Russia leave, then rises about 2% to 3% a year because capex (about 4.8% to 5.0% of sales) exceeds D&A (about 4.0%).

Below the line.

  • Net finance cost is about flat at CHF 1.40bn to 1.47bn. Disposal-led deleveraging saves about CHF 0.12bn (FY27) and 0.07bn (FY28). Against that, about 46% of debt refinances over five years at about 4.0% against 2.3% (Rothschild & Co Redburn), costing about CHF 0.09bn a year, and Russian interest income on CHF 0.9bn of cash is lost.
  • Associates less minorities grow about 3% a year: L'Oréal earnings growth less euro translation, plus about CHF 0.05bn a year from the 50% Peranel stake.
  • Tax is 22% underlying throughout.

Cash.

  • Capex is held below 5% of sales (guidance).
  • Working capital falls from 6.1% of sales at end-FY25 (which carried expensive coffee inventory and settled payables) to 5.0% (FY26) and 4.8% (FY27 to FY28) as coffee and cocoa inventories reprice. It rises back to 5.0% as commodities re-inflate. I assume reverse factoring (CHF 6.21bn) is stable.
  • Lease payments are my estimate, not in the source files.
  • Acquisitions less disposals:
  • FY26: about CHF -0.2bn of bolt-ons (yfood, Hsu Fu Chi) and CHF -0.9bn of cash derecognised with Russia.
  • FY27: Peranel net proceeds of CHF 2.8bn, mainstream vitamins about CHF 0.8bn and ice cream about CHF 1.0bn, less about CHF 0.3bn of bolt-ons.
  • FY28 to FY30: bolt-ons only.
  • Buybacks. None until leverage settles at about 2.5x. Token programmes of CHF 0.5bn (FY29) and CHF 1.0bn (FY30). The dividend absorbs about 90% of free cash flow after leases.

Dividend. Progressive, at CHF 0.05 a year. FY30 rises CHF 0.10 as EPS growth resumes. The payout is about 69% to 71% of underlying EPS.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Revenue. The company, FactSet and consensus all use reported sales. Consensus revenue for FY27 to FY28 mixes perimeters: the broker range for FY27 runs from CHF 84.5bn (Santander) to CHF 94.3bn (UBS, Deutsche Bank). The median of CHF 91.6bn implies little removal of Russia, Waters, vitamins and ice cream.
  2. Operating profit. The company's UTOP is the brokers' EBIT basis (FY26 consensus 16.3%). FactSet's history line "EBIT (adjusted)" (CHF 14,267m, 15.9% in FY25) runs about CHF 0.14bn below company UTOP (CHF 14,408m, 16.1%). Restructuring and net other trading items (CHF 1.7bn in FY25; CHF 0.8bn in H1-26), impairments (CHF 0.9bn in FY25; CHF 1.3bn in H1-26) and the Russia write-off (CHF 1.6bn, plus a possible CHF 1.4bn of non-cash currency translation recycling that may overlap) sit below UTOP. They are excluded from underlying EPS.
  3. EBITDA. My EBITDA input is UTOP plus D&A, the same construction as consensus (consensus FY26 EBITDA of CHF 18,376m less D&A of CHF 3,641m equals consensus EBIT of CHF 14,744m). FactSet's FY25 "brokers'-basis" actual of CHF 17,605m (19.7%) is not on this basis. On the forecast basis FY25 was about CHF 18,041m (20.2%), so consensus FY26 EBITDA growth is about +1.9%, not +4.4%. FactSet operating EBITDA history (CHF 17,900m, 20.0%) is a third basis.
  4. Below the line. - Consensus pre-tax profit excludes associates. The EBIT-to-pre-tax gap (CHF 1,560m, 1,318m and 996m for FY26 to FY28) is net finance cost. - FactSet's Model-sheet consensus net income (CHF 10,289m for FY26) is pre-tax profit taxed at about 22%, so it excludes associates and minorities. - Broker-sheet net income (CHF 11,693m) and consensus EPS include them. The difference of about CHF 1.3bn to 1.4bn is associates less minorities.
  5. EPS.
Year Reported basic EPS FactSet adjusted diluted EPS Brokers' basis (company underlying)
FY25 CHF 3.51 CHF 3.94 CHF 4.42 (the base for consensus growth)
FY26 (my estimate) About CHF 2.0 to 2.8 — CHF 4.55

FY26 reported EPS is depressed by the held-for-sale write-down, Russia and restructuring, and the reported tax rate was 28.7% in H1-26.

  1. Free cash flow. - The company defines FCF as operating cash flow less capex and intangibles (CHF 10.67bn in FY24; CHF 9.15bn in FY25, which excluded Froneri's CHF 1.98bn extraordinary distribution). - FactSet's history line ("free cash flow", CHF 3,528m in FY25) is after dividends. - Consensus FCF (CHF 9,378m, 10,230m and 10,669m) is on the company's basis. - My cross-check below deducts lease payments as well (company basis less leases). The company-basis equivalents are CHF 9.85bn, 9.66bn, 9.87bn, 10.07bn and 10.58bn.
  2. Net debt. The company's net debt (CHF 51.4bn at end-FY25, 2.85x; CHF 56.3bn at June 2026) differs from FactSet's (CHF 56.7bn at June 2026, 3.22x on a trailing basis including leases).

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH), the world's largest branded food and beverage group, reporting in Swiss francs; four core categories (Coffee: Nescafé, Nespresso, Starbucks at-home; Petcare: Purina; Nutrition: infant formula, medical nutrition, premium vitamins; Food & Snacks: KitKat, Maggi, frozen and prepared foods) reported through three zones (Americas; Europe; Asia, Oceania and Africa) plus Nespresso and Waters & Premium Beverages, which is moving into the Peranel joint venture

Request: T-D1

Peers: Unilever (ULVR-GB), the closest precedent of a volume-led turnaround funded by productivity alongside portfolio separations; Danone (BN-FR), the Renew productivity-to-reinvestment programme in a similar food and nutrition mix; Kraft Heinz, the precedent of deep cost cuts followed by volume and brand erosion and a forced reinvestment; Mondelez International, cocoa-exposed snacking that reinvests gross margin gains in advertising; JDE Peet's, a coffee-focused cost base and pass-through through the same bean cycle; Lindt & Sprüngli, a Swiss-franc reporter whose premium pricing reached a volume ceiling; Orkla (ORK-NO), decentralised European branded food facing a concentrated retail triopoly; General Mills (Blue Buffalo) and Colgate-Palmolive (Hill's), US petcare competitors; Mars (private), the largest petcare and a major snacking competitor; Keurig Dr Pepper, single-serve coffee systems against compatibles Operating conditions: falling coffee and cocoa costs with European retailers and buying alliances demanding price give-backs; renewed agricultural commodity inflation (including an El Niño event in 2027); Swiss franc appreciation; weak developed-market consumers with premiumising private label and expanding discounters; rising GLP-1 weight-loss drug adoption in the United States and United Kingdom; emerging-market growth with currency devaluation; a large-scale white-collar and factory headcount reduction during a performance-culture change Inputs: Can a large mature food group sustain real internal growth (volume and mix) of at least 2% a year, the company's medium-term target, once mechanical tailwinds pass and pricing falls toward 1%? Nestlé's RIG was -0.3%, 0.8% and 0.8% in 2023 to 2025, and 1.2%, 1.8% and 2.8% in Q1 to Q3 2026, with Q3 helped by a heatwave in Waters and confectionery phasing (company; Deutsche Bank, 22 October 2026). Q1 2027 laps a 90bp infant formula recall drag (company). Can the underlying trading operating margin rise from 16.1% (2025) to the 17%+ medium-term target while advertising and marketing rise from 8.6% of sales (2025) to 8.9% (H1 2026) and beyond (company)? The company's record on margin targets: 17.5% to 18.5% (set 2017); an 18.5% ambition; reset to "at or above 16%" for 2025 at the November 2024 capital markets day. What share of gross productivity programmes reached operating margin at the named peers? Nestlé's Fuel for Growth targets CHF 3.0bn gross by end-2027, with CHF 1.1bn delivered in 2025 and CHF 1.7bn cumulative by H1 2026; its 2016–2020 programme delivered CHF 2.8bn against CHF 1.8bn first targeted (company). Danone reinvested 59bp of a 77bp operational improvement in 2025, and JDE Peet's reinvests 50% of its savings (company disclosures). What do peer precedents show about delivery of large headcount reductions (Nestlé: 16,000 roles, CHF 1.0bn of white-collar savings by end-2027 at about CHF 2.0bn cash cost; company) and about disruption to sales? Does new petcare capacity (Batavia, Ohio, opened August 2026, over USD 550m; company) translate into durable share and margin gains, judged on past capacity cycles at Purina, Mars and Hill's? How fully and how fast did coffee margins rebuild after past bean-price spikes? Jefferies (21 July 2026) reports 2025 margins about 360bp lower at Nespresso and about 540bp lower in other coffee than in the previous inflation cycle. Which of the company's strengths are demonstrated (procurement scale, emerging-market distribution, petcare science, Nespresso direct-to-consumer, medical nutrition) and which would it need to acquire (bargaining power with European retailers without conceding price, a performance culture, retail-media and digital capability)? Focus: Whether a large, mature branded food group starting from a reset margin, with share losses in parts of its portfolio and a new chief executive, can rebuild volume and margin at the same time from internal savings alone, without buybacks and while shrinking its perimeter; and how long successful precedents took and what separated them from failures.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Unilever (ULVR-GB), separations, stranded costs and EPS bridges after disposals; Danone (BN-FR), currency translation and associate and joint-venture accounting; Lindt & Sprüngli, a Swiss-franc reporter's translation and transaction effects; Orkla (ORK-NO), earnings dependence on an equity-accounted associate (Jotun) and portfolio simplification; Kraft Heinz, forecasts resting on cost savings; JDE Peet's, coffee cost pass-through and its effect on revenue and margin; Tesco (TSCO-GB), working-capital and supplier-finance effects on free cash flow Operating conditions: a shrinking consolidated perimeter through disposals, joint ventures and a forced deconsolidation; Swiss franc appreciation or depreciation; rising refinancing costs on maturing debt; falling then rising coffee and cocoa costs with a 6 to 12 month hedge lag; equity-accounted associate earnings moving with an unrelated sector (beauty) Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers that separate Nestlé's FY2027 to FY2028 reported revenue and EPS from FY2021 to FY2025? Test these drivers:

  • Perimeter. Russia, deconsolidated from end-September 2026 (about 2% of group sales and 3% to 4% of underlying trading operating profit; company, 22 October 2026). Waters & Premium Beverages into Peranel, with closing expected in H1 2027 and CHF 2.8bn of net proceeds (company). Mainstream vitamins to Yellow Wood Partners (USD 1.2bn of 2025 sales, USD 1.0bn price; company, 1 September 2026). The remaining ice cream to Froneri (about CHF 1bn of sales; company). Which brokers include each in FY2027 and FY2028? FactSet broker FY2027 revenue ranges from CHF 84.5bn to CHF 94.3bn (16 November 2026). Broker estimates of the Russia effect range from -1% (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 23 October 2026) to -4% (Morgan Stanley, 22 October 2026) of FY2027 EPS.
  • Currency. Translation was -7.8% (2023), -3.7% (2024), -5.7% (2025), -6.2% (H1 2026) and -3.7% (nine months 2026) on sales (company). What currency paths do brokers assume, and what is the historical distribution of Nestlé's annual translation effect on sales and EPS since 2010?
  • Net finance costs. FactSet consensus (16 November 2026) implies the gap between EBIT and pre-tax profit narrows from about CHF 1.56bn (FY2026) to about CHF 1.32bn (FY2027) and about CHF 1.00bn (FY2028). What explains this, given about 46% of debt refinancing over five years at a weighted coupon of about 4.0% against a 2.3% effective rate (Rothschild & Co Redburn)?
  • The commodity cycle. Morgan Stanley expects commodity costs to rise again in H2 2027; Jefferies (3 November 2026) expects coffee prices to fall about 20% in 2026 and about 11% in 2027.
  • Associate income. The 20.1% L'Oréal stake.
  • Net savings drop-through.

How often have forecasts for Nestlé and comparable staples resting on one or two of these drivers been met? FactSet consensus FY2027 EPS has ranged from CHF 4.65 to CHF 5.28, and FY2028 from CHF 4.95 to CHF 5.62, over its history. Focus: Which one or two drivers decide Nestlé's reported revenue and EPS in FY2027 to FY2028 once perimeter, currency and below-operating-profit items are modelled consistently, and how wide and how skewed is the realistic range of outcomes over the next 18 months.

Request: T-I1

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 18 November 2026, 10:50 to 11:18 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH): Sets 3 and 4 answers. How predictable the forecasts are over the next 18 months, and how far our assessment is already reflected in guidance, sell-side estimates and the market narrative (written 2026-11-18)

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's note: conventions, headline conclusions, the model's flagged gaps, and where I differ from the earlier sets

Conventions. As in the second set: "margin" is the underlying trading operating profit (UTOP) margin; "EPS" is underlying EPS on the brokers' basis (CHF 4.80, 4.77 and 4.42 for FY23 to FY25); "RIG" is real internal growth (volume and mix); "FX" is currency translation on reported Swiss franc figures. "Consensus" is the FactSet median of 16 November 2026 (EPS of CHF 4.51, 4.72 and 5.02 for FY26 to FY28). The "company-compiled consensus" is Nestlé's own collection of 9 October 2026, made before the Q3 sales and before Russia left the accounts (EPS of CHF 4.55 and 4.80 for FY26 and FY27). The "base case" is the second set's forecast (EPS of CHF 4.55, 4.53, 4.59, 4.76 and 4.92 for FY26 to FY30). The 18 months run to about May 2028 and hold six company events: FY26 results (18 February 2027), Q1-27 sales (late April 2027), H1-27 results (late July 2027), Q3-27 sales (October 2027), FY27 results (February 2028) and Q1-28 sales (April 2028).

Headline conclusions, which the Consumer should test first.

  1. The operating forecast is predictable. The reported forecast is much less so, and one variable causes most of the difference. For FY27 I put the plausible range of EPS at about CHF 4.32 to 4.74 around the base case of CHF 4.53 (about ±4.6%). At constant currency the range is about CHF 4.42 to 4.64 (about ±2.4%). The franc alone is about three-quarters of the variance (D-04).
  2. The improvement on the last six years rests on two drivers Nestlé does not control. On the company's own basis EPS was flat from FY19 to FY25, and the base case grows it by about 1% to 2% a year. The whole of that improvement comes from the franc appreciating more slowly and from the margin ceasing to fall, and about half of the margin change is coffee and cocoa (D-03).
  3. EPS momentum peaks at the next results. On the base case the half-year EPS changes against a year earlier are about +8% (H2-26, reported on 18 February 2027), about 0% (H1-27) and about -1% to -2% (H2-27). At constant currency and perimeter the engine grows about 5% throughout. The print the market sees gets worse while the business does not (D-04).
  4. What moves the share price is not what moves EPS. The share price has followed quarterly RIG. EPS is least sensitive to RIG (one point is about 1.4% of EPS) and most sensitive to the franc, the perimeter and the price-cost spread. The market has so far looked through the second group (Group judgement: Risks).
  5. Guidance will probably be met at every event and cannot carry our difference from consensus. Estimates must. Nestlé guides organic growth, margin percentage and free cash flow. The first excludes currency and perimeter, the second is helped by the exits, and only the third shows them. I expect consensus FY27 EPS to fall from CHF 4.72 to about CHF 4.60 by mid-2027 and FY28 from CHF 5.02 to about CHF 4.75 to 4.85 over 18 months (I-01, I-02).
  6. On our earnings the shares trade on 19.3 times, not 17.6 times. The 17.6 times that screens show uses consensus EPS that I expect to fall. The guideline return of +16.2% to December 2027 needs 20.17 times our earnings, which is 18.2 times today's consensus for the same period (The fourth set as a whole).
  7. I change no forecast input. I tested five candidate changes. None moves FY27 or FY28 EPS by more than 0.6%, and they largely offset (D-01).

The model's differences from the second set's cross-check, explained.

Free cash flow (flagged in all five years). The model's equity free cash flow is CHF 1.16bn to 1.55bn a year above the cross-check. No input is wrong. I rebuilt the model's FY27 figure from the inputs and it reconciles exactly: EBITDA 18,226, less tax 2,911, less net financial items 90, less capital spending 4,300, less lease payments 880, plus working capital released 280, equals 10,325. The gap exists because the form has no line for two cash items.

CHF m FY26 FY27 FY28 FY29 FY30
Model 10,340 10,325 10,269 10,360 10,850
Cross-check 8,950 8,780 9,020 9,200 9,690
Gap 1,390 1,545 1,249 1,160 1,160
Restructuring cash, which sits below EBITDA and is not in the model 1,000 800 400 300 300
Associate income the model counts as cash but Nestlé does not receive about 500 about 500 about 500 about 550 about 550
Not explained by those two items about -110 about +245 about +350 about +310 about +310
  • The associate item. The input "net financial items" carries about CHF 1.33bn to 1.49bn of associate income less minorities. Nestlé receives only about CHF 0.8bn to 0.9bn of it as dividends. In 2025 L'Oréal contributed CHF 1,158m of earnings and CHF 710m of dividends (company annual report, cited by AlphaSense, 18 November 2026).
  • The residual. From FY27 the cross-check is a further CHF 0.25bn to 0.35bn a year below what the two named items explain. The second set's text does not account for it. The likeliest contents are cash items that sit between EBITDA and operating cash flow at Nestlé every year: monetary adjustments in hyperinflation countries (CHF -50m in H1-26 and CHF -74m in H1-25), pension contributions, and the cash settlement of recall and litigation provisions.
  • Which figure to use. The cross-check is the figure of judgement. It is the more cautious of the two by about 3% beyond the named items. On the cross-check, FY27 free cash flow after leases covers the dividend paid in 2027 (CHF 3.15 a share, about CHF 8.1bn) 1.08 times. On the model's figure the cover would read 1.27 times, which is wrong.

EPS for FY26 (model CHF 4.35, cross-check CHF 4.55; not in the model's own list of differences, but 4.4% apart). The difference lies wholly in H1-26, a reported period that the model takes from FactSet on the Viewforth definition. That definition applies the reported tax charge (a rate of 28.7% in H1-26, raised by non-deductible held-for-sale losses) to profit before those losses. The model's FY26 tax rate is therefore 23.5% (net income 11,190 on pre-tax profit 14,621), not the 20% input, which applies only to the forecast half. The forecast half agrees with the cross-check. Three figures in the summary are artefacts of this and should not be quoted: FY26 EPS growth of +1.5% (on the brokers' basis it is +2.9%, CHF 4.42 to 4.55); FY27 EPS growth of +4.1% (it is -0.4%, CHF 4.55 to 4.53); and "FY26 EPS 3.6% below consensus" (it is 0.9% above). The return calculation is unaffected, because it uses two-year-forward EPS.

Net debt of record (not flagged, but unusable for leverage). The model's year-end net debt runs from CHF -61.0bn (FY26) to CHF -51.2bn (FY30). The second set's path is about CHF -51.4bn to -46.1bn. Two things cause the difference. First, the model charges each year's declared dividend in that fiscal year: the change in net debt in FY27 (+6,394) equals free cash flow 10,325 plus disposals 4,300 less 8,231, which is CHF 3.20 times 2,572m shares, the FY27 dividend that Nestlé will pay in April 2028. FY26 therefore carries the April 2026 payment (in the reported first half) and the FY26 dividend of about CHF 8.1bn as well, and the model shows net debt rising by CHF 4.2bn in H2-26, a half in which Nestlé pays no dividend. Every year-end from FY26 is overstated by about one dividend. Second, the free cash flow overstatement above accumulates the other way (CHF 6.5bn by FY30). This is my inference from the summary's figures; the summary does not state the model's dividend timing. The memo should take leverage from the second set's path (about 2.8x, 2.5x and 2.5x net debt to EBITDA at end-FY26 to FY28), not from the model's net cash line.

Notes to the Financial Analyst.

  • The Financial Analyst should confirm whether the model charges the dividend declared for a fiscal year in that same year, and if so either move each dividend to the following year, when Nestlé pays it (April), or label the net cash line as "after the dividend declared for the year", because as it stands end-FY26 net debt of CHF 61.0bn is about CHF 8bn to 9bn above what the balance sheet will show at 31 December 2026.
  • The Financial Analyst should, if the model allows a line for it, deduct from equity free cash flow and from net cash the restructuring cash (CHF 1,000m, 800m, 400m, 300m and 300m for FY26 to FY30) and the part of associate income not received in cash (about CHF 500m a year, CHF 550m from FY29); if it does not, the summary should label equity free cash flow as "before restructuring cash and before the non-cash part of associate income".
  • The Financial Analyst should show FY26 EPS on the brokers' basis (CHF 4.55, with H1-26 at the company's underlying CHF 2.22) beside the model's CHF 4.35, so that the growth rates and the comparison with consensus for FY26 and FY27 are like for like.

Where I differ from the earlier sets.

  1. Portfolio mix: the first set was right and the second set's correction was an arithmetic slip. The second set's reader's note credits about +30bp of margin from Waters and about +20bp from vitamins and ice cream, deducts about 24bp for Russia, and concludes "about zero to +10bp". The sum is about +26bp. On the second set's own margins (Waters about 9%, vitamins about 6%, ice cream about 8%, Russia about 27%) the three exits add 52bp and Russia takes 25bp, a net +27bp, which matches Jefferies' +50bp less 20bp (25 October 2026). The second set's margin bridge uses +32bp for the three exits. The effect on the forecasts is small, because the bridge is loose in the other direction too (D-01).
  2. "Six years of falling EPS" is a FactSet artefact. The first set (C-49) used FactSet's adjusted diluted series (CHF 5.10 in FY19 to CHF 3.94 in FY25). On the company's underlying basis EPS was CHF 4.41 in FY19 and CHF 4.42 in FY25: flat over six years, with a rise to CHF 4.80 in FY22 and FY23 and a fall of 8% since. The FY19 to FY22 figures (CHF 4.41, 4.21, 4.42 and 4.80) are from my own knowledge of the company's published results, not from the files; the Consumer should verify them before quoting. The point matters for D-03.
  3. Russia's one-off charges do not overlap. The second set left open whether the CHF 1.6bn write-off of net assets and the CHF 1.4bn of recycled currency losses overlap. The company presents them as distinct and both non-cash, with a separate CHF 0.9bn rise in net debt from derecognised cash (22 October 2026). That puts FY26 reported net income at about CHF 5.5bn to 6bn (reported EPS of about CHF 2.15 to 2.35), the lower middle of the second set's CHF 5bn to 7bn. The FY26 dividend of CHF 3.15 will be about 135% to 145% of reported EPS. That is a headline, not a cash problem.
  4. Russia was larger than the second set assumed. The company gives nine-month 2026 sales of CHF 1.5bn, UTOP of CHF 0.4bn (a margin of 26.7%) and free cash flow of CHF 0.3bn. Annualised that is about CHF 2.0bn of sales and CHF 0.53bn of UTOP, against the second set's CHF 1.8bn and about CHF 0.49bn.
  5. The consensus finance-cost fall is less cleanly measured than the second set states. The FY26 gap between consensus EBIT and pre-tax profit (CHF 1.56bn) cannot be pure net finance cost, because the reported H1-26 expense was CHF 688m (CHF 1.38bn annualised). FactSet's medians for EBIT and pre-tax profit may also rest on different sets of brokers: it gives a broker count for EBIT (16) and none for pre-tax profit. The level that matters, about CHF 1.0bn implied for FY28 against the second set's CHF 1.40bn, is worth about CHF 0.12 a share if taken at face value. I treat it as CHF 0.05 to 0.12 (I-02).
  6. Valuation basis. The first set judged the shares "at their five-year median" on 17.6 times. On the base case's EPS the multiple is 19.3 times, above the first set's own fair range of 16 to 19 times (C-50).

The third set of questions: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading (Layer 4) The fourth set of questions: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative
Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of NESN-CH with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-17 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Peers (from the dashboard data file): Associated British Foods (ABF-GB), Aryzta (ARYN-CH), Barry Callebaut (BARN-CH), Bell Food Group (BELL-CH), Danone (BN-FR), Cloetta (CLA.B-SE), Cranswick (CWK-GB), Ebro Foods (EBRO-ES), Emmi (EMMN-CH), Glanbia (GL9-IE), Greencore (GNC-GB), Hilton Food (HFG-GB), Chocoladefabriken Lindt & Spruengli (LISN-CH), Lotus Bakeries (LOTB-BE), LDC (LOUP-FR), NewPrinces (NWL-IT), Orkla (ORK-NO), Premier Foods (PFD-GB), Savencia (SAVE-FR), Spadel (SPA-BE), Suedzucker (SZU-DE), Viscofan (VIS-ES); no price history for CLA.B-SE

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH), the world's largest branded food and beverage group, reporting in Swiss francs; four core categories (Coffee; Petcare; Nutrition; Food & Snacks) reported through three zones (Americas; Europe; Asia, Oceania and Africa) plus Nespresso and Waters & Premium Beverages, which is moving into the Peranel joint venture.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH), what is the current state of momentum and of investor opinion since the third-quarter 2026 sales of 22 October 2026? Cover: every change in broker rating and target price, by house and date, with the reason given; the direction and size of estimate revisions for FY2026 to FY2028 by house; investor commentary outside the sell side (fund-manager letters and interviews, buy-side views reported in the press, investor conferences, and social-media commentary such as on X/Twitter) and what it says about the volume recovery, the deconsolidation of Russia, the exits of Waters, mainstream vitamins and ice cream, the Swiss franc, the dividend, share buybacks and the 20.1% L'Oréal stake; ownership and positioning (changes among the largest holders, short interest, hedge-fund positioning, options pricing); and share-price technical momentum. Give the same for these named peers: Unilever, Danone, Mondelez International, Lindt & Sprüngli, Orkla, Kraft Heinz, General Mills and Colgate-Palmolive, including each one's latest guidance changes and estimate revisions; and for the European consumer staples sector as a whole, including fund flows between defensive and cyclical sectors and the sector's valuation relative to the market as bond yields have moved. Which houses and which investors are the most positive and the most negative on Nestlé, since when, and what does each say would change its view? State how many covering houses and how many non-sell-side sources you found and used.

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH) and for large branded consumer staples in general, how has the forward price-to-earnings multiple behaved through the stages of a turnaround, and in particular in periods when sell-side EPS estimates were being cut for mechanical reasons (disposals, joint ventures, deconsolidations, currency translation) while organic volume growth was improving? Cover these cases: Nestlé in 2017 to 2019 (the Third Point campaign, the 17.5% to 18.5% margin target, the US confectionery sale and the share buyback) and in 2025 to 2026; Unilever from 2023 to 2026 (new chief executive, ice cream demerger, sale of Foods); Danone under Renew Danone from 2022 to 2025; Procter & Gamble's brand exits of 2014 to 2019; Mondelez International; Kraft Heinz after 2019; Reckitt; and Colgate-Palmolive. For each case give: the forward multiple at its low, at the point volume growth turned positive, at the point EPS estimates stopped falling, and at its peak; how long each stage lasted; whether the re-rating came before or after estimate upgrades; how the shares reacted when estimates were re-based for disposals; and what brokers called a fair multiple at each stage. Nestlé's 24-month-forward price-to-earnings multiple has ranged from 14.9 times to 24.9 times over five years, with a median of 17.7 times (FactSet, 13 November 2026). What multiple do brokers currently regard as fair for Nestlé, on what earnings basis (including or excluding the L'Oréal stake), and what conditions do they attach to a multiple above the historical median? How have dividend cover, the absence of share buybacks and leverage (Nestlé's net debt to EBITDA was 2.85 times at end-2025; company) affected the multiples of comparable staples, and how did those multiples move when buybacks were restarted?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Nestlé S.A. (NESN-CH), what does each covering broker assume for FY2026, FY2027 and FY2028 on the lines between organic growth and underlying EPS, and what does each expect the company to say at its full-year results on 18 February 2027? By broker and date, give: the perimeter (whether and from what date Russia, Waters & Premium Beverages, mainstream vitamins and the remaining ice cream businesses are removed, and the sales and operating profit removed for each); currency translation on sales; the underlying trading operating profit margin and any bridge given (savings, commodity costs, advertising, portfolio mix, the non-recurrence of the pension credit in the first half of 2026); net financial expense; income from associates and joint ventures (L'Oréal, Froneri, Cereal Partners Worldwide, Peranel); non-controlling interests; the underlying tax rate; the share count; free cash flow, restructuring cash costs and net debt. Give the same lines from the company-compiled consensus of 9 October 2026 and from any later company-compiled consensus. Nestlé reported net financial expense of CHF 688m in the first half of 2026 (company); FactSet consensus at 16 November 2026 shows EBIT of CHF 14,744m, 15,050m and 15,706m and pre-tax profit of CHF 13,184m, 13,732m and 14,710m for FY2026 to FY2028: what explains the narrowing difference between the two lines? What do brokers expect FY2027 guidance to be for organic growth, margin and free cash flow; how do they expect the company to present the scope effects and to restate its base; and do any expect a new savings programme, a capital markets day, a change to the medium-term targets (organic growth of 4%+, real internal growth of at least 2%, an underlying trading operating profit margin of 17%+; company) or a statement on capital returns? How did the estimates and the shares of Unilever, Danone and other staples move when those companies re-based guidance and consensus for disposals or deconsolidations?
